Parameterized splitting theorems and bifurcations for potential operators, Part I: Abstract theory Thanks: Partially supported by the NNSF 11271044 of China. 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification. Primary: 58E07, 58E09; Secondary: 58E05, 47J15. Key words and phrases. Bifurcation, potential operator, splitting theorem.
Guangcun Lu

Abstract
This is the first part of a series devoting to the generalizations and applications of common theorems in variational bifurcation theory. Using parameterized versions of splitting theorems in Morse theory we generalize some famous bifurcation theorems for potential operators by weakening standard assumptions on the differentiability of the involved functionals, which opens up a way of bifurcation studies for quasi-linear elliptic boundary value problems.



1
Introduction

Let
H
be a real Hilbert space,
U
an open neighborhood of
0
in
H
,
and
I
an open interval in
\mathbb{R}
. Suppose that

\mathcal{F}:I\times U\to\mathbb{R}
is Gâteaux differentiable in the second variable
and that

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{u}(\lambda,u)=0

(1.1)

possesses the trivial solution
u=0
for each
\lambda\in I
.
A point
(\mu,0)\in I\times H
is called a
bifurcation point
of (
1.1
)
if every neighborhood of it in
I\times H
contains a solution
(\lambda,u)
of (
1.1
)
with
u\neq 0
.
(In the literature people also said
there is
continuous bifurcation
of (
1.1
) at
\mu

provided that
\mathcal{S}=\{(\lambda,u)\in I\times U\,|\,\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{u}(\lambda,u)=0\;\hbox{and}\;u\neq 0\}

has a connected subset
\mathcal{C}
such that
\bar{\mathcal{C}}\cap({\mathbb{R}}\times\{0\})=\{(\mu,0)\}
.)
Bifurcation theory is concerned with the structure of the solutions of (
1.1
) near
a bifurcation point.
H. Poincare
[
56
]
initiated the mathematical study of this subject in 1885.
A. Liapunov
[
38
]
and E. Schmidt
[
61
]
reduced bifurcation theory to a finite-dimensional problem.
M. A. Krasnosel’skii
[
37
]
and J. Cronin
[
20
]

studied bifurcation problems with the method of topological degrees.
The former also first used variational methods to study such problems,
which leaded to tremendous progress since the late 1960s,
see
[
2
,
3
,
4
,
6
,
5
,
9
,
11
,
75
,
14
,
25
,
26
,
27
,
31
,
35
,
39
,
48
,
49
,
57
,
59
,
67
,
73
]

etc and references therein. Nevertheless, the key first step in all of these studies is to reduce the problem
to a finite dimensional situation via either the Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction or the center
manifold theorems. It was because of this point that the functional
\mathcal{F}
was often assumed to be
C^{2}
, except

[
11
,
25
,
31
,
35
,
39
,
50
,
67
]
,
where functionals of class
C^{1,1}
or even
C^{1}
are considered.
Using topological degree C. A. Stuart also studied bifurcation equations of some
non-potential operators with Hadamard differentiability in
[
66
,
68
]
.

In this paper we also use variational methods, precisely Morse theory, to study bifurcation theory
of (
1.1
) for the functional
\mathcal{F}
with lower smoothness.
The reference
[
57
]
by G. Prodi seems to be the first paper studying bifurcation problems with Morse theory. See also
M. Berger
[
5
]
, Böhme
[
9
]
and Marino
[
48
]
.
L. Nirenberg
[
53
]
used the Morse lemma and the splitting theorem to study
such problems from a different view.
K. C. Chang
[
75
]
gave a Morse theory proof of Rabinowitz bifurcation theorem

[
59
]
. The basic idea is to study changes of
critical groups of
\mathcal{F}(\lambda,\cdot)
at
0
as
\lambda
varying.
As we know, main tools to compute critical groups are the splitting theorem and its corollary (the shifting theorem), which
are stated for
C^{2}
functionals on Hilbert spaces (
[
12
, Theorem I.5.1]
and
[
49
, Theorem 8.3]
.
In fact, the first step in the proof of the splitting theorem
(cf.
[
12
,
49
]
) is also the Lyapunov-Schmidt finite-dimensional reduction, which requires

C^{2}
-smoothness of functionals. Thus the Rabinowitz bifurcation theorem
are inapplicable for studying bifurcations
of quasilinear elliptic equations of potential type
since the corresponding variational functionals on natural chosen Sobolev spaces
cannot be of class
C^{2}
in general.
Recently, the author developed Morse theory methods for a class of
quasilinear elliptic equations by proving some splitting theorems for some classes of non-
C^{2}
functionals
[
40
]
–
[
45
]
.
In their proofs our finite-dimensional reductions either required weaker differentiability for potential operators or
were completed on a smaller space.
The ideas of this paper come from these studies.

(
Notations
. Let
X,Y
be Banach spaces, and
H
a Hilbert space. Throughout the paper,
we denote by
\mathscr{L}(X,Y)
the space of all bounded linear operators from
X
to
Y
,
by
\mathscr{L}(X)=\mathscr{L}(X,X)
, and by
\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
the space of all bounded linear self-adjoint
operators on
H
, by
B_{X}(x,r)
(resp.
\bar{B}_{X}(x,r)
) the open (resp. closed) ball in
X
with
center
x\in X
and radius
r>0
. For a map
f
from
X
to
Y
,

Df(x)
(resp.
df(x)
or
f^{\prime}(x)
) denotes the Gâteaux (resp. Fréchet) derivative of
f
at
x\in X
,
which is an element in
\mathscr{L}(X,Y)
. Of course, we also use
f^{\prime}(x)
to denote
Df(x)

without occurring of confusions. When
Y=\mathbb{R}
,
f^{\prime}(x)\in\mathscr{L}(X,\mathbb{R})=X^{\ast}
,
and if
X=H
we call the Riesz representation of
f^{\prime}(x)
in
H

gradient of
f
at
x
, denoted by
\nabla f(x)
. The the Fréchet (or Gâteaux) derivative of
\nabla f
at
x\in H

is denoted by
f^{\prime\prime}(x)
, which is an element in
\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
.
(
f^{\prime\prime}(x)
can be seen as a symmetric bilinear form on
H
without confusion occurring.)
Moreover, we always use
0
to
denote the origin in different vector spaces without special statements.
As usual
\mathbb{N}
denotes the set of all positive integers, and
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.
The unit sphere in a Banach space
X
is denoted by
SX
. If
G
is a compact Lie group, and

M
is a
G
-space or
G
-module, we use
M^{G}
to denote the fixed point set of the
G
-action,
i.e.,
M^{G}=\{x\in M\,|\,gx=x\;\forall g\in G\}
.)

Our main aim is to study generalizations of some previous famous bifurcation theorems
such as those by Krasnosel’skii
[
37
]
, by Rabinowitz
[
59
]
, and by Fadelll and Rabinowitz
[
26
,
27
]

with parameterized versions of two new splitting theorems established by the author
[
45
]
and
[
40
,
41
,
43
]

(see Appendix
A
) and the parameterized version of a splitting lemma by Bobylev and Burman
[
8
]
(see
Appendix
B
). The following are our basic assumptions related to them.

Hypothesis 1.1
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space with inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{H}

and the induced norm
\|\cdot\|
, and let
X
be a dense linear subspace in
H
.
Let
U
be an open neighborhood of
0
in
H
,
and let
\mathcal{L}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfy
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(0)=0
.
Assume that the gradient
\nabla\mathcal{L}
has a Gâteaux derivative
B(u)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
at every point

u\in U\cap X
, and that the map
B:U\cap X\to\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
has a decomposition

B=P+Q
, where for each
x\in U\cap X
,
P(x)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is positive definitive and

Q(x)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is compact.
B
,
P
and
Q
are also assumed to satisfy the following
properties:

(D1)

\{u\in H\,|\,B(0)u=\mu u,\;\mu\leq 0\}\subset X
.

(D2)

For any sequence
(x_{k})\subset U\cap X
with
\|x_{k}\|\to 0
,
\|P(x_{k})u-P(0)u\|\to 0
for any
u\in H
.

(D3)

The map
Q:U\cap X\to\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is continuous at
0
with respect to the topology
on
H
.

(D4)

For any sequence
(x_{k})\subset U\cap X
with
\|x_{k}\|\to 0
, there exist
constants
C_{0}>0
and
k_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

(P(x_{k})u,u)_{H}\geq C_{0}\|u\|^{2}
for all
u\in H
and for all
k\geq k_{0}
.

By
[
45
, Lemma 2.7]
the condition (D4) is equivalent to the following

(D4*)

There exist positive constants
\eta_{0}>0
and
C^{\prime}_{0}>0
such that
\bar{B}_{H}(0,\eta_{0})\subset U
and

(P(x)u,u)\geq C^{\prime}_{0}\|u\|^{2}\quad\forall u\in H,\;\forall x\in\bar{B}_{H}(0,\eta_{0})\cap X.

Hypothesis 1.2
.

Let
U\subset H
be as in Hypothesis
1.1
,
\mathcal{L}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfy

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(0)=0
and the gradient
\nabla\mathcal{L}
have the Gâteaux derivative

\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(u)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
at any
u\in U
, which is a compact operator
and approaches to
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)
in
\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
as
u\to 0
in
H
.

Hypothesis 1.3
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space with inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{H}

and the induced norm
\|\cdot\|
, and let
X
be a Banach space with
norm
\|\cdot\|_{X}
, such that
X\subset H
is dense in
H
and
\|x\|\leq\|x\|_{X}\;\forall x\in X
. For an open neighborhood
U
of
0
in
H
,
U\cap X

is also an open neighborhood of
0
in
X
, denoted by
U^{X}
.
Let
\mathcal{L}:U\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous functional satisfying the following
conditions:

(F1)

\mathcal{L}
is continuously directional
differentiable and
D\mathcal{L}(0)=0
.

(F2)

There exists a continuous and continuously directional differentiable
map
A:U^{X}\to X
, which is also strictly Fréchet differentiable
at
0
, such that

D\mathcal{L}(x)[u]=(A(x),u)_{H}
for all
x\in U\cap X
and
u\in X
.

(F3)

There exists a map
B:U\cap X\to\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
such that

(DA(x)[u],v)_{H}=(B(x)u,v)_{H}
for all
x\in U\cap X
and

u,v\in X
. (So
B(x)
induces an element in
\mathscr{L}(X)
, denoted by
B(x)|_{X}
,
and
B(x)|_{X}=DA(x)\in\mathscr{L}(X),\;\forall x\in U\cap X
.)

(C)

\{u\in H\,|\,B(0)(u)\in X\}\subset X
,
in particular
{\rm Ker}(B(0))\subset X
.

(D)

B
satisfies the conditions (D1)–(D4) as in Hypothesis
1.1
for the present
X
.

(
Note
: (F2) in
[
41
, §8]
does not have the requirement that
A:U^{X}\to X
is continuous since
we consider that it is contained in the assumption of the strict Fréchet differentiability of
A
at
0
.
Actually, the latter may only imply that
A
is continuous near
0\in X
, which is not sufficient
for us using the mean value theorem in the third line of
[
41
, page 2958]
.
Actually, in order to assure effectiveness of such an argument we only need the assumption that

A:U^{X}\to X
is continuous and Gâteaux differentiable, which is weaker since
the continuously directional differentiability of
A
implies that
A
is Gâteaux differentiable.)

Hypothesis 1.4
.

Let
H,X
and
U\subset H
be as in Hypothesis
1.3
,

\mathcal{L}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
has the critical point
0\in U
, and there exist
maps
A\in C^{1}(U^{X},X)
and
B:U\cap X\to\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
satisfying (F2)-(F3) in
Hypothesis
1.3
and

(D*)

For each
x\in U\cap X
,
B(x)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is a compact linear operator
and the map
B:U\cap X\to\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is continuous at
0
with respect to the topology on
H
.

Clearly, Hypothesis
1.2
and Hypothesis
1.4
are stronger than
Hypothesis
1.1
and Hypothesis
1.3
, respectively.
In applications, bifurcation theorems obtained under Hypothesises
1.1
,
1.2

are mainly applied to quasi-linear elliptic systems and Lagrange systems;
those obtained under Hypothesises
1.3
,
1.4
or the following are
more powerful for us studying bifurcations in Lagrange systems and geodesics on Finsler manifolds.

An important case of the bifurcation problem (
1.1
)
is that of eigenvalues of nonlinear problems. The following are two related hypothesises.

Hypothesis 1.5
.

Let tuples
(H,X,U,\mathcal{L},A,B)
and

(H,X,U,\widehat{\mathcal{L}},\widehat{A},\widehat{B})
satisfy (F1)-(F3)
in Hypothesis
1.3
,
\mathcal{L},\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
and
A,\widehat{A}\in C^{1}(U^{X},X)
.
Suppose that
\lambda^{\ast}
is an eigenvalue of

B(0)v-\lambda\widehat{B}(0)v=0,\quad v\in H,

(1.2)

and that for each
\lambda
near
\lambda^{\ast}
the operator
\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}:=B(0)-\lambda\widehat{B}(0)

satisfies (C) in Hypothesis
1.3
and (D1) in Hypothesis
1.1
, i.e.,

\{u\in H\,|\,\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}u\in X\}\cup\{u\in H\,|\,\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}u=\mu u,\;\mu\leq 0\}\subset X.

Assume also that one of the following two conditions holds:

(I)

\lambda^{\ast}\neq 0
,
and for each
x\in U\cap X
,
B(x)
has a decomposition

B(x)=P(x)+Q(x)
,
where
P(x)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is positive definitive,

Q(x)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is compact, and both satisfy the properties (D2)–(D4) in Hypothesis
1.1
;

\widehat{B}
satisfies (D*) in Hypothesis
1.4
, i.e., for each
x\in U\cap X
,

\widehat{B}(x)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is compact and
\widehat{B}(x)\to\widehat{B}(0)
in
\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)

as
x\to 0
along
X\cap U
in
H
.

(II)

\lambda^{\ast}=0
,
B
is as in (I), and
\widehat{B}(x)\to\widehat{B}(0)
in
\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)

as
x\to 0
along
X\cap U
in
H
.

Under Hypothesis
1.5
, for each
\lambda
near
\lambda^{\ast}
it is easily checked that the functional

{\mathcal{L}}_{\lambda}:={\mathcal{L}}-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}
satisfies Hypothesis
1.3
.
In fact, this also holds under the following weaker one.

Hypothesis 1.6
.

“
\mathcal{L},\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
and
A,\widehat{A}\in C^{1}(U^{X},X)
”
in Hypothesis
1.5
is replaced by
“
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
and
\widehat{A}\in C^{1}(U^{X},X)
”.

Let us outline our main ideas to study bifurcation problem of (
1.1
)
when
\mathcal{F}(\lambda,u)=\mathcal{L}(u)-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(u)
, where
\mathcal{L}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
is as in Hypothesis
1.1
with
X=H
and
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfies Hypothesis
1.2
.
Note that for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}:=\mathcal{L}-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}

also satisfies Hypothesis
1.1
with
X=H
. Let
\lambda^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}

be an eigenvalue of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)u=\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)u
.
New finite-dimensional reduction in the proofs of
[
45
, Theorems 2.12,2.16]

shows that solving

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(u)-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(u)=0

for
(\lambda,u)
near
(\lambda^{\ast},0)
in
\mathbb{R}\times H

is equivalent to solving

d\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}(z)=0

for
(\lambda,z)
near
(\lambda^{\ast},0)
in
\mathbb{R}\times H^{0}
,
where
H^{0}={\rm Ker}(B(0)-\lambda^{\ast}\widehat{B}(0))
and
(\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta)\ni\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}\in C^{1}(\bar{B}_{H^{0}}(0,\epsilon))
is continuous.
Hence under suitable additional conditions we may carry out other
arguments along
[
59
,
26
,
27
,
75
,
73
,
3
,
2
]
etc, and obtain many bifurcation theorems.
For example, suppose that the eigenvalue
\lambda^{\ast}
is also isolated. Then for each
\lambda\neq\lambda^{\ast}

close to
\lambda^{\ast}
the origin
0\in H
is a nondegenerate critical point
of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
in the sense of
[
45
]
, in particular
an isolated critical point of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
and thus
0\in H^{\circ}

is such a critical point of
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}
as well. Under some additional conditions we
can use the parameterized shifting theorem in
[
45
]

to compute critical groups of
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}
at
0\in H^{0}

and conclude that
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}
takes a local maximum (resp.
minimum) at
0\in H^{0}
as
\lambda
varies in one (resp. other) side of
\lambda^{\ast}
.
Thus a generalization of Rabinowitz bifurcation theorem
[
59
]
can follow from these and
Canino’s finite dimension version
[
11
, Theorem 5.1]

for an extension of the Rabinowitz’s theorem by Ioffe and Schwartzman
[
31
]
.
If
H
is equipped with an orthogonal action of a compact Lie group
G

for which
U
,
\mathcal{L}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}
are
G
-invariant,
we may apply methods in Fadelll and Rabinowitz
[
26
,
27
]
to

G
-invariant
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}
to generalize their results as
G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
or
S^{1}
.
Sometime, (for example, when
\mathcal{L}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}
satisfy Hypothesis
1.3
and
Hypothesis
1.4
, respectively) the reduced functional
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}
is of class
C^{2}
,
we may apply a result by Bartsch and Clapp
[
3
, §4]
to

G
-invariant
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}
to obtain corresponding generalizations for any compact Lie group
because we may explicitly compute the number
d
in
[
3
, §4]
in our situation.

Once parameterized versions of other splitting theorems on Banach spaces are established,
our above methods are applicable. For example, we may also write the parameterized versions of the splitting
lemmas at infinity in
[
42
]
, and then use them to derive some theorems of bifurcations at infinity.
These will be given otherwise.

The bifurcation theorems by Krasnosel’skii
[
37
]
, by Rabinowitz
[
59
]
, and by Fadelll and Rabinowitz
[
26
,
27
]
,
and their previous some generalizations, often require that functionals
\mathcal{F}(\lambda,x)

depend on
(\lambda,x)
in
C^{2}
way and that corresponding potential operators

\nabla_{x}\mathcal{F}(\lambda,x)
have forms either
A_{\lambda}x+o(\|x\|)
or

(A-\lambda)x+o(\|x\|)
for
x\to 0
. Compared these with the above six hypothesises
it is easily seen that our functionals require lower smoothness and corresponding potential operators
might have higher nonlinearity.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.
Section
2
contains some notions, simple recall of necessary results about stability of critical groups,
and a proposition about the uniform (PS) condition for a family of functionals.
In Section
3
, for
\mathcal{F}\in C^{0}(I\times U,\mathbb{R})
with nonlinear dependence on parameters,
we first discuss some necessary conditions for a point
(\mu,0)\in I\times H
to be a bifurcation point of (
1.1
),
and then as applications of splitting theorems in Appendix
A

we prove two sufficient conditions for bifurcations occurring,
which are part generalizations of results by Chow and Lauterbach
[
15
]
and by Kielhöfer
[
35
]
.
Some comparisons of our results with previous work are also given in Section
3.3
.
In Sections
4
,
5
we shall use the parameterized versions of splitting theorems

[
45
,
40
,
41
,
43
]
to generalize some older bifurcation theorems.
Section
4
is dedicated to some generalizations of Rabinowitz bifurcation theorem
[
59
]
.
Section
5
deals with the equivariant case;
some previous bifurcation theorems, such as those by Fadelll and Rabinowitz
[
26
,
27
]

by Bartsch and Clapp
[
3
]
are generalized so that they can be used to study
variational bifurcation for the integral functionals as in
[
45
, (1.3)]
.
In order to compare our methods with previous those we state corresponding results
for
C^{2}
functionals which can be obtained with our methods
at the end of each of Sections
3
,
4
,
5
.
In Section
6
, for potential operators of Banach-Hilbert regular functionals
(cf. Appendix
B
) we prove parallel results to some bifurcation theorems in last two sections.
In Appendixes
A
,
B
we give more general parameterized versions of splitting lemmas by author
[
40
,
41
,
45
]
, and
by Bobylev-Burman
[
8
]
, respectively.

2
Preliminary materials

In this section, for the reader’s reference we list necessary notions and
results on stability of critical groups,
and then give related propositions.

Recall that a
C^{1}
functional
f
on a Banach space
X
is said to satisfy
the
Palais-Smale condition
((PS)
condition
, for short) in a closed subset
S\subset X
if
every sequence
(u_{n})\subset S
with
f^{\prime}(u_{n})\to 0
and
(f(u_{n}))
bounded
has a subsequence converging to
u\in X
. For some
c\in\mathbb{R}
, if
“
(f(u_{n}))
bounded” is replaced by “
f(u_{n})\to c
”, we say that
f
satisfies
the
Palais-Smale condition at level
c
(
(PS)_{c}

condition
, for short) in
S
.
A family of
C^{1}
functionals on
X
parameterized by
a metric space
\Lambda
,
\{f_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
, is called to satisfy
the
uniform Palais-Smale condition
(
uniform
(PS)
condition
, for short) in a closed subset
S\subset X
if
every sequence
(\lambda_{n},u_{n})\subset\Lambda\times S
such that
f^{\prime}_{\lambda_{n}}(u_{n})\to 0
and
(f_{\lambda_{n}}(u_{n}))
bounded
has a subsequence converging to
(\lambda,u)\in\Lambda\times S

with
f^{\prime}_{\lambda}(u)=0
(cf.
[
19
, Definition 2.4]
).
(If
\Lambda
is compact we can assume
\lambda_{n}\to\lambda\in\Lambda
in this definition.)

Lemma 2.1

(
[
19
, Remark 2.1(b)]
)
.

For a
C^{1}
functional
f
on a Banach space (or
C^{1}
Finsler manifold)
X
with norm
\|\cdot\|
,
the
weak slope

|df|(u)
of
f
(as a continuous functional on the metric space
X
)
at any
u\in X
(cf.
[
22
, Definition (2.1)]
) is equal to
\|f^{\prime}(u)\|
.

This lemma and
[
19
, Theorem 5.1]
directly lead to:

Theorem 2.2
.

For a family of functionals on a Banach space
X
,
\{f_{\sigma}\in C^{1}(X,\mathbb{R})\,|\,\sigma\in[0,1]\}
,
suppose that there exists an open set
U
such that

(i)

z_{\sigma}\in U
is a unique critical point of
f_{\sigma}
in
\overline{U}
,
\forall\sigma\in[0,1]
;

(ii)

\sigma\to f_{\sigma}
is continuous in
C^{1}(\overline{U})
topology;

(iii)

f_{\sigma}
satisfies the (PS) condition in
\overline{U}
,
\forall\sigma\in[0,1]
.

Then
C_{\ast}(f_{\sigma},z_{\sigma};{\bf K})
is independent of
\sigma
for any Abel group
{\bf K}
.

Actually,
[
19
, Theorem 5.1]
was stated for
{\bf K}=\mathbb{R}
. However, the proof therein is still effective for any
any Abel group
{\bf K}
because the arguments involving in
{\bf K}
is to use
[
19
, Proposition 5.2]
and the latter
also clearly holds true if
\mathbb{R}
is replaced by
{\bf K}
by its proof.
Moreover, by
[
18
, Proposition 3.7]
the definition of critical groups used in
[
19
]
is equivalent to the usual one
as in
[
12
,
13
,
49
,
50
]
, i.e.,

C_{q}(f,z;{\bf K})=H_{q}(\{f\leq f(z)\}\cap U,\{f\leq f(z)\}\cap(U\setminus\{z\});{\bf K})

if
z\in X
is an isolated lower critical point of
f
, where
U
is a neighborhood of
z
.

When
X
is a Hilbert space, Theorem
2.2
was proved in
[
49
, Theorem 8.8]
(if
f_{\sigma}\in C^{2-0}(X,\mathbb{R})
),
in Theorem 5.6 of
[
12
, Chapter I]
(if
f_{\sigma}\in C^{2}(X,\mathbb{R})
), and in
[
13
, Corollary 5.1.25]
(if
f_{\sigma}\in C^{1}(X,\mathbb{R})
).

The conditions (ii)-(iii) in Theorem
2.2
can also be replaced by others.

Theorem 2.3

(
[
16
, Theorem 3.6]
)
.

For a family of functionals on a Banach space
X
,
\{f_{\sigma}\in C^{1}(X,\mathbb{R})\,|\,\sigma\in[0,1]\}
,
suppose that there exists an open set
U
such that

(i)

for each
\sigma\in[0,1]
,
z_{\sigma}\in U
is a unique critical point of
f_{\sigma}
in
\overline{U}
, and

[0,1]\ni\sigma\to z_{\sigma}\in U
is also continuous;

(ii)

\sigma\to f_{\sigma}
is continuous in
C^{0}(\overline{U})
topology;

(iii)

\{f_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[0,1]}
satisfies the uniform (PS) condition on
\overline{U}
, that is,
for every sequence
\sigma_{k}\to\sigma
in
[0,1]
and
(u_{k})
in
\overline{U}
with
f^{\prime}_{\sigma_{k}}(u_{k})\to 0
and

(f_{\sigma_{k}}(u_{k}))
bounded, there exists a subsequence
(u_{k_{j}})
convergent to some
u
with
f^{\prime}_{\sigma}(u)=0
.

Then
C_{\ast}(f_{\sigma},z_{\sigma};{\bf K})
is independent of
\sigma
for any Abel group
{\bf K}
.

Let
X
be a Banach space with dual space
X^{\ast}
.
A map
T
from a subset
D
of
X
to
X^{\ast}
is said to be of
class
(S)_{+}

if for any sequence
(u_{j})\subset D
with converging weakly to
u
in
X

for which
\varlimsup_{j\to\infty}\langle T(u_{j}),u_{j}-u\rangle\leq 0
, it
follows that
(u_{j})
converges strongly to
u
in
X
.
The definition was introduced by Browder
[
10
]
and Skrypnik
[
62
]
(the condition of belonging to class
(S)_{+}
is called condition
\alpha
in the latter paper).

Proposition 2.4
.

Under Hypothesis
1.1
or Hypothesis
1.3
the restriction of the gradient
\nabla\mathcal{L}

to a small neighborhood of
0\in H
is of the class
(S)_{+}
.
Thus
\mathcal{L}
satisfies the (PS) condition in a closed neighborhood of
0\in H
.

The case that Hypothesis
1.3
is satisfied was proved in the proof of Theorem 2.12 of
[
41
, pages 2966-2967]
.
If Hypothesis
1.1
holds the proposition may be proved
by almost same arguments. It may also be contained in the proof of the following more general claim.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
H
,
X
and
U
be as in Hypothesis
1.1
,
I
an interval in
\mathbb{R}
.
Suppose that
\{\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})\,|\,\lambda\in I\}
satisfies:

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(0)=0
, the gradient
\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
has a Gâteaux derivative
B_{\lambda}(u)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
at every point

u\in U\cap X
, and that the map
B_{\lambda}:U\cap X\to\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
has a decomposition

B_{\lambda}=P_{\lambda}+Q_{\lambda}
, where for each
x\in U\cap X
,

Q_{\lambda}(x)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is compact. For
some small
\delta>0
with
\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)\subset U
operators
P_{\lambda}
,
Q_{\lambda}
and
\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
are also assumed to satisfy the following
properties:

(i)

There exist positive constants
c_{0}>0
such that

(P_{\lambda}(x)u,u)\geq c_{0}\|u\|^{2}\quad\forall u\in H,\;\forall x\in\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)\cap X,\quad\forall\lambda\in I.

(ii)

As
x\in U\cap X
approaches
0
in
H
,

Q_{\lambda}(x)\to Q_{\lambda}(0)
in
\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
uniformly with respect to
\lambda\in I
.

(iii)

If
(\lambda_{n})\subset I
converges to
\lambda\in I
then

\|Q_{\lambda_{n}}(0)-Q_{\lambda}(0)\|\to 0\quad\hbox{and}\quad\|\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n}}(x)-\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(x)\|\to 0\quad\forall x\in\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)\cap X.

Then for any sequences
\lambda_{n}\to\lambda_{0}
in
I
and
(u_{n})\subset\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)
such that

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{\lambda_{n}}(u_{n})\to 0
and
(\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n}}(u_{n}))
is bounded, there exists a subsequence
u_{n_{k}}\to u_{0}\in\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)

with
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{\lambda_{0}}(u_{0})=0
.

Moreover, if
X
itself is a normed line space with norm
\|\cdot\|
such that the inclusion
X\hookrightarrow H
is
continuous (and so
U\cap X
is an open neighborhood of
0
in
X
, denoted by
U^{X}
as before),
then the sentence “
the gradient
\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
has a Gâteaux derivative
B_{\lambda}(u)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
at every point

u\in U\cap X
” in the above statement can be replaced by
“
there exists a map
B_{\lambda}:U^{X}\to\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
and a continuous and continuously directional differentiable
map
A_{\lambda}:U^{X}\to X
such that

\displaystyle D\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(x)[u]=(A_{\lambda}(x),u)_{H}\quad\hbox{and}\quad(DA_{\lambda}(x)[u],v)_{H}=(B_{\lambda}(x)u,v)_{H}

(2.1)

for all
x\in U^{X}
and
u,v\in X
”.

Proof.

By (ii) we may shrink
\delta>0
so that

\|Q_{\lambda}(x)-Q_{\lambda}(0)\|<\frac{c_{0}}{2},\quad\forall x\in\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)\cap X,\quad\forall\lambda\in I.

It follows from this and (i) that for all
x\in\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)\cap X
and
u\in H
,

\displaystyle\bigl(B_{\lambda}(x)u,u\bigr)_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl(P_{\lambda}(x)u,u\bigr)_{H}+\bigl([Q_{\lambda}(x)-Q_{\lambda}(0)]u,u\bigr)_{H}+\bigl(Q_{\lambda}(0)u,u\bigr)_{H}

(2.2)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{2}\|u\|^{2}+\bigl(Q_{\lambda}(0)u,u\bigr)_{H}.

Clearly,
(u_{n})
has a subsequence
(u_{n_{k}})
weakly converging to
u_{0}\in\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)
.
Since
\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)\cap X
is dense in
\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)
, and
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}\in C^{1}(U)
, we have sequences

(v_{n_{k}})\subset\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)\cap X
and
(u_{0m})\subset\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)\cap X
such that
for all
k,m=1,2,\cdots
,

\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}\|u_{0m}-u_{0}\|<\frac{1}{m},\quad\|v_{n_{k}}-u_{n_{k}}\|<\frac{1}{k}\quad\hbox{and}\\
\|\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(v_{n_{k}})-\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(u_{n_{k}})\|<\frac{1}{k}.\end{array}\right\}

(2.3)

Since
\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
is continuous and has a Gâteaux derivative
B_{\lambda}(u)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
at every point

u\in U\cap X
, for each fixed
k
using the mean value theorem we have
\tau\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(v_{n_{k}}),v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m})_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(v_{n_{k}})-\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(u_{0m}),v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m})_{H}-(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(u_{0m}),v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m})_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl(D(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}})(\tau v_{n_{k}}+(1-\tau)u_{0m})[v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m}],v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m}\bigr)_{H}

\displaystyle-(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(u_{0m}),v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m})_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl(B_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(\tau v_{n_{k}}+(1-\tau)u_{0m})(v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m}),v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m}\bigr)_{H}

\displaystyle-(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(u_{0m}),v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m})_{H}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{2}\|v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m}\|^{2}-(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(u_{0m}),v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m})_{H}

\displaystyle+(Q_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(0)(v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m}),v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m})_{H},

where the final inequality is because of (
2.2
).
Since
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}\in C^{1}(U)
letting
m\to\infty
we get

\displaystyle(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(v_{n_{k}}),v_{n_{k}}-u_{0})_{H}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{2}\|v_{n_{k}}-u_{0}\|^{2}-(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(u_{0}),v_{n_{k}}-u_{0})_{H}

\displaystyle+(Q_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(0)(v_{n_{k}}-u_{0}),v_{n_{k}}-u_{0})_{H},\quad\forall k=1,2,\cdots.

Note that (
2.3
) implies
\lim_{k\to\infty}\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(v_{n_{k}})=\lim_{k\to\infty}\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(u_{n_{k}})=0
and
v_{n_{k}}\rightharpoonup u_{0}
.
It easily follows these and (iii) that

\frac{c_{0}}{2}\lim_{k\to\infty}\|v_{n_{k}}-u_{0}\|^{2}\leq\lim_{k\to\infty}(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(v_{n_{k}}),v_{n_{k}}-u_{0})_{H}=0

and thus
u_{n_{k}}\to u_{0}
in
H
. The desired claim is proved.

In order to prove the second part, we only need to change the seven lines below (
2.3
) into:

“Since
A_{\lambda}:U^{X}\to X
is continuous, and continuously directional differentiable,
and satisfies (
2.1
), for each fixed
k
using the mean value theorem we have
\tau\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(v_{n_{k}}),v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m})_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(v_{n_{k}})-\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(u_{0m}),v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m})_{H}-(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(u_{0m}),v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m})_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(A_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(v_{n_{k}})-A_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(u_{0m}),v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m})_{H}-(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(u_{0m}),v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m})_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl(DA_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(\tau v_{n_{k}}+(1-\tau)u_{0m})[v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m}],v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m}\bigr)_{H}

\displaystyle-(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n_{k}}}(u_{0m}),v_{n_{k}}-u_{0m})_{H}

”the same conclusion is obtained.
∎

Corollary 2.6
.

Suppose that one of the following two conditions holds:

(I)

\mathcal{L}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
and
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfy
Hypothesis
1.1
and Hypothesis
1.2
, respectively.

(II)

\mathcal{L}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
and
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfy
Hypothesis
1.3
and Hypothesis
1.4
, respectively.

Then for a bounded interval
I
in
\mathbb{R}
and for
some small
\delta>0
with
\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)\subset U

the conclusion of Proposition
2.5
holds true on
\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)

for the family
\{\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}:=\mathcal{L}-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\,|\,\lambda\in I\}
,
that is, for any sequences
\lambda_{n}\to\lambda_{0}
in
I
and
(u_{n})\subset\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)
such that

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{\lambda_{n}}(u_{n})\to 0
and
(\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n}}(u_{n}))
is bounded, there exists
a subsequence
u_{n_{k}}\to u_{0}\in\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)

with
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{\lambda_{0}}(u_{0})=0
.

Proof.

Case (I)
.
The gradient
\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}=\nabla\mathcal{L}-\lambda\nabla\mathcal{G}
has a Gâteaux derivative
B_{\lambda}(u)=B(u)-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(u)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
at every point
u\in U\cap X
, and that
B_{\lambda}(x)=P_{\lambda}(x)+Q_{\lambda}(x)=P(x)+Q_{\lambda}(x)
for each
x\in U\cap X
, where

Q_{\lambda}(x)=Q(x)-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(x)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is compact.
By (D4) or (D4*) in Hypothesis
1.1
,
P_{\lambda}\equiv P
satisfies Proposition
2.5
(i) for some small
\delta>0
with
\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)\subset U
.
Since the internal
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is bounded, it is easily seen that
the conditions (ii) and (iii) in Proposition
2.5
are satisfied.

Case (II)
. For each
x\in U\cap X
, let
A_{\lambda}(x)=A(x)-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(x)
,

B_{\lambda}(x)=B(x)-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(x)
,
P_{\lambda}\equiv P
and
Q_{\lambda}(x)=Q(x)-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(x)
.
Similar arguments show that the conditions of Proposition
2.5
may be satisfied.
∎

3
New necessary conditions and sufficient criteria for bifurcations of gradient mappings

For a completely continuous operator
A:U\to H
,
if
A
is the gradient of a weakly continuous and uniformly
differentiable functional
f:U\to\mathbb{R}
and has the Fréchet derivative
A^{\prime}(0)
,
(which must be compact and self-adjoint,) Krasnosel’ski
[
37
]

proved that each nonzero eigenvalue
\mu
of
A^{\prime}(0)
gives a bifurcation point

(\mu,0)
of the equation
A(u)=\lambda u
.
More precisely, for any sufficiently small
r>0
there exists
\lambda_{r}\in\mathbb{R}
,
u_{r}\in SH

such that
A(u_{r})=\lambda_{r}u_{r}
and
\lambda_{r}\to\mu
as
r\to 0
.
Since then several extensions and improvements of this work have been made.
For example, if
f\in C^{2}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfies
f^{\prime}(0)=0

and
\mu
is an isolated eigenvalue of
f^{\prime\prime}(0)
of finite multiplicity,
Rabinowitz
[
58
]
proved that
(\mu,0)

is a bifurcation point of the equation
\nabla f(u)=\lambda u
.
See
[
58
,
69
]
and references therein for further details.
In this section we give necessary conditions and
sufficient criteria for a point
(\mu,0)\in I\times H
to be a bifurcation point of (
1.1
)
under some new assumptions of
\mathcal{F}
. More sufficient criteria
will be given in next three sections.

3.1
Necessary conditions

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
H
,
X
and
U
be as in Hypothesis
1.1
,
and
\Lambda
a topological space.
For each
\lambda\in\Lambda
, let
\{\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})\,|\,\lambda\in\Lambda\}

satisfy
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(0)=0
, and let the gradient
\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
have
a Gâteaux derivative
B_{\lambda}(u)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
at every point

u\in U\cap X
. Suppose that the map
B_{\lambda}:U\cap X\to\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
has a decomposition
B_{\lambda}=P_{\lambda}+Q_{\lambda}
, where for each
x\in U\cap X
,

P_{\lambda}(x)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is positive definitive and

Q_{\lambda}(x)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is compact, and
that
P_{\lambda}
and
Q_{\lambda}
satisfy the following conditions:

(i)

If
(x_{k})\subset U\cap X
approaches to
0
in
H
and
(\lambda_{k})\subset\Lambda
converges to
\lambda^{\ast}
then

\|P_{\lambda_{k}}(x_{k})h-P_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)h\|\to 0
for each
h\in H
.

(ii)

For some small
\delta>0
, there exists a positive constant
c_{0}>0
such that

(P_{\lambda}(x)u,u)\geq c_{0}\|u\|^{2}\quad\forall u\in H,\;\forall x\in\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)\cap X,\quad\forall\lambda\in\Lambda.

(iii)

Q_{\lambda}:U\cap X\to\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is uniformly continuous at
0
with respect to
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

(iv)

If
(\lambda_{n})\subset\Lambda
converges to
\lambda^{\ast}
then

\|Q_{\lambda_{n}}(0)-Q_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)\|\to 0
.

Then
0\in H
is a degenerate critical point of
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
if

({\lambda}^{\ast},0)\in\Lambda\times U
is a bifurcation point of

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{{\lambda}}(u)=0,\quad(\lambda,u)\in\Lambda\times U.

(3.1)

Proof.

Since
({\lambda}^{\ast},0)\in\Lambda\times U
is a bifurcation point of the equation (
3.1
),
there exists a sequence
({\lambda}_{k},\bar{u}_{k})\in\Lambda\times(U\setminus\{0\})

such that
{\lambda}_{k}\to{\lambda}^{\ast}
,
\bar{u}_{k}\to 0
and

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{{\lambda}_{k}}(\bar{u}_{k})=0,\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{N}.

(3.2)

Passing to a subsequence, if necessary, we can assume
\bar{v}_{k}=\bar{u}_{k}/\|\bar{u}_{k}\|\rightharpoonup v^{\ast}
.
By (ii) there exist positive constants
\eta_{0}>0
and
c_{0}>0
such that
B_{H}(0,\eta_{0})\subset U
and

(P_{\lambda}(u)h,h)_{H}\geq c_{0}\|h\|^{2},\quad\forall h\in H,\;\forall u\in B_{H}(0,\eta_{0})\cap X,\;\forall\lambda\in I.

(3.3)

Clearly, we can assume that
(\bar{u}_{k})\subset B_{H}(0,\eta_{0})\setminus\{0\}
.
Since
B_{H}(0,\eta_{0})\cap X
is dense in
B_{H}(0,\eta_{0})
we may choose

({u}_{k})\subset(B_{H}(0,\eta_{0})\cap X)\setminus\{0\}
such that

\displaystyle\|u_{k}-\bar{u}_{k}\|\to 0\quad\hbox{and so}\quad u_{k}\to 0,\;v_{k}:={u}_{k}/\|{u}_{k}\|\rightharpoonup v^{\ast},

(3.4)

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{\|u_{k}\|}\nabla\mathcal{F}_{{\lambda}_{k}}(u_{k})-\frac{1}{\|\bar{u}_{k}\|}\nabla\mathcal{F}_{{\lambda}_{k}}(\bar{u}_{k})\right\|<\frac{1}{2^{k}},\;\forall k,

(3.5)

\displaystyle\biggl|\frac{1}{\|u_{k}\|^{2}}(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{{\lambda}_{k}}(u_{k}),u_{k})_{H}-\frac{1}{\|\bar{u}_{k}\|^{2}}(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{{\lambda}_{k}}(\bar{u}_{k}),\bar{u}_{k})_{H}\biggr|<\frac{1}{2^{k}},\;\forall k.

(3.6)

For each fixed
k
, since
\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{k}}(0)=0

using the Mean Value Theorem we get
t_{k}\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\|u_{k}\|^{2}}(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{{\lambda}_{k}}(u_{k}),u_{k})_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(D(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{{\lambda}_{k}})(t_{k}u_{k})v_{k},v_{k})_{H}

(3.7)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(P_{{\lambda}_{k}}(t_{k}u_{k})v_{k},v_{k})_{H}+(Q_{{\lambda}_{k}}(t_{k}u_{k})v_{k},v_{k})_{H}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle c_{0}+(Q_{{\lambda}_{k}}(t_{k}u_{k})v_{k},v_{k})_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{0}+([Q_{{\lambda}_{k}}(t_{k}u_{k})-Q_{{\lambda}_{k}}(0)]v_{k},v_{k})_{H}

\displaystyle+(Q_{{\lambda}_{k}}(0)v_{k},v_{k})_{H}.

Since
t_{k}u_{k}\to 0
by (
3.4
), using (iii) and (iv), respectively, we deduce that for sufficiently
k
,

\begin{split}|([Q_{{\lambda}_{k}}(t_{k}u_{k})-Q_{{\lambda}_{k}}(0)]v_{k},v_{k})_{H}|&<\frac{c_{0}}{4}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\\
|(Q_{{\lambda}_{k}}(0)v_{k},v_{k})_{H}-(Q_{{\lambda}^{\ast}}(0)v^{\ast},v^{\ast})_{H}|&<\frac{c_{0}}{2}\end{split}

and thus (
3.7
) leads to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\|u_{k}\|^{2}}(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{{\lambda}_{k}}(u_{k}),u_{k})_{H}>\frac{c_{0}}{2}+(Q_{{\lambda}^{\ast}}(0)v^{\ast},v^{\ast})_{H}.

By (
3.2
) and (
3.6
) the left side approaches to zero.
Hence
v^{\ast}\neq 0
.

Obverse that (
3.2
) and (
3.5
) imply

\Big|\frac{1}{\|u_{k}\|}(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{{\lambda}_{k}}(u_{k}),h)_{H}\Bigr|\leq\frac{1}{2^{k}}\|h\|,\quad\forall h\in H,\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{N}.

(3.8)

Fixing
h\neq 0
, as in (
3.7
), for some
\tau_{k}\in(0,1)
, depending on
u_{k}
and
h
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\|u_{k}\|}(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{{\lambda}_{k}}(u_{k}),h)_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(D(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{{\lambda}_{k}})(\tau_{k}u_{k})v_{k},h)_{H}

(3.9)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(P_{{\lambda}_{k}}(\tau_{k}u_{k})v_{k},h)_{H}+(Q_{{\lambda}_{k}}(\tau_{k}u_{k})v_{k},h)_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(v_{k},P_{{\lambda}_{k}}(\tau_{k}u_{k})h)_{H}+(v_{k},Q_{{\lambda}_{k}}(\tau_{k}u_{k})h)_{H}.

As above, by (iii)-(iv) we deduce that
(v_{k},Q_{{\lambda}_{k}}(\tau_{k}u_{k})h)_{H}\to(v^{\ast},Q_{{\lambda}^{\ast}}(0)h)_{H}
.
Moreover, (i) implies that
P_{{\lambda}_{k}}(\tau_{k}u_{k})h\to P_{{\lambda}^{\ast}}(0)h
. It follows from these
and (
3.4
), (
3.8
) and (
3.9
) that

(v^{\ast},P_{{\lambda}^{\ast}}(0)h)_{H}+(v^{\ast},Q_{{\lambda}^{\ast}}(0)h)_{H}=0,\quad\forall h\in H,

and thus
D(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{{\lambda}^{\ast}})(0)v^{\ast}=0
.
∎

Similarly, we have

Theorem 3.2
.

In Theorem
3.1
, if we replace “Hypothesis
1.1
” by
“Hypothesis
1.3
”, and “the gradient
\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
has
a Gâteaux derivative
B_{\lambda}(u)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
at every point

u\in U\cap X
” by “there exists a map
B_{\lambda}:U\cap X\to\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
and a continuous and continuously directional differentiable
map
A_{\lambda}:U^{X}\to X
such that
D\mathcal{L}(x)[u]=(A_{\lambda}(x),u)_{H}
and

(DA_{\lambda}(x)[u],v)_{H}=(B_{\lambda}(x)u,v)_{H}
for all
x\in U\cap X
and

u,v\in X
”, then
0\in H
is a degenerate critical point of
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
provided that

({\lambda}^{\ast},0)\in\Lambda\times U^{X}
is a bifurcation point of

A_{{\lambda}}(u)=0,\quad(\lambda,u)\in\Lambda\times U^{X}.

Indeed, in the proof of Theorem
3.1
we only need to replace

D(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{{\lambda}_{k}})(t_{k}u_{k})
and
D(\nabla\mathcal{F}_{{\lambda}_{k}})(\tau_{k}u_{k})

by
A(t_{k}u_{k})
and
A(\tau_{k}u_{k})
, respectively, and complete the proof of Theorem
3.2
.

Corollary 3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{L}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfy
Hypothesis
1.1
with
X=H
, and let

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{j}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
,
j=1,\cdots,n
, satisfy
Hypothesis
1.2
.
Suppose that
(\vec{\lambda}^{\ast},0)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times U
is a (multiparameter) bifurcation point for the equation

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(u)=\sum^{n}_{j=1}\lambda_{j}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{j}(u),\quad u\in U.

(3.10)

Then
\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}=(\lambda^{\ast}_{1},\cdots,\lambda^{\ast}_{n})
is an eigenvalue of

\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)v-\sum^{n}_{j=1}\lambda_{j}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}_{j}(0)v=0,\quad v\in H,

(3.11)

that is,
0
is
a degenerate critical point of the functional
\mathcal{L}-\sum^{n}_{j=1}\lambda^{\ast}_{j}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{j}

in the sense stated above Theorem
A.1
.
Moreover, if
\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}=0
, we only need that
each
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{j}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
has properties:

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{j}^{\prime}(0)=0
and the gradient
\nabla\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{j}
has the Gâteaux derivative

\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(u)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
at any
u\in U
, which approaches to

\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)
in
\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
as
u\to 0
in
H
.

This result generalizes the necessity part of Theorem 12 in
[
62
, Chapter 4, §4.3]

(including the classical Krasnoselsi potential bifurcation theorem
[
37
]
).
The sufficiency part of Theorem 12 in
[
62
, Chapter 4, §4.3]
is contained in the case that the
condition (a) in Corollary
4.4
holds.

Denoted by
H(\vec{\lambda})
the solution space of (
3.11
).
It is of finite dimension as the kernel of a linear Fredholm operator.

When
n=1
, comparing with Theorem 12 in
[
63
, Chapter 4, §4.3]
, the latter also required:

(a)

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}
is
weakly continuous and uniformly differentiable in
U
;

(b)

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
has uniformly positive definite Frechèt derivatives and satisfies the condition

\alpha)
in
[
63
, Chapter 3, §2.2]
.

Proof of Corollary
3.3
.

First, we consider the case that
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{j}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
,
j=1,\cdots,n
, satisfy
Hypothesis
1.2
.
Let
B=P+Q
and
\widehat{B}_{j}=\widehat{P}_{j}+\widehat{Q}_{j}
be the
corresponding operators with
\mathcal{L}
and

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{j}
,
j=1,\cdots,n
, respectively.
Then
\widehat{P}_{j}=0
and
\widehat{Q}_{j}=\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}_{j}
for
j=1,\cdots,n
.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{\vec{\lambda}}(u)=\mathcal{L}(u)-\sum^{n}_{j=1}\lambda_{j}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{j}(u)
.
Denote by
B_{\vec{\lambda}}=P_{\vec{\lambda}}+Q_{\vec{\lambda}}

the corresponding operators.
Then
P_{\vec{\lambda}}=P
and
Q_{\vec{\lambda}}=Q-\sum^{n}_{j=1}\lambda_{j}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}_{j}(u)
.
Take
\Lambda
to be any compact neighborhood of
\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
It is easily checked that
\{\mathcal{F}_{\vec{\lambda}}\,|\,\vec{\lambda}\in\Lambda\}

satisfies the conditions of Theorem
3.1
.

Next, we prove the part of “Moreover”.
We can assume that there exist positive constants
\eta_{0}>0
and
c_{0}>0
such that
B_{H}(0,\eta_{0})\subset U
and

(P(u)h,h)_{H}\geq 2c_{0}\|h\|^{2},\quad\forall h\in H,\;\forall u\in B_{H}(0,\eta_{0})\cap X.

(3.12)

Since each
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}_{j}(u)
is continuous at
0\in H
, we can shrink
\eta_{0}>0
and choose a
small compact neighborhood
\Lambda
of
0\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}_{j}(u)-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}_{j}(0)\|<1,\quad\forall u\in B_{H}(0,\eta_{0}),\;j=1,\cdots,n,

(3.13)

\displaystyle\sum^{n}_{j=1}|\lambda_{j}(\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}_{j}(u)h,h)_{H}|<c_{0}\|h\|^{2},\;\forall u\in B_{H}(0,\eta_{0}),\;\forall\vec{\lambda}\in\Lambda,\;\forall h\in H.

(3.14)

Let us write
Q_{\vec{\lambda}}\equiv Q
and
P_{\vec{\lambda}}(u)=P(u)-\sum^{n}_{j=1}\lambda_{j}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}_{j}(u)
.
Then (
3.14
) and (
3.12
) lead to

(P_{\vec{\lambda}}(u)h,h)_{H}\geq c_{0}\|h\|^{2},\quad\forall h\in H,\;\forall u\in B_{H}(0,\eta_{0})\cap X.

(3.15)

Moreover, if
(x_{k})\subset B_{H}(0,\eta_{0})\cap X
approaches to
0
in
H
and
(\vec{\lambda}_{k})\subset\Lambda
converges to
0
then
for any fixed
h\in H
we deduce from (
3.14
) and the definition of
P_{\vec{\lambda}}
that

\displaystyle\|P_{\vec{\lambda}_{k}}(x_{k})h-P_{0}(0)h\|\leq\sum^{n}_{j=1}|\lambda_{k,j}|\|\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}_{j}(x_{k})h\|\leq\sum^{n}_{j=1}|\lambda_{k,j}|(\|\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}_{j}(0)\|+1)\|h\|\to 0.

These show that
Q_{\vec{\lambda}}
and
P_{\vec{\lambda}}
for
\vec{\lambda}\in\Lambda

satisfy the assumptions of Theorem
3.1
.
∎

Using Theorem
3.2
and similar reasoning we can obtain

Corollary 3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{L}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfy
Hypothesis
1.3
, and let

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{j}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
,
j=1,\cdots,n
, satisfy
Hypothesis
1.4
.
Suppose that
(\vec{\lambda}^{\ast},0)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times U^{X}
is a (multiparameter) bifurcation point for the equation

A(u)=\sum^{n}_{j=1}\lambda_{j}\widehat{A}_{j}(u),\quad u\in U^{X}.

(3.16)

Then
\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}=(\lambda^{\ast}_{1},\cdots,\lambda^{\ast}_{n})
is an eigenvalue of

B(0)v-\sum^{n}_{j=1}\lambda_{j}\widehat{B}_{j}(0)v=0,\quad v\in H,

(3.17)

that is,
0
is
a degenerate critical point of the functional
\mathcal{L}-\sum^{n}_{j=1}\lambda^{\ast}_{j}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{j}

in the sense stated above Theorem
A.1
.
Moreover, if
\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}=0
, we only need
that each
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{j}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfies
Hypothesis
1.4
without requirement that
B(x)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is compact.

Conversely, if
\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}
is an isolated eigenvalue of (
3.17
), under some additional conditions
we shall show in Theorem
4.9
that
(\vec{\lambda}^{\ast},0)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times U^{X}
is a bifurcation point
of (
3.16
).

3.2
Sufficient criteria

Changes of Morse type numbers imply existence of bifurcation instants
[
9
,
5
]
.
Different generalizations are given in
[
15
,
35
,
49
,
65
,
55
,
60
]
.
For example,
[
49
, Theorem 8.8]
showed that changes of critical groups lead to bifurcations.
However, it is difficult to compute critical groups for non-twice continuously differentiable functionals.
In this section, with helps of splitting theorems in
[
41
,
42
,
45
]

we give some general bifurcation results for potential operator families of a class of non-twice continuously differentiable functionals.

Let
H
be a real Hilbert space,
I
a bounded open interval containing
0
in
\mathbb{R}
,
and
\{B_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in I}\subset\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)

such that
\|B_{\lambda}-B_{0}\|\to 0
as
\lambda\to 0
.
Suppose that
0
is an isolated point of the spectrum
\sigma(B_{0})

with
n=\dim{\rm Ker}(B_{0})\in(0,\infty)
, and that

{\rm Ker}(B_{\lambda})=\{0\}\;\forall\pm\lambda\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
for some
positive number
\varepsilon_{0}\ll 1
.
By
[
36
, Remark I.21.1]
the generalized eigenspace
E_{0}
of
B_{0}
with eigenvalue
0

is equal to
N(B_{0})={\rm Ker}(B_{0})
(i.e., the algebraic and geometric multiplicities of
0
are same),
and
H=E_{0}\oplus R(B_{0})
, where
R(B_{0})={\rm Im}(B_{0})
.
It was shown in Sections II.5.1 and III.6.4 of
[
34
]
that
the generalized eigenspace
E_{0}

is perturbed to an invariant space
E_{\lambda}
of
B_{\lambda}
of dimension
n
,
and all perturbed eigenvalues near
0
(the so-called
0
-group in Kato’s terminology in
[
34
]
),
denoted by
{\rm eig}_{0}(B_{\lambda})
,
are eigenvalues of the finite-dimensional operator
B_{\lambda}
restricted to the
n
-dimensional
invariant space
E_{\lambda}
(see
[
36
, Remark II.4]
). In other words,

{\rm eig}_{0}(B_{\lambda})
is the set of eigenvalues of
B_{\lambda}
which approach
0
as
\lambda\to 0
.
Hence shrinking
\varepsilon_{0}
if necessary, for each
\lambda\in(-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0})\setminus\{0\}
,
B_{\lambda}
has exactly

n
eigenvalues (depending continuously on
\lambda
) near zero, and none of them is zero.
Let
r(B_{\lambda})
be the number of elements in
{\rm eig}_{0}(B_{\lambda})\cap\mathbb{R}^{-}
and

r^{+}_{B_{\lambda}}=\lim_{\lambda\to 0+}r(B_{\lambda}),\qquad r^{-}_{B_{\lambda}}=\lim_{\lambda\to 0-}r(B_{\lambda}).

(3.18)

Then if
|\lambda|>0
is small enough we have
\mu_{\lambda}-\mu_{0}=r^{+}_{B_{\lambda}}
for
\lambda>0
,
and
\mu_{\lambda}-\mu_{0}=r^{-}_{B_{\lambda}}
for
\lambda<0
, where
\mu_{\lambda}

is the dimension of negative definite space of
B_{\lambda}
.

In some sense the following may be viewed as a converse of Theorem
3.1
.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
H
and
I
be as above,
U
an open neighborhood of
0
in
H
,
and let
\mathcal{F}:I\times U\to\mathbb{R}
be such that each

\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}:=\mathcal{F}(\lambda,\cdot)
satisfies Hypothesis
1.1
on
U

with corresponding operators
B_{\lambda}
,
P_{\lambda}
and
Q_{\lambda}
.
Suppose that the following eight conditions are satisfied:

(i)

For some small
\delta>0
,
\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}

is continuous at
\lambda=0
in
C^{0}(\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta))
topology.

(ii)

For some small
\delta>0
, there exist positive constants
c_{0}>0
such that

(P_{\lambda}(x)u,u)\geq c_{0}\|u\|^{2}\quad\forall u\in H,\;\forall x\in\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)\cap X,\quad\forall\lambda\in I.

(iii)

Q_{\lambda}:U\cap X\to\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is uniformly continuous at
0
with respect to
\lambda\in I
.

(iv)

If
(\lambda_{n})\subset I
converges to
\lambda\in I
then

\|Q_{\lambda_{n}}(0)-Q_{\lambda}(0)\|\to 0\quad\hbox{and}\quad\|\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda_{n}}(x)-\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(x)\|\to 0\quad\forall x\in U\cap X.

(v)

{\rm Ker}(B_{\lambda}(0))=\{0\}

for small
|\lambda|\neq 0
.

(vi)

B_{\lambda}(0)\to B_{0}(0)
as
\lambda\to 0
;

(vii)

0\in\sigma(B_{0}(0))
.

(viii)

r^{+}_{B_{\lambda}(0)}\neq r^{-}_{B_{\lambda}(0)}
.

Then
(0,0)\in I\times U
is a bifurcation point of the equation (
1.1
).
Moreover, the same conclusion still holds if the above conditions (i)-(iv) are replaced by the following two

(a)

for some small
\delta>0
,
\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}

is continuous at
\lambda=0
in
C^{1}(\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta))
topology;

(b)

for some small
\delta>0
, each
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
satisfies the (PS) condition in
\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)
.

Proof.

By a contradiction, suppose that
(0,0)\in I\times U
is not a bifurcation point of the equation
(
1.1
). Then by shrinking
\delta>0
we can find
0<\varepsilon_{0}\ll 1
such that for each

\lambda\in[-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}]
the functional
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
has a unique
critical point
0
sitting in
\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)
.

By the first part of Proposition
2.5
we see that
\{\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}\}_{|\lambda|\leq\varepsilon_{0}}
satisfies
the uniform (PS) condition on
\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)
. It follows from this, (i) and Theorem
2.3
that

\displaystyle C_{\ast}(\mathcal{F}_{\lambda},0;{\bf K})=C_{\ast}(\mathcal{F}_{0},0;{\bf K}),\quad\forall\lambda\in[-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}].

(3.19)

It remains to prove that the assumptions (v)-(viii) insure that (
3.19
) cannot occur.

By (v), we can assume that
0
is a nondegenerate critical point of
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}

for
0<|\lambda|\leq\varepsilon_{0}
by shrinking
\varepsilon_{0}>0
if necessary.
It follows from this, (
3.19
) and
Theorem
A.1
with
\lambda=0
(or
[
45
, Theorem 2.1]
)
that all
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
,
0<|\lambda|\leq\varepsilon_{0}
,
have the same Morse index
\mu_{\lambda}
at
0\in H
, that is,

\displaystyle[-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}]\setminus\{0\}\ni\lambda\mapsto\mu_{\lambda}\quad\hbox{is constant.}

(3.20)

On the other hand, by
[
41
, Proposition B.2]
, each
\varrho\in\sigma(B_{0}(0))\cap\{t\in\mathbb{R}^{-}\,|\,t\leq 0\}
is an isolated point in
\sigma(B_{0}(0))
, which is also an
eigenvalue of finite multiplicity. (This can also be derived from
[
8
, Lemma 2.2]
.)
Since
0\in\sigma(B_{0}(0))
by (vii),

0
is an isolated point of the spectrum
\sigma(B_{0}(0))
and an eigenvalue of
B_{0}(0)
of the
finite multiplicity
s_{0}
by
[
8
, Lemma 2.2]
.
Thus we can assume

\sigma(B_{0}(0))\cap\{t\in\mathbb{R}^{-}\,|\,t\leq 0\}=\{0,\varrho_{1},\cdots,\varrho_{k}\},

where
\varrho_{i}<0
and has multiplicity
s_{i}
for each
i=1,\cdots,k
.
As above (
3.18
), we may use this and (vi) to prove:
if
0<|\lambda|
is small enough,
B_{\lambda}(0)

has exactly
s_{i}
(possible same) eigenvalues near
\varrho_{i}
, but total dimension
of corresponding eigensubspaces is equal to that of eigensubspace of
\varrho_{i}
.
Hence if
\lambda\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
(resp.
-\lambda\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
) is small enough
we obtain

\mu_{\lambda}=\mu_{0}+r^{+}_{B_{\lambda}(0)}

(resp.
\mu_{-\lambda}=\mu_{0}+r^{-}_{B_{\lambda}(0)}
).
These and (viii) imply

\mu_{\lambda}-\mu_{-\lambda}=r^{+}_{B_{\lambda}(0)}-r^{-}_{B_{\lambda}(0)}\neq 0\quad\hbox{for small $\lambda\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]$},

which contradicts the claim in (
3.20
).

In order to prove the final part, note that under the assumptions of the first paragraph
we may use (a)-(b) and Theorem
2.2
to derive (
3.19
).
The remained arguments are same.
∎

Correspondingly, we have the following converse of Theorem
3.2
.

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
H
,
X
and
U
be as in Hypothesis
1.3
,
and
I\subset\mathbb{R}
a bounded open interval containing
0
.
Let
\mathcal{F}:I\times U\to\mathbb{R}
be such that each

\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}:=\mathcal{F}(\lambda,\cdot)
satisfies Hypothesis
1.3

with corresponding operators
A_{\lambda}
,
B_{\lambda}
,
P_{\lambda}
and
Q_{\lambda}
.
Suppose that either (i)-(viii) in Theorem
3.5
or
(a)-(b) and (v)-(viii) in Theorem
3.5

are satisfied. Then
(0,0)\in I\times U^{X}
is a bifurcation point of the equation

\displaystyle A_{\lambda}(x)=0.

(3.21)

The proof is almost repeating that of Theorem
3.5
. In fact, it suffices to replace
“By Proposition
2.5
” and
“Theorem
A.1
with
\lambda=0
(or
[
45
, Theorem 2.1]
)”
with “By the latter part of Proposition
2.5
” and
“Theorem
A.2
with
\lambda=0
(or
[
41
, (2.7)]
)”, respectively.

Corollary 3.7
.

Let
\lambda^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
. Suppose that
\mathcal{L}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfy
Hypothesis
1.1
without (D1),
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfy Hypothesis
1.2
,
and that for each real
\lambda
near
\lambda^{\ast}
the following holds:

(I.a)

\{u\in H\,|\,{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)u-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)u=\mu u,\,\mu\leq 0\}\subset X
for each
\lambda
near
\lambda^{\ast}
;

(I.b)

{\rm Ker}({\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)-\lambda^{\ast}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0))\neq\{0\}
;

(I.c)

{\rm Ker}({\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0))=\{0\}
for each
\lambda\neq\lambda^{\ast}
near
\lambda^{\ast}
;

(I.d)

\lim_{\lambda\to 0+}r({\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)-(\lambda+\lambda^{\ast})\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0))\neq\lim_{\lambda\to 0-}r({\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)-(\lambda+\lambda^{\ast})\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0))
.

Then
(\lambda^{\ast},0)\in\mathbb{R}\times U
is a bifurcation point of the equation

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(u)-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(u)=0.

(3.22)

Moreover, the condition (I.d) can be replaced by

(I.d’)

{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is invertible,
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)=\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0){\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)

and the positive and negative indexes of inertia of the restriction of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
to
H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}:={\rm Ker}({\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)-\lambda^{\ast}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0))

are different.

Corollary 3.8
.

Let
\lambda^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
. Suppose that
\mathcal{L}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfy
Hypothesis
1.3
without (C) and (D1),
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfy Hypothesis
1.4

with corresponding operators
\widehat{A}
and
\widehat{B}=\widehat{P}+\widehat{Q}
,
and that for each real
\lambda
near
\lambda^{\ast}

the following holds:

(II.a)

\{u\in H\,|\,B(0)u-\lambda\widehat{B}(0)u=\mu u,\,\mu\leq 0\}\cup\{u\in H\,|\,B(0)u-\lambda\widehat{B}(0)u\in X\}\subset X
for each
\lambda
near
\lambda^{\ast}
;

(II.b)

{\rm Ker}(B(0)-\lambda^{\ast}\widehat{B}(0))\neq\{0\}
;

(II.c)

{\rm Ker}(B(0)-\lambda\widehat{B}(0))=\{0\}
for each
\lambda\neq\lambda^{\ast}
near
\lambda^{\ast}
;

(II.d)

\lim_{\lambda\to 0+}r(B(0)-(\lambda+\lambda^{\ast})\widehat{B}(0))\neq\lim_{\lambda\to 0-}r(B(0)-(\lambda+\lambda^{\ast})\widehat{B}(0))
.

Then
(\lambda^{\ast},0)\in\mathbb{R}\times U^{X}
is a bifurcation point of the equation

\displaystyle A(u)-\lambda\widehat{A}(u)=0.

(3.23)

Moreover, the condition (II.d)) can be replaced by

(II.d’)

{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
and
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
in (I.d’) are replaced by
B(0)
and
\widehat{B}(0)
, respectively.

If
\lim_{\lambda\to 0+}r(B(0)-(\lambda+\lambda^{\ast})\widehat{B}(0))-\lim_{\lambda\to 0-}r(B(0)-(\lambda+\lambda^{\ast})\widehat{B}(0))
is an odd, which is stronger than (II.d),
then it follows from Theorem
3.16
(
[
66
, Theorem 6.3]
) that there is continuous bifurcation at
\lambda^{\ast}
;
see Comparison
3.19
in Section
3.3
.

Consider
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}:=\widehat{\mathcal{L}}-(\lambda+\lambda^{\ast})\widehat{\mathcal{L}}
for each real
\lambda
near
0
.
Then Corollary
3.7
(resp. Corollary
3.8
) can follows from Corollary
2.6
and
Theorem
3.5
(resp. Corollary
2.6
and Theorem
3.6
)
if (I) (resp. (II)) holds. As to the “Moreover” parts in these two corollaries, by (
4.24
) in the proof of Corollary
4.4
,
we see that
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
has different Morse indexes at
0\in H
as
\lambda
varies in both sides of
\lambda^{\ast}
.

Based on the arguments in
[
40
]
, we may use
Theorem
3.5
to get a generalization of
[
24
, Theorem 5.4.1]
immediately. See

[
46
, Theorem 3.10]

for a high dimensional analogue (corresponding to

[
24
, Theorems 5.4.2 and 5.7.4]
).

In Corollary
3.7
, if the condition “
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is invertible” in (I.d’)
is strengthened as “
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is positive definite”, other conditions are unnecessary.
That is, we have

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
\mathcal{L}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
(resp.
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
) satisfy
Hypothesis
1.1
with
X=H
(resp. Hypothesis
1.2
).
Suppose that
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is positive definite.
Then
(\lambda^{\ast},0)\in\mathbb{R}\times U
is a bifurcation point of the equation
(
3.22
) if and only if
\lambda^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
is an eigenvalue of

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)u-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)u=0,\;u\in H.

(3.24)

Proof.

Necessity is contained in Corollary
3.3
. It remains to prove sufficiency.
Let
J
denote the inverse of
(\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0))^{1/2}
, which is in
\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)

and commutes with any
S\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
that commutes with
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)
. Define functionals

\mathfrak{L}(u):=\mathcal{L}(Ju),\quad\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}(u):=\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(Ju),\quad u\in J^{-1}(U).

Then for any
u\in J^{-1}(U)
and all
v\in H
we have
\nabla\mathfrak{L}(u)=J\nabla\mathcal{L}(Ju)
,

\nabla\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}(u)=J\nabla\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(Ju)
and

D(\nabla\mathfrak{L})(u)[v]=JD(\nabla\mathcal{L})(Ju)[Jv]\quad\hbox{and}\quad D(\nabla\widehat{\mathfrak{L}})(u)[v]=JD(\nabla\widehat{\mathcal{L}})(Ju)[Jv].

It follows that
\mathfrak{L}\in C^{1}(J^{-1}(U),\mathbb{R})
(resp.
\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}\in C^{1}(J^{-1}(U),\mathbb{R})
) satisfy
Hypothesis
1.1
with
X=H
(resp. Hypothesis
1.2
).
Moreover, the bifurcation problem (
3.22
) is equivalent to the following

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}(u)-\lambda\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}^{\prime}(u)=0,\quad u\in J^{-1}(U)

(3.25)

and that
\lambda^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
is an eigenvalue of (
3.24
) if and only if it is that of

\displaystyle J\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)Ju-\lambda J\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)Ju=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad u-\lambda Lu=0,

(3.26)

where
L:=J\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)J
. Since
L\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is compact,
The eigenvalues of (
3.26
) consists of a sequence of nonzero reals

\{\lambda_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
diverging to infinity, and each of them is
of finite multiplicity. Hence
\lambda^{\ast}=\lambda_{k_{0}}
for some
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
H_{k}
be the eigensubspace corresponding to
\lambda_{k}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, i.e.,

H_{k}={\rm Ker}(I-\lambda_{k}L)={\rm Ker}(\mathfrak{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)-\lambda_{k}\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)),\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{N}.

(3.27)

Then
H=\oplus^{\infty}_{k=0}H_{k}
, where
H_{0}={\rm Ker}(L)={\rm Ker}(\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0))
.

Now the eigenvalue
\lambda^{\ast}=\lambda_{k_{0}}
is isolated and
has finite multiplicity. Let us choose
\delta>0
such that
(\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta)\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}
has no intersection with
\{\lambda_{k}\}^{\infty}_{k=0}
,
where
\lambda_{0}=0
. Since
\mathfrak{L}_{\lambda}:=\mathfrak{L}-\lambda\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}

satisfies Hypothesis
1.1
with
X=H
, it has finite Morse index
\mu_{\lambda}
and nullity
\nu_{\lambda}
at
0
.
Moreover, since each
H_{k}
is an invariant subspace of
\mathfrak{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)-\lambda\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)=I-\lambda L
, and

H=\oplus^{\infty}_{k=0}H_{k}
is an orthogonal decomposition, we have

\mu_{\lambda}=\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\mu_{\lambda,k}
,
where
\mu_{\lambda,k}
is the dimension of
the maximal negative definite space of the quadratic functional

H_{k}\ni u\mapsto f_{\lambda,k}(u):=(\mathfrak{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)u-\lambda\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)u,u)_{H}
.
Note that
f_{\lambda,k}(h)=(1-\lambda/\lambda_{k})(h,h)_{H},\;\forall h\in H_{k}
.
We deduce that
\mu_{\lambda,k}
is equal to

\dim H_{k}
(resp.
0
) if
\lambda>\lambda_{k}
(resp.
\lambda<\lambda_{k}
).
Hence the Morse index of
\mathfrak{L}_{\lambda}
at
0
,

\displaystyle\mu_{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda_{k}<\lambda}\dim H_{k}.

(3.28)

This implies that

\mu_{\lambda}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}},&\quad\forall\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}),\\
\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}+\nu_{\lambda^{\ast}},&\quad\forall\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta),\end{array}\right.

Theorem
A.1
with
\lambda=0
(or
[
45
, Theorem 2.1]
) we obtain that

C_{q}(\mathcal{L}_{\lambda},0;{\bf K})=C_{q}(\mathfrak{L}_{\lambda},0;{\bf K})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\delta_{q\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}}{\bf K},&\quad\forall\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}),\\
\delta_{q(\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}+\nu_{\lambda^{\ast}})}{\bf K},&\quad\forall\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta).\end{array}\right.

On the other hand, as in the proof of Theorem
3.5
,
suppose that
(\lambda^{\ast},0)\in\mathbb{R}\times U
is not a bifurcation point of the equation
(
3.22
). Then
C_{q}(\mathcal{L}_{\lambda},0;{\bf K})
is independent of

\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta)
by shrinking
\delta>0
(if necessary),
which leads to a contradiction.
∎

It is easily seen that the functional
\mathcal{L}
in

[
62
, §4.3, Theorem 4.3]
or in
[
64
, Chap.1, Theorem 3.4]

satisfies the conditions of Theorem
3.9
. Hence the latter is a generalization of

[
64
, Chap.1, Theorem 3.4]
. A stronger generalization will be given in Corollary
4.4
.

Since
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)(X)\subset X
implies
J(X)\subset X
,
slightly modifying the proof of Theorem
3.9
we can obtain:

Theorem 3.10
.

\mathcal{L}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfy
Hypothesis
1.3
without (C) and (D1),
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfy Hypothesis
1.4

with corresponding operators
\widehat{A}
and
\widehat{B}=\widehat{P}+\widehat{Q}
.
Suppose that
B(0)
is positive definite and that

\{u\in H\,|\,B(0)u-\lambda\widehat{B}(0)u=\mu u,\,\mu\leq 0\}\cup\{u\in H\,|\,B(0)u-\lambda\widehat{B}(0)u\in X\}\subset X

for each
\lambda
.
Then
(\lambda^{\ast},0)\in\mathbb{R}\times U^{X}
is a bifurcation point of the equation
(
3.23
) if and only if
\lambda^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
is an eigenvalue of

\displaystyle B(0)u-\lambda\widehat{B}(0)u=0,\;u\in H.

(3.29)

If
\lambda^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
is an eigenvalue of (
3.29
) of odd multiplicity,
then it follows from Theorem
3.16
(
[
66
, Theorem 6.3]
) that there is
continuous bifurcation at
\lambda^{\ast}
;
see Comparison
3.20
in Section
3.3
.

Note that the proofs of Theorems
3.5
,
3.6
,
3.9
and
3.10

do not need the parameterized versions of the splitting lemmas and Morse-Palais lemmas.
In next three sections we shall use them to get stronger results.

In order to compare our method with previous those let us consider the case that
the classical splitting lemma and Morse-Palais lemma for
C^{2}
functionals can be used.
Indeed, it is easily seen that our above proof methods of Theorem
3.5

can lead to:

Theorem 3.11
.

Let
H
,
I
and
U
be as in Theorem
3.5
,
and let
\mathcal{F}:I\times U\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy the following conditions:

(1)

Each
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}\in C^{2}(U,\mathbb{R})
,
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(0)=0
and
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is a Fredholm operator.

(2)

The assumptions (v)-(viii) in Theorem
3.5
hold with
B_{\lambda}(0)=\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(0)
.

(3)

Either the assumptions (a)-(b) in Theorem
3.5
hold, or (i) in Theorem
3.5
holds and

\{\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}\}_{|\lambda|<\epsilon}
satisfies the uniform (PS) condition on some closed neighborhood of
0\in H
for
small
\epsilon>0
.

Then
(0,0)\in I\times U
is a bifurcation point of the equation (
1.1
).

By (vi) in Theorem
3.5
with
B_{\lambda}(0)=\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(0)
,

\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is Fredholm for each
\lambda
near
0\in\mathbb{R}
.

Corollary 3.12
.

Let
U
be an open neighborhood of
0
in a real Hilbert space
H
,
and let
\mathcal{L},\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\in C^{2}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfy

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(0)=0
and
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(0)=0
. Suppose that

\lambda^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
is an isolated eigenvalue of (
3.24
)
of finite multiplicity, that the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

For each
\lambda
near
\lambda^{\ast}
,

\mathcal{L}-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}
satisfies the (PS) condition in a fixed closed neighborhood of
0
.

(2)

\lim_{\lambda\to 0+}r(\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)-(\lambda+\lambda^{\ast})\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0))\neq\lim_{\lambda\to 0-}r(\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)-(\lambda+\lambda^{\ast})\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0))
.

Then
(0,0)\in I\times U
is a bifurcation point of the equation (
3.22
).
Moreover, the condition (2) can be replaced by one of form (I.d’) in Corollary
3.7
.

Since
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)-\lambda^{\ast}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is a Fredholm operator, so is

\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
for each real
\lambda
near
\lambda^{\ast}
.
Compare Corollary
3.12
with Corollaries
4.12
,
4.13
.

By the proof of Theorem
3.9
we easily get

Theorem 3.13
.

Let
U
be an open neighborhood of
0
in a real Hilbert space
H
,
and let
\mathcal{L},\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\in C^{2}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfy

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(0)=0
and
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(0)=0
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is positive definite and
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is compact.
Then
(\lambda^{\ast},0)\in\mathbb{R}\times U
is a bifurcation point of the equation
(
3.22
) if and only if
\lambda^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
is an eigenvalue of
(
3.24
).

Since
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is positive definite we have
\mu_{0}=0
and thus each
\mu_{\lambda}

(the Morse index of
\mathcal{L}-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}
at
0\in H
) is finite by
(
3.28
).

3.3
Comparison with previous work

Comparison with work in
[
15
,
35
]

Based on the center manifold theory Chow and Lauterbach
[
15
]
proved: if
\mathcal{F}\in C^{2}(I\times U,\mathbb{R})

satisfies the following four conditions:

1)

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(0)=0\;\forall\lambda\in I
,

2)

0<\dim{\rm Ker}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}_{0}(0))<\infty
,

3)

0
is isolated in
\sigma(\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}_{0}(0))
,

4)

r^{+}_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(0)}\neq r^{-}_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(0)}
,

then
(0,0)\in I\times U
must be a bifurcation point of (
1.1
).

This result was generalized by Kielhöfer
[
35
]

with the Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction and Conley’s theorem on bifurcation of invariant sets.
The main result of
[
35
]
(see also
[
36
, Theorem II.7.3]
)
can be stated as follows (in our notations):

Let
X\subset H
be a continuously embedded subspace having norm
\|\cdot\|_{X}
,

G:I\times X\to H
continuous,
G(\lambda,0)=0\;\forall\lambda\in I
. Suppose

(A)

G
has a continuous
Fréchet derivative with respect to
u
in a neighborhood of
(0,0)\in I\times X
,
G^{\prime}_{u}(\lambda,0)=D_{\lambda}
,
and
D_{0}:X\to H
is a Fredholm operator of index zero having an isolated eigenvalue
0
;

(B)

there is a differentiable potential
\mathcal{F}:I\times X\to\mathbb{R}

such that
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{u}(\lambda,u)[h]=(G(\lambda,u),h)
for
all
h\in X
and for all
(\lambda,u)
in a neighborhood of
(0,0)\in I\times D
;

(C)

the crossing number of
D_{\lambda}
at
\lambda=0

(defined in
[
36
, Definition II.7.1]
) is nonzero, (which is equivalent to the condition that

r^{+}_{B_{\lambda}}\neq r^{-}_{B_{\lambda}}
if
D_{\lambda}=B_{\lambda}
as in (
3.18
)).

Then
(0,0)\in I\times X
is a bifurcation point for the equation
G(\lambda,u)=0
.

The major difference between these two results and our Theorems
3.5
,
3.6

is that they require the higher smoothness for
G
or
\mathcal{F}
, while we
require some kinds of (PS) conditions.
In particular, comparing Theorem
3.11
with the result by Chow and Lauterbach
[
15
]

stated above it is easily seen that my methods and theirs have both advantages and disadvantages. In applications to quasilinear elliptic systems it is impossible to require higher smoothness for potential functionals.

Comparison with work in
[
25
,
66
,
67
]

For convenience we state the main result and related notions of
[
66
]
in closer notation
in this paper.

Let
X
and
Y
be real Banach spaces, and let

I\subset\mathbb{R}
and
U
be an open interval and an open neighborhood of the origin of
X
, respectively.
The
uniform partial Lipschitz modulus
of a function
G:I\times U\to Y
with respect to
X
at
(\mu,0)\in I\times U

is defined by

L_{X}(G,\mu)=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\sup_{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\lambda,u),(\lambda,v)\in B((\mu,0),\delta)\\
u\neq v\end{array}}\frac{\|G(\lambda,u)-G(\lambda,v)\|}{\|u-v\|},

(3.1)

where
B((\mu,0),\delta)
is the open ball of radius
\delta
in
I\times U
centered at
(\mu,0)
.
For a point
(\mu,0)\in I\times U
and
\delta>0
such that
B((\mu,0),\delta)\subset I\times U
there exists a similar quantity defined by

\Delta_{\delta}(G,\mu)=\sup_{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\lambda,u)\in B((\mu,0),\delta)\\
u\neq 0\end{array}}\frac{\|G(\lambda,u)-G(\mu,u)\|}{\|u\|}.

(3.2)

Let
\Phi_{0}(X,Y)
(resp.
{\rm Iso}(X,Y)
) denote the set of
linear and bounded Fredholm operators of index (resp. Banach space isomorphisms) from
X
to
Y
, and let

F(X,Y)=\{L\in\mathscr{L}(X,Y)\,|\,\dim R(L)<\infty\}
. All of them are equipped with the metric inherited from
\mathscr{L}(X,Y)

without special statements.
Each
L\in\Phi_{0}(X,Y)
has the well-defined
essential conditioning number

\gamma(L)
(
[
66
, page 1042]
).

A
path of Fredholm operators of index zero
is a continuous function
L
from an interval
J

to
\Phi_{0}(X,Y)
. Call
\lambda_{0}\in J
an
isolated singular point
of
L
if there
exists
\delta>0
such that
J_{\lambda_{0},\delta}:=(\lambda_{0}-\delta,\lambda_{0}+\delta)\subset J

and
L(\lambda)\in{\rm Iso}(X,Y)
for
0<|\lambda-\lambda_{0}|<\delta
.
There always exists
S\in C(J_{\lambda_{0},\delta},{\rm Iso}(Y,X))
such that
S(t)L(t)=I_{X}-K(t)\;\forall t\in J_{\lambda_{0},\delta}
,
where each
K(t):X\to X
is a compact linear operator.
For
\lambda\in J_{\lambda_{0},\delta}\setminus\{\lambda_{0}\}
let
{\rm deg}_{\rm L.S}(I_{X}-K(\lambda))
denote
the Leray-Schauder degree of the restriction of
I_{X}-K(\lambda)
to a neighborhood of the origin.
The quantity

\sigma(L,\lambda_{0}):={\rm deg}_{\rm L.S}(I_{X}-K(\lambda_{l})){\rm deg}_{\rm L.S}(I_{X}-K(\lambda_{r}))\in\{-1,1\}

(3.3)

where
\lambda_{0}-\delta<\lambda_{l}<\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{r}<\lambda_{0}+\delta
, does not depend on the choices
of
S
and
\lambda_{l}<\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{r}
, and is called the
local parity
of the path
L

at an isolated singularity
\lambda_{0}
. It is invariant under homotopies and reparametrizations.

Lemma 3.14

(
[
66
, CRITERION 1, page 1048]
)
.

Let
K\in\mathscr{L}(X)
be compact,
L(\lambda)\\
=K-\lambda I_{X}
and
\lambda_{0}\in\sigma(K)\setminus\{0\}
. Then

\lambda_{0}\in J
is an isolated singular point of
L

and
\sigma(L,\lambda_{0})=(-1)^{n}
, where
n
is the algebraic multiplicity of
\lambda_{0}
as an eigenvalues of
K
.

Theorem 3.15

(
[
29
, Theorem 5.12]
)
.

Let
X
and
Y
be real Banach spaces,
X\subset Y
and
X\hookrightarrow Y
be continuous.
Suppose that
\lambda_{0}\in J
is an isolated singular point of a continuous path
L:J\to\Phi_{0}(X,Y)

and that
0
is isolated in the real spectrum of
L(\lambda_{0})
.
Then for any sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
there exists a
\delta>0
such that if
0<|\lambda-\lambda_{0}|\leq\delta
,
then
L(\lambda)
has only a finite number of eigenvalues in
(-\varepsilon,0)
each of which is
finite algebraic multiplicity. If, for
0<|\lambda-\lambda_{0}|\leq\delta
,
n(\lambda)
denotes the sum
of the algebraic multiplicities of the eigenvalues of
L(\lambda)
in
(-\varepsilon,0)
, then
n(\lambda)

is constant on
[\lambda_{0}-\delta,\lambda_{0})
and on
(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}+\delta]
, and moreover

\sigma(L,\lambda_{0})=(-1)^{n(\lambda_{0}+\delta)+n(\lambda_{0}-\delta)}.

We mainly compare our results with the following.

Theorem 3.16

(
[
66
, Theorem 6.3]
)
.

Let
X
and
Y
be real Banach spaces, and let

I\subset\mathbb{R}
and
U
be an open interval and an open neighborhood
of the origin of
X
, respectively.
A map
F:I\times U\to Y
satisfies the following properties.

(B1)

F\in C(I\times U,Y)
and
F(\lambda,0)=0
for all
(\lambda,0)\in I\times U
.

(B2)

F(\lambda,\cdot):X\to Y
is Hadamard differentiable at
0
for all
(\lambda,0)\in I\times U
and

\lambda\mapsto D_{x}F(\lambda,0)\in\mathscr{L}(X,Y)
is continuous.

(B3)

(\lambda_{0},0)\in I\times U
and
D_{x}F(\lambda_{0},0)\in\Phi_{0}(X,Y)
.

(B4)

L_{X}(R,\lambda_{0})<\infty
, where
R(\lambda,x)=F(\lambda,x)-D_{x}F(\lambda,0)x
for
(\lambda,x),(\lambda,0)\in I\times U
, and

\gamma(D_{x}F(\lambda_{0},0)L_{X}(R,\lambda_{0})<1.

(3.4)

(i)

If
\lambda_{0}
is an isolated singular point of
D_{x}F(\cdot,0)
with
\sigma(D_{x}F(\cdot,0),\lambda_{0})=-1
,
then
\lambda_{0}
is a bifurcation point for
F(\lambda,u)=0
. If, in addition,

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\Delta_{\delta}(F,\lambda)=0\quad\hbox{for all $\lambda$ in an open neighbourhood of $\lambda_{0}$},

(3.5)

there is
continuous bifurcation
at
\lambda_{0}
.

(ii)

If
{\rm ker}D_{x}F(\lambda_{0},0)=\{0\}
and
\lim_{\delta\to 0}\Delta_{\delta}(F,\lambda_{0})=0
, then
\lambda_{0}
is not a bifurcation
point for
F(\lambda,u)=0
.

We also need the following lemma.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space with inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{H}

and the induced norm
\|\cdot\|
, and let
X
be a Banach space with
norm
\|\cdot\|_{X}
, such that
X\subset H
is dense in
H
and
\|x\|\leq\|x\|_{X}\;\forall x\in X
.
Let
\mathfrak{B}\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
be a Fredholm operator of index zero.
Suppose that
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}:=\mathfrak{B}|_{X}\in\mathscr{L}(X)
and that

\{u\in H\,|\,\mathfrak{B}u\in X\}\subset X
. Then

i)

\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
is also a Fredholm operator of index zero;

ii)

0
sits in the resolvent set
\rho(\mathfrak{B})
of
\mathfrak{B}
if and only if
0
belongs to
\rho(\hat{\mathfrak{B}})
.

Moreover, if
\{u\in H\,|\,\mathfrak{B}u=\mu u,\,\mu<0\}\subset X
then
\mu<0

is an eigenvalue of
\mathfrak{B}
if and only if it is that of
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
with same (algebraic and geometry) multiplicity.

Proof.

Let
N={\rm Ker}(\mathfrak{B})
. Then
N\subset X
and
N={\rm Ker}(\hat{\mathfrak{B}})
has finite dimension.
Since
\mathfrak{B}
is self-adjoint, the range
R(\mathfrak{B})
of
\mathfrak{B}
is closed and
is equal to
N^{\bot}
. We claim that
R(\hat{\mathfrak{B}})
is closed in
X
. In fact,
let
(x_{k})\subset R(\hat{\mathfrak{B}})
converge to
\bar{x}
in
X
.
Using
H=R(\mathfrak{B})\oplus N
we can decompose
\bar{x}
into
y+z
, where
y\in R(\mathfrak{B})
and
z\in N
.
Take
u\in\mathfrak{B}^{-1}(y)
. Then
\mathfrak{B}(u)=y=\bar{x}-z\in X
.
By the assumption we get that
u\in X
and so
y\in R(\hat{\mathfrak{B}})
. Moreover,
since
(x_{k},z)_{H}=0\;\forall k
, letting
k\to\infty
we get
0=(\bar{x},z)_{H}=(y,z)_{H}+(z,z)_{H}=(z,z)_{H}
.
That is to say,
z=0
and hence
\bar{x}\in X
. The above arguments also show that

X
has a direct sum decomposition of vector spaces
X=R(\hat{\mathfrak{B}})\dot{+}N
.
Thus
\dim X/R(\hat{\mathfrak{B}})=\dim N<\infty
. i) is proved.
Sine the assumption implies
{\rm Ker}(\mathfrak{B})={\rm Ker}(\hat{\mathfrak{B}})
,
the open mapping theorem leads to ii).

Clearly, it suffices to prove that each negative eigenvalue
\mu
of
\mathfrak{B}

is that of
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
. Let
v\in H\setminus\{0\}
be such that

\mu u=\mathfrak{B}u
. By the second assumption we see that
u\in X
and so an eigenvector
of
\hat{\mathfrak{B}}
belonging to
\mu
.

Note that each eigenvalue
\lambda
of a self-adjoint operator on a Hilbert space has the same algebraic and geometry multiplicities, that is,

{\rm Ker}((\lambda I-\mathfrak{B})^{n})={\rm Ker}(\lambda I-\mathfrak{B})
for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
For a negative eigenvalue
\mu
of
\mathfrak{B}
, the second assumption we deduce that

{\rm Ker}(\lambda I-\mathfrak{B})={\rm Ker}(\lambda I_{X}-\hat{\mathfrak{B}})\subset{\rm Ker}((\lambda I_{X}-\hat{\mathfrak{B}})^{n})\subset{\rm Ker}((\lambda I-\mathfrak{B})^{n})

and hence
{\rm Ker}(\lambda I_{X}-\hat{\mathfrak{B}})\subset{\rm Ker}((\lambda I_{X}-\hat{\mathfrak{B}})^{n})
for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
∎

Having the above preparations we can make comparisons with
work of
[
25
,
66
,
67
]
, precisely, Theorem
3.16
and
[
25
, Theorems 4.2, 5.1]
(or
[
67
, Theorems 3.2,3.3]
).

Clearly, for sufficiencies of bifurcations
we only need to compare Theorem
3.16
(i) and
[
25
, Theorem 5.1]
(or
[
67
, Theorem 3.3]
) with results in Section
3.2

because those in Sections
4
,
5
and
6
are either of Rabinowitz type or Fadell–Rabinowitz type.

Theorem
3.5
, Corollary
3.7
and Theorem
3.9
only involve one or two of Hypothesises
1.1
,
1.2
.
Corresponding potential operators are only Gâteaux differentiable, and they have no
comparisons with Theorem
3.16
(i) and
[
25
, Theorem 5.1]
in which
related operators are required to be either Hadamard differentiable or
w
-Hadamard differentiable at
u=0
(see
[
25
,
67
]
for precise definitions).

Theorems
3.6
,
3.10
and Corollary
3.8

have no any relations with
[
25
, Theorem 5.1]

(since the latter is stated in Hilbert spaces).

Of course,
[
25
, Theorem 5.1]
for even functionals which are bounded below cannot lead to
Theorems
3.11
,
3.13
yet.

Hence it remains to make comparisons between Theorem
3.16
(i) and any one of Theorem
3.6
,
Corollary
3.8
and Theorems
3.10
,
3.11
,
3.13
.

Comparison 3.18

(
Comparison with Theorem
3.6
)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
3.6
let us define
F:I\times U^{X}\to X
by
F(\lambda,x)=A_{\lambda}(x)
,
where
U^{X}
is explained as in Hypothesis
1.3
.
Our assumptions on
\mathcal{F}:I\times U^{X}\to\mathbb{R}
cannot give the continuity of
F
.
But (B1) of Theorem
3.16
requires
F
to be continuous.
Our condition “each
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}:=\mathcal{F}(\lambda,\cdot)
satisfies Hypothesis
1.3
”
implies that
F(\lambda,\cdot)
has the strict Fréchet derivative at
0\in X
,
