D_{x}F(\lambda,0)=B_{\lambda}(0)|_{X}\in\mathscr{L}(X)
.
But the condition (vi) in Theorem
3.5
only says that
I\ni\lambda\mapsto B_{\lambda}(0)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)

is continuous at
\lambda=0
, and has no any conclusions for the continuity of the path

I\ni\lambda\mapsto B_{\lambda}(0)|_{X}\in\mathscr{L}(X)
. Hence Theorem
3.6
cannot be included in
Theorem
3.16
(i) (
[
66
, Theorem 6.3(i)]
).

Comparison 3.19

(
Comparison with Corollary
3.8
)
.

Let the assumptions of Corollary
3.8
be satisfied.
Define
F:I\times U^{X}\to X
by
F(\lambda,x)=A(x)-\lambda\widehat{A}(x)
.
By Hypothesis
1.3
this is continuous, and each

F(\lambda,\cdot)
has the strict Fréchet derivative at
0\in X
,
D_{x}F(\lambda,0)=B(0)|_{X}-\lambda\widehat{B}(0)|_{X}\in\mathscr{L}(X)
.
Hence
F
satisfies the assumptions (B1) and (B2) in Theorem
3.16
. By the assumptions,

B(0)-\lambda\widehat{B}(0)=P(0)+(Q(0)-\lambda\widehat{B}(0))\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
,
P(0)
is positive definite,
and
Q(0)-\lambda\widehat{B}(0)
is compact. Thus

B(0)-\lambda\widehat{B}(0):H\to H
is a
Fredholm operator of index zero. Using (II.a) and Lemma
3.17
we deduce that each

D_{x}F(\lambda,0)=B(0)|_{X}-\lambda\widehat{B}(0)|_{X}\in\mathscr{L}(X)
is also
a Fredholm operator of index zero. This means that the assumption (B3) in Theorem
3.16

holds with
F
and
\lambda_{0}=\lambda\in I
. For any fixed
\lambda_{0}\in I
we claim that

L_{X}(R,\lambda_{0})=0
, where for
(\lambda,x)\in I\times U
,

R(\lambda,x)=F(\lambda,x)-D_{x}F(\lambda,0)x=A(x)-(B(0)|_{X})x-\lambda(\widehat{A}(x)-(\widehat{B}(0)|_{X})x).

In fact, since
A(0)=0=\widehat{A}(0)
, and
A
and
\widehat{A}
have strict Fréchet derivatives

B(0)|_{X}
and
\widehat{B}(0)|_{X}
at
0\in X
, respectively, for any
\epsilon>0
there exists a small
\nu>0
such that

\|A(u)-A(v)-B(0)|_{X}(u-v)\|_{X}\leq\epsilon\|u-v\|_{X}\quad\hbox{and}\quad

\|\widehat{A}(u)-\widehat{A}(v)-\widehat{B}(0)|_{X}(u-v)\|_{X}\leq\epsilon\|u-v\|_{X}

for any
u,v\in U
satisfying
\|u\|_{X}<\nu
and
\|v\|_{X}<\nu
. It follows that

\|R(\lambda,u)-R(\lambda,v)\|_{X}\leq(1+|\lambda|)\epsilon\|u-v\|_{X}

and hence
L_{X}(R,\lambda_{0})=0
by (
3.1
). Then (B4)
and (
3.4
) in Theorem
3.16
hold with any
\lambda_{0}\in I
.

Moreover, for any
\lambda,\mu\in I
and
u\in U
, if
|\mu-\lambda|+\|u\|_{X}<\nu
we have

\displaystyle\|F(\lambda,u)-F(\mu,u)\|_{X}=|\lambda-\mu|\cdot\|\widehat{A}(u)\|_{X}\leq|\lambda-\mu|(\|\widehat{B}(0)|_{X}\|+\epsilon)\|u\|_{X}.

By (
3.2
) this means

\Delta_{\nu}(F,\lambda)\leq|\lambda-\mu|(\|\widehat{B}(0)|_{X}\|+\epsilon).

It follows that
\lim_{\nu\to 0}\Delta_{\nu}(F,\lambda)=0
for any
\lambda\in I
, that is,
(
3.5
) holds.

Define
\alpha:I\to\Phi_{0}(H,H)
by
\alpha(\lambda)=B(0)-\lambda\widehat{B}(0)
.
It is a path of Fredholm operators of index zero. By (II.b) and (II.c) of Corollary
3.8

we see that
\lambda^{\ast}
is an isolated singular point of
\alpha
.
Using Hypothesis
1.3
we have
\alpha^{X}(\lambda):=B(0)|_{X}-\lambda\widehat{B}(0)|_{X}\in\mathscr{L}(X)
.
It follows from Lemma
3.17
and (II.a) of Corollary
3.8
that
\alpha^{X}:I\to\Phi_{0}(X,X)

is also a path of Fredholm operators of index zero and has an isolated singular point
\lambda^{\ast}
and that

\alpha(\lambda)
and
\alpha^{X}(\lambda)
have same negative eigenvalues and
algebraic multiplicities. Let

\displaystyle r_{+}=\lim_{\lambda\to 0+}r(B(0)-(\lambda+\lambda^{\ast})\widehat{B}(0))\quad\hbox{and}

\displaystyle r_{-}=\lim_{\lambda\to 0-}r(B(0)-(\lambda+\lambda^{\ast})\widehat{B}(0)).

By the definitions of
r(B_{\lambda})
above (
3.18
) and
n(\lambda)
in Theorem
3.15

we have
r_{+}=n(\lambda^{\ast}+\delta)
and
r_{-}=n(\lambda^{\ast}-\delta)
for
\delta>0
small enough.
Then Theorem
3.15
gives

\sigma(\alpha,\lambda^{\ast})=\sigma(\alpha^{X},\lambda^{\ast})=(-1)^{r_{+}+r_{-}}.

Clearly, the condition that
\sigma(\alpha^{X},\lambda^{\ast})=-1
is stronger than the assumption
r_{+}\neq r_{-}
. This shows that Corollary
3.8
cannot be derived
from Theorem
3.16
(i).

If
\sigma(\alpha^{X},\lambda^{\ast})=-1
, i.e.,
r_{+}-r_{-}
is an odd, then by Theorem
3.16
(i) there is continuous bifurcation at
\lambda^{\ast}
.

Comparison 3.20

(
Comparison with Theorem
3.10
)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
3.10
, since
B(0)
is positive definite and

\widehat{B}(0)
is compact, each eigenvalue of (
3.29
) is isolated and has finite algebraic multiplicity.
By arguments in Comparison
3.19
we see that the map

F:{\mathbb{R}}\times U^{X}\to X
defined by
F(\lambda,x)=A(x)-\lambda\widehat{A}(x)
satisfies
(B1)-(B4) and (
3.5
) in Theorem
3.16
with
\lambda_{0}=\lambda^{\ast}
.
Let
\alpha
and
\alpha^{X}
be as above. Then we have
\sigma(\alpha,\lambda^{\ast})=\sigma(\alpha^{X},\lambda^{\ast})
.
Moreover, by Lemma
3.14
we get
\sigma(\alpha,\lambda^{\ast})=(-1)^{n}
,
where
n
is the algebraic multiplicity of
\lambda^{\ast}
as an eigenvalues of (
3.29
).
In order to apply Theorem
3.16
(i) it is required that
n
is an odd. But our
condition in Theorem
3.10
is that
n\neq 0
, i.e.,
\lambda^{\ast}
is an eigenvalues of (
3.29
).
As above, if
\sigma(\alpha,\lambda^{\ast})=-1
then it follows from Theorem
3.16
(i) that
there is continuous bifurcation at
\lambda^{\ast}
.

Comparison 3.21

(
Comparison with Theorems
3.11
,
3.13
)
.

Theorem
3.11
is a special case of Theorem
3.5
.
Though each potential operator
\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
is
C^{1}
,
Theorem
3.11
cannot be derived from Theorem
3.16
(i) yet because

I\times U\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto\nabla\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}\in H
is not assumed to be continuous.

Theorem
3.13
is a special case of Theorem
3.9
.
Though
I\times U\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto\nabla\mathcal{L}-\lambda\nabla\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\in H

is
C^{1}
and hence satisfies (B1)-(B4) and (
3.5
) in Theorem
3.16
with
\lambda_{0}=\lambda^{\ast}
,
using the same arguments as those at the end of Comparison
3.19
or
3.20

we see that Theorem
3.13
cannot follow from Theorem
3.16
(i) yet.

For necessary conditions of bifurcations we only need to compare
Theorem
3.16
(ii) and
[
25
, Theorem 4.2]
(or
[
67
, Theorem 3.2]
)
with results in Section
3.1
.

Theorem
3.1
and Corollary
3.3
cannot be derived from any one of
Theorem
3.16
(ii) and
[
25
, Theorem 4.2]
because potential operators in the preceding two results
are only Gâteaux differentiable and related operators in the latter two results
involve either Hadamard differentiability or
w
-Hadamard differentiability.

[
25
, Theorem 4.2]
is stated in Hilbert spaces and hence cannot include
Theorem
3.2
and Corollary
3.4
.
As in Comparison
3.18
, Theorem
3.2
cannot follow from Theorem
3.16
(ii).

Finally, let us compare Theorem
3.16
(ii) with Corollary
3.4
with
n=1
.
By the arguments in Comparison
3.19
we see that
the map
F:I\times U^{X}\to X
defined by
F(\lambda,x)=A(x)-\lambda\widehat{A}_{1}(x)

satisfies (B1)-(B2), (B4) and (
3.5
) in Theorem
3.16
with
\lambda_{0}=\lambda^{\ast}
.
But in the present case there is no the condition “
\{u\in H\,|\,B(0)u-\lambda\widehat{B}(0)u\in X\}\subset X
for each
\lambda
near
\lambda^{\ast}
”
as in (II.a) of Corollary
3.8
. Thus we cannot use it and Lemma
3.17
to deduce that each

D_{x}F(\lambda,0)=B(0)|_{X}-\lambda\widehat{B}(0)|_{X}\in\mathscr{L}(X)
is also
a Fredholm operator of index zero, that is, the assumption (B3) in Theorem
3.16
.
Hence Corollary
3.4
with
n=1
cannot be derived from Theorem
3.16
(ii).

4
Some new bifurcation results of Rabinowitz type

Let
H
be a real Hilbert space,
U
an open neighborhood of
0
in
H
,
and let
f\in C^{2}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfy
f^{\prime}(0)=0
.
A classical bifurcation theorem by Rabinowitz
[
59
]
claimed:
If
\lambda^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
is an isolated eigenvalue of
f^{\prime\prime}(0)
of finite multiplicity,
then
(\lambda^{\ast},0)
is a bifurcation point of the equation
\nabla f(u)=\lambda u
and
at least one of the following alternatives occurs:

(i)

(\lambda^{\ast},0)
is not an isolated solution in
\{\lambda^{\ast}\}\times U
of the equation
\nabla f(u)=\lambda u
;

(ii)

for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
near
\lambda^{\ast}
there is a nontrivial solution

u_{\lambda}
of the equation
\nabla f(u)=\lambda u
converging to
0
as
\lambda\to\lambda^{\ast}
;

(iii)

there is an one-sided neighborhood
\Lambda
of
\lambda^{\ast}
such that
for any
\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}
, the equation
\nabla f(u)=\lambda u
has at least
two nontrivial solutions converging to zero as
\lambda\to\lambda^{\ast}
.

Under the same hypotheses Böhme
[
9
]
and Marino
[
48
]
independently proved that
for each small
r>0
, the equation
\nabla f(u)=\lambda u
possesses at least
two distinct one parameter families of solutions
(\lambda(r),u(r))
having
\|u(r)\|=r
and
\lambda(r)\to\lambda^{\ast}

as
r\to 0
. McLeod and Turner
[
50
]
extended the latter result to functions
of class
C^{1,1}
depending linearly on two parameters and such that the Lipschitz constant of the gradient goes to
zero as the parameters and
u
go to zero. Ioffe and Schwartzman
[
31
]

generalized the above Rabinowitz’s bifurcation theorem to equations
Lu+\nabla\varphi_{\lambda}(u)=\lambda u

with
\varphi
continuous jointly in
(\lambda,u)
and
\varphi_{\lambda}
of class
C^{1,1}
, where
L\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
and
the Lipschitz constant of
\nabla\varphi_{\lambda}
satisfies some additional conditions.

In this section we shall give a few new bifurcation results as the above Rabinowitz bifurcation theorem.
As said in Introduction, we can reduce some of them to the following
finite-dimensional result of the above Rabinowitz’s type, which may be obtained as a corollary of
[
31
, Theorem 2]

(or
[
19
, Theorem 4.2]
).

Theorem 4.1

(
[
11
, Theorem 5.1]
)
.

Let
X
be a finite dimensional normed space, let
\delta>0
,
\epsilon>0
,
\lambda^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
and
for every
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]
, let

f_{\lambda}:B_{X}(0,\epsilon)\to\mathbb{R}
be a function of class
C^{1}
.
Assume that

a)

the functions
\{(\lambda,u)\to f_{\lambda}(u)\}
and

\{(\lambda,u)\to f^{\prime}_{\lambda}(u)\}
are continuous on

[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\times B_{X}(0,\epsilon)
;

b)

u=0
is a critical point of
f_{\lambda^{\ast}}
;

c)

f_{\lambda}
has an isolated local minimum (maximum) at zero for every

\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]
and an isolated local maximum (minimum) at
zero for every
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast})
.

Then one at least of the following assertions holds:

i)

u=0
is not an isolated critical point of
f_{\lambda^{\ast}}
;

ii)

for every
\lambda\neq\lambda^{\ast}
in a neighborhood of
\lambda^{\ast}
there is a nontrivial critical point of

f_{\lambda}
converging to zero as
\lambda\to\lambda^{\ast}
;

iii)

there is an one-sided (right or left) neighborhood of
\lambda^{\ast}
such that for every

\lambda\neq\lambda^{\ast}
in the neighborhood there are two distinct nontrivial critical points of
f_{\lambda}

converging to zero as
\lambda\to\lambda^{\ast}
.

In particular,
(\lambda^{\ast},0)\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\times B_{X}(0,\epsilon)

is a bifurcation point of
f^{\prime}_{\lambda}(u)=0
.

Note that the local minimum (maximum) in the assumption c) must be strict!

The following is the main result in this section.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{L}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
(resp.
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
) satisfy
Hypothesis
1.1
with
X=H
(resp. Hypothesis
1.2
),
and let
\lambda^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
be an isolated eigenvalue of

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)v-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)v=0,\quad v\in H.

(4.1)

(If
\lambda^{\ast}=0
, it is enough that
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfies Hypothesis
1.2

without requirement that each
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(u)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is compact.)
Suppose that the Morse indexes of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}:=\mathcal{L}-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}

at
0\in H
take, respectively, values
\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}
and
\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}+\nu_{\lambda^{\ast}}

as
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
varies in
two deleted half neighborhoods of
\lambda^{\ast}
,
where
\mu_{\lambda}
and
\nu_{\lambda}
are the Morse index and the nullity of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}

at
0
, respectively. Then one of the following alternatives occurs:

(i)

(\lambda^{\ast},0)
is not an isolated solution in
\{\lambda^{\ast}\}\times U
of

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(u)=\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}(u);

(4.2)

(ii)

for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
near
\lambda^{\ast}
there is a nontrivial solution

u_{\lambda}
of (
4.2
) converging to
0
as
\lambda\to\lambda^{\ast}
;

(iii)

there is an one-sided neighborhood
\Lambda
of
\lambda^{\ast}
such that
for any
\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}
,
(
4.2
) has at least two nontrivial solutions converging to
zero as
\lambda\to\lambda^{\ast}
.

In particular,
(\lambda^{\ast},0)\in\mathbb{R}\times U
is a bifurcation point for the equation
(
4.2
).

When
X=H
let us compare the assumptions of Theorem
4.2
with those of Corollary
3.7
.
The assumption on the Morse indexes of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
at
0\in H
in the former
is stronger than the condition (I.d) in the latter. It means that one of

\lim_{\lambda\to 0+}r(B(0)-(\lambda+\lambda^{\ast})\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0))
and

\lim_{\lambda\to 0-}r(B(0)-(\lambda+\lambda^{\ast})\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0))

is equal to
0
, and another is equal to
\nu_{\lambda^{\ast}}
.

Clearly, our assumptions and those by Ioffe and Schwartzman
[
31
]
cannot be contained each other.
In Remark
4.5
we shall compare it with the above Rabinowitz bifurcation theorem.

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.

Take
\delta>0
such that
[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}

contains no eigenvalues of (
4.1
). Then

0\in H
is a nondegenerate (and thus isolated) critical point of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}

for each
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}
.

Firstly, we assume that
the Morse index
\mu_{\lambda}
of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mu_{\lambda}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}},&\quad\forall\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}),\\
\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}+\nu_{\lambda^{\ast}},&\quad\forall\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta].\end{array}\right.

It follows from Theorem
A.1
that for any
q\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\displaystyle C_{q}(\mathcal{L}_{\lambda},0;{\bf K})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\delta_{q\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}}{\bf K},&\quad\forall\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}),\\
\delta_{q(\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}+\nu_{\lambda^{\ast}})}{\bf K},&\quad\forall\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta].\end{array}\right.

Let
H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
be the eigenspace of (
4.1
) associated with
\lambda^{\ast}
and
(H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}})^{\bot}

the orthogonal complementary of
H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
in
H
.
Applying
[
45
, Theorem 2.16]

to
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}=\mathcal{L}_{\lambda^{\ast}}+(\lambda-\lambda^{\ast})\widehat{\mathcal{L}}

with
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]
,
we have
\epsilon>0
and a unique continuous map

\psi:[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\times(B_{H}(0,\epsilon)\cap H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}})\to(H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}})^{\bot}

such that for each
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]
,

\psi(\lambda,0)=0
and

\displaystyle P^{\bot}_{\lambda^{\ast}}\nabla\mathcal{L}(z+\psi(\lambda,z))-\lambda P^{\bot}_{\lambda^{\ast}}\nabla\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(z+\psi(\lambda,z))=0\quad\forall z\in B_{H}(0,\epsilon)\cap H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}},

where
P^{\bot}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
is the orthogonal projection onto
(H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}})^{\bot}
; moreover
the functional

\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}:B_{H}(0,\epsilon)\cap H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}\to\mathbb{R},\;z\mapsto\mathcal{L}(z+\psi(\lambda,z))-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(z+\psi(\lambda,z))

(4.9)

is of class
C^{1}
, and has differential at
z\in B_{H}(0,\epsilon)\cap H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
given by

\displaystyle D\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}(z)[h]=D\mathcal{L}(z+\psi(\lambda,z))[h]-\lambda D\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(z+\psi(\lambda,z))[h],\quad\forall h\in H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}.

(4.10)

Note that for each
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]
,
the map
z\mapsto z+\psi({\lambda},z))
induces an one-to-one correspondence
between the critical points of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}^{\circ}
near
0\in H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}

and those of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
near
0\in H
. Hence the problem is reduced to finding
the critical points of
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}

near
0\in H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
for
\lambda
near
\lambda^{\ast}
.

Now by Theorem
A.1
and
[
45
, Theorem 2.18]
,

0\in H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
is also an
isolated critical point of
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}
, and
from (
4
) and (
4
) we may derive that for any
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\displaystyle C_{j}(\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda},0;{\bf K})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\delta_{j0}{\bf K},&\quad\forall\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}),\\
\delta_{j\nu_{\lambda^{\ast}}}{\bf K},&\quad\forall\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta].\end{array}\right.

For a
C^{1}
function
\varphi
on a neighborhood of the origin
0\in\mathbb{R}^{N}

we may always find
\tilde{\varphi}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N},\mathbb{R})
such that it agrees with
\varphi
near
0\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and is also
coercive (so satisfies the (PS)-condition).
Suppose that
0
is an isolated critical point of
\varphi
. By Corollary 6.96, Proposition 6.97 and Example 6.45 in
[
52
]
,
we obtain

\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}&C_{k}(\varphi,0;{\bf K})=\delta_{k0}\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\hbox{ $0$ is a strict local minimizer of $\varphi$},\\
&C_{k}(\varphi,0;{\bf K})=\delta_{kN}\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\hbox{ $0$ is a strict local maximizer of $\varphi$}\end{array}\right\}

(4.14)

and thus
C_{0}(\varphi,0;{\bf K})=0=C_{N}(\varphi,0;{\bf K})

if
0\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
is neither a local maximizer nor a local
minimizer of
\varphi
.

Because of these, (
4
) and (
4.14
) lead to

\displaystyle 0\in H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}\;\hbox{is a strict local}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\hbox{minimizer of}\;\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda},&\quad\forall\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}),\\
\hbox{maximizer of}\;\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda},&\quad\forall\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta].\end{array}\right.

By this and Theorem
4.1
, one of the following possibilities occurs:

(1)

0\in H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
is not an isolated critical point of
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
;

(2)

for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
near
\lambda^{\ast}
,

\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}
has a nontrivial critical point converging to

0\in H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
as
\lambda\to\lambda^{\ast}
;

(3)

there is an one-sided neighborhood
\Lambda
of
\lambda^{\ast}
such that
for any
\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}
,

\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}
has two nontrivial critical points
converging to zero as
\lambda\to\lambda^{\ast}
.

Obviously, they lead to (i), (ii) and (iii), respectively.

Next, assume

\displaystyle\mu_{\lambda}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}+\nu_{\lambda^{\ast}},&\quad\forall\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}),\\
\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}},&\quad\forall\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta].\end{array}\right.

Then we can obtain

\displaystyle 0\in H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}\;\hbox{is a strict local}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\hbox{maximizer of}\;\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda},&\quad\forall\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}),\\
\hbox{minimizer of}\;\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda},&\quad\forall\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta].\end{array}\right.

This also leads to either (i) or (ii) or (iii).∎

From the proof of Theorem
4.2
it is easily seen that Theorem
4.2
(i)
may be replaced by “
0\in H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
is not an isolated critical point of

\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
”. In fact, they are equivalent in the present case.

The following two corollaries strengthen Corollary
3.7
.

Corollary 4.3
.

Let
\mathcal{L}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
(resp.
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
) satisfy
Hypothesis
1.1
with
X=H
(resp. Hypothesis
1.2
),
and let
\lambda^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
be an isolated eigenvalue of (
4.1
).
(If
\lambda^{\ast}=0
, it is enough that
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfies Hypothesis
1.2

without requirement that each
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(u)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is compact.)
Suppose that
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is either semi-positive or semi-negative.
Then the conclusions of Theorem
4.2
hold true.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that the assumption about
the Morse indexes of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}=\mathcal{L}-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}

at
0\in H
in Theorem
4.2
can be satisfied.
Since
\lambda^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
is an isolated eigenvalue of (
4.1
),
by the proof of Theorem
4.2
we have
\delta>0
such that

0\in H
is a nondegenerate critical point of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
for each
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}
. This is equivalent to
the fact that
0\in H
is a nondegenerate critical point of the
C^{\infty}
functional

H\ni u\mapsto\mathfrak{L}_{\lambda}(u):=([\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)]u,u)_{H}

for each
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}
. Hence

\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)u-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)u\neq 0,\quad\forall u\in H\setminus\{0\},\quad\forall\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}.

This shows that
0\in H
is a unique critical point of each
\mathfrak{L}_{\lambda}
.
Since
\nabla\mathfrak{L}_{\lambda}(u)=\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)u-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)u=P(0)u+Q(0)u-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)u
is a bounded linear Fredholm operator,
it is easily seen that
\mathfrak{L}_{\lambda}
satisfies the (PS) condition on any closed ball.
Hence for any small
0<\epsilon<\delta
,
the families

\{\mathfrak{L}_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}-\epsilon]\}
and

\{\mathfrak{L}_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}+\epsilon,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\}

satisfy the conditions of Theorem
2.2
on any closed ball
\bar{B}_{H}(0,r)
.
Thus the critical groups
C_{\ast}(\mathfrak{L}_{\lambda},0;{\bf K})

are all isomorphic for all
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast})
(resp.

\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]
) because of arbitrariness of
\epsilon>0
.
Note that

C_{q}(\mathfrak{L}_{\lambda},0;{\bf K})=\delta_{q\mu_{\lambda}}{\bf K}

for each
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}
.
It follows that

\mu_{\lambda}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}-\delta},&\quad\forall\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}),\\
\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}+\delta},&\quad\forall\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta].\end{array}\right.

(4.24)

We assume now that
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is semi-positive. Then

\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)-\lambda_{1}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)\geq\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)-\lambda_{2}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0),\quad\forall\;\lambda^{\ast}-\delta\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\lambda^{\ast}+\delta.

Since all
\mu_{\lambda}
and
\nu_{\lambda}
are finite,
from this,
[
1
, Proposition 2.3.3]
and (
4.24
) we derive

\displaystyle\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}-\delta}\leq\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}\leq\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}+\delta},

\displaystyle\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}-\delta}\leq\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}+\nu_{\lambda^{\ast}}\leq\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}+\delta},

\displaystyle\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}\leq\lim_{\lambda\to\lambda^{\ast}}\inf\mu_{\lambda}=\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}-\delta},

\displaystyle\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}+\nu_{\lambda^{\ast}}\geq\lim_{\lambda\to\lambda^{\ast}}\sup\mu_{\lambda}=\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}+\delta}.

These imply that (
4
) is satisfied.

Similarly, if

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is semi-negative, then (
4
) holds true.
The desired conclusions are obtained.
∎

Note that (
3.11
) has no isolated eigenvalues if

\cap^{n}_{j=1}{\rm Ker}(\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}_{j}(0))\cap{\rm Ker}(\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0))\neq\{0\}
.
It is natural to ask when
\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}
is an isolated eigenvalue of (
3.11
).
We also consider the case
n=1
merely.
Suppose that
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is invertible.
Then
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)
cannot be negative definite because
\mathcal{L}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfies Hypothesis
1.1
with
X=H
.
It follows that
0
is not an eigenvalue of (
4.1
), and that
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}

is an eigenvalue of (
4.1
) if and only if
1/\lambda

is an eigenvalue of the compact linear self-adjoint operator
L:=[\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)]^{-1}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
.
By Riesz-Schauder theory, the spectrum of
L
,
\sigma(L)
,
consists of zero and a sequence of real eigenvalues of finite multiplicity
converging to zero,
\{1/\lambda_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
. (Hence
|\lambda_{k}|\to\infty
as
k\to\infty
.)
Let
H_{k}
be the eigenspace corresponding to
1/\lambda_{k}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then
H=\oplus^{\infty}_{k=0}H_{k}
, where
H_{0}={\rm Ker}(L)={\rm Ker}(\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0))
and

H_{k}={\rm Ker}(I/\lambda_{k}-L)={\rm Ker}(\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)-\lambda_{k}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)),\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{N}.

(4.25)

Corollary 4.4
.

Let
\mathcal{L}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
(resp.
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
) satisfy
Hypothesis
1.1
with
X=H
(resp. Hypothesis
1.2
). Suppose that the following two conditions
satisfied:

(a)

\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is invertible
and
\lambda^{\ast}=\lambda_{k_{0}}
is an eigenvalue of (
4.1
).

(b)

\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)=\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)
(so
each
H_{k}
is an invariant subspace of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)
), and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)

is either positive or negative on
H_{k_{0}}
.

Then the conclusions of Theorem
4.2
hold true.
Moreover, if
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is positive definite, the condition (b) is unnecessary.

Clearly, the “Moreover” part strengthens Theorem
3.9
and so

[
62
, §4.3, Theorem 4.3]
or in
[
64
, Chap.1, Theorem 3.4]
.

Proof of Corollary
4.4
.

We only need to prove that the conditions of Theorem
4.2
are satisfied.
By the assumptions the eigenvalue
\lambda^{\ast}\neq 0
is isolated and
has finite multiplicity. Let us choose
\delta>0
such that
(\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta)\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}
has no intersection with
\{\lambda_{k}\}^{\infty}_{k=0}
,
where
\lambda_{0}=0
. Since each
H_{k}
is an invariant subspace of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
by (b), and

H=\oplus^{\infty}_{k=0}H_{k}
is an orthogonal decomposition, we have

\mu_{\lambda}=\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\mu_{\lambda,k}
,
where
\mu_{\lambda,k}
is the dimension of
the maximal negative definite space of the quadratic functional

H_{k}\ni u\mapsto f_{\lambda,k}(u):=(\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)u-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)u,u)_{H}
.
Note that
H_{k}
has an orthogonal decomposition
H_{k}^{+}\oplus H_{k}^{-}
, where

H_{k}^{+}
(resp.
H_{k}^{-}
) is the positive (resp. negative) definite subspace
of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)|_{H_{k}}
,
and
f_{\lambda,k}(h)=(1-\lambda/\lambda_{k})(\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)h,h)_{H},\;\forall h\in H_{k}
, we deduce
that
\mu_{\lambda,k}
is equal to

\dim H^{+}_{k}
(resp.
\dim H^{-}_{k}
) if
\lambda>\lambda_{k}
(resp.
\lambda<\lambda_{k}
).
Hence the Morse index of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
at
0
,

\displaystyle\mu_{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda_{k}<\lambda}\dim H_{k}^{+}+\sum_{\lambda_{n}>\lambda}\dim H_{n}^{-}.

(4.26)

(Here we do not use the finiteness of
\mu_{\lambda}
.) Let
\nu^{+}_{\lambda^{\ast}}=\dim H^{+}_{k_{0}}
(resp.
\nu^{-}_{\lambda^{\ast}}=\dim H^{-}_{k_{0}}
).
Using (
4.26
) it is easy to verify that

\mu_{\lambda}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}+\nu^{-}_{\lambda^{\ast}},&\quad\forall\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}),\\
\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}+\nu^{+}_{\lambda^{\ast}},&\quad\forall\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta),\end{array}\right.

Since
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)

is either positive or negative on
H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
, we get
either (
4
) or (
4
).
Note that
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}:=\mathcal{L}-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}

satisfies Hypothesis
1.1
with
X=H
. It has finite Morse index
\mu_{\lambda}
and nullity
\nu_{\lambda}
at
0
.
Thus (
4
) and (
4
) can be obtained.

If
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is positive definite, by the proof of Theorem
3.9

we have (
4
) and hence (
4
).
∎

Remark 4.5
.

It is possible to prove a generalization of
[
45
, Theorem 2.16]
similar to Theorem
A.3
,
which leads to a more general version of Theorem
4.2
as the following Theorem
4.6
.
These will be explored otherwise.

Now we study corresponding analogue of the above results in the setting of
[
40
,
41
]
.
Here is a more general form of the analogue of Theorem
4.2
.

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
H
,
X
and
U
be as in Hypothesis
1.3
,
and let
\{\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})\,|\,\lambda\in\Lambda\}
be a continuous family of functionals
parameterized by an open interval
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}
containing
\lambda^{\ast}
.
For each
\lambda\in\Lambda
, assume
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(0)=0
, and that
there exists a map
A_{\lambda}\in C^{1}(U^{X},X)

such that for all
x\in U\cap X
and
u,v\in X
,

D\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}(x)[u]=(A_{\lambda}(x),u)_{H}\quad\hbox{and}\quad(DA_{\lambda}(x)[u],v)_{H}=(B_{\lambda}(x)u,v)_{H}.

Suppose also that the following conditions hold.

(a)

B_{\lambda}
has a decomposition

B_{\lambda}=P_{\lambda}+Q_{\lambda}
, where for each
x\in U\cap X
,

P_{\lambda}(x)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is positive definitive and

Q_{\lambda}(x)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is compact, so that

(\mathcal{L}_{\lambda},H,X,U,A_{\lambda},B_{\lambda}=P_{\lambda}+Q_{\lambda})

satisfies Hypothesis
1.3
.

(b)

For each
h\in H
, it holds that
\|P_{\lambda}(x)h-P_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)h\|\to 0

as
x\in U\cap X
approaches to
0
in
H
and
\lambda\in\Lambda
converges to
\lambda^{\ast}
.

(c)

For some small
\delta>0
, there exist positive constants
c_{0}>0
such that

(P_{\lambda}(x)u,u)\geq c_{0}\|u\|^{2}\quad\forall u\in H,\;\forall x\in\bar{B}_{H}(0,\delta)\cap X,\quad\forall\lambda\in\Lambda.

(d)

Q_{\lambda}:U\cap X\to\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is uniformly continuous at
0
with respect to
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

(e)

If
\lambda\in\Lambda
converges to
\lambda^{\ast}
then

\|Q_{\lambda}(0)-Q_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)\|\to 0
.

(f)

{\rm Ker}(B_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0))\neq\{0\}
,
{\rm Ker}(B_{\lambda}(0))=\{0\}
for any
\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}
,
and the Morse indexes of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}

at
0\in H
take, respectively, values
\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}
and
\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}+\nu_{\lambda^{\ast}}

as
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
varies in
two deleted half neighborhoods of
\lambda^{\ast}
,
where
\mu_{\lambda}
and
\nu_{\lambda}
are the Morse index and the nullity of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}

at
0
, respectively.

Then one of the following alternatives occurs:

(i)

(\lambda^{\ast},0)
is not an isolated solution in
\{\lambda^{\ast}\}\times U^{X}
of the equation

A_{\lambda}(u)=0,\quad(\lambda,u)\in\Lambda\times U^{X}.

(4.27)

(ii)

For every
\lambda\in\Lambda
near
\lambda^{\ast}
there is a nontrivial solution
u_{\lambda}
of (
4.27
) in
U^{X}
,
which converges to
0
in
X
as
\lambda\to\lambda^{\ast}
.

(iii)

There is an one-sided neighborhood
\Lambda^{\ast}
of
\lambda^{\ast}
such that for any
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\ast}\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}
,
(
4.27
) has at least two nontrivial solutions in
U^{X}
,
which converge to
0
in
X
as
\lambda\to\lambda^{\ast}
.

Therefore
(\lambda^{\ast},0)\in\Lambda\times U^{X}
is a bifurcation point for the equation
(
4.27
); in particular
(\lambda^{\ast},0)\in\Lambda\times U
is a bifurcation point for the equation

D\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}(u)=0,\quad(\lambda,u)\in\Lambda\times U.

(4.28)

Proof.

Though the proof is almost repeat of that of Theorem
4.2

we give it for completeness.
Let
H^{+}_{\lambda}
,
H^{-}_{\lambda}
and
H^{0}_{\lambda}
be the positive definite, negative definite and zero spaces of

{B}_{\lambda}(0)
. Denote by
P^{0}_{\lambda}
and
P^{\pm}_{\lambda}
the orthogonal projections onto

H^{0}_{\lambda}
and
H^{\pm}_{\lambda}=H^{+}_{\lambda}\oplus H^{-}_{\lambda}
,
and by
X^{\star}_{\lambda}=X\cap H^{\star}_{\lambda}
for
\star=+,-
, and by
X^{\pm}_{\lambda}=P^{\pm}_{\lambda}(X)
.
By Theorem
A.3
there exists
\delta>0
with
[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\subset\Lambda
,

\epsilon>0
and a (unique)
C^{1}
map

\psi:[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\times B_{H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\to X^{\pm}_{\lambda^{\ast}}

which is
C^{1}
in the second variable and
satisfies
\psi(\lambda,0)=0\;\forall\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]
and

P^{\pm}_{\lambda^{\ast}}A_{\lambda}(z+\psi(\lambda,z))=0\quad\forall(\lambda,z)\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\times B_{H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon),

an open neighborhood
W
of
0
in
H
and an origin-preserving homeomorphism

\displaystyle\hskip-22.76219pt[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\times B_{H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\times\left(B_{H^{+}_{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)+B_{H^{-}_{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\right)\to[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\times W,

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt({\lambda},z,u^{+}+u^{-})\mapsto({\lambda},\Phi_{{\lambda}}(z,u^{+}+u^{-})),

such that for each
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}\circ\Phi_{\lambda}(z,u^{+}+u^{-})=\|u^{+}\|^{2}-\|u^{-}\|^{2}+\mathcal{L}_{{\lambda}}(z+\psi({\lambda},z))

(4.29)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\forall(z,u^{+}+u^{-})\in B_{H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\times\left(B_{H^{+}_{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)+B_{H^{-}_{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\right).

(Clearly, each
\Phi_{\lambda}
is an origin-preserving homeomorphism.)
Moreover, the functional

\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}^{\circ}:B_{H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\to\mathbb{R},\;z\mapsto\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}(z+\psi({\lambda},z))

(4.30)

is of class
C^{2}
,
and for each
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]

the map
z\mapsto z+\psi({\lambda},z))
induces an one-to-one correspondence
between the critical points of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}^{\circ}
near
0\in H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}

and solutions of
A_{\lambda}(u)=0
near
0\in U^{X}
.
Hence the problem is reduced to finding
the critical points of
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}

near
0\in H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
for
\lambda
near
\lambda^{\ast}
.

By (f), we can shrink
\delta>0
so that for each
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}
,

0\in H
is a nondegenerate critical point
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
and the Morse indexes of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}

at
0\in H
take constant values in
[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast})
and
(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]
, respectively.
Assume that (
4
) is satisfied.
Then Theorem
A.2
with
\lambda=0
leads to
(
4
). From these and Corollary
A.5
we deduce
(
4
) and therefore the expected conclusions as in the proof of Theorem
4.2
.
Another case can be proved similarity.
∎

As showed below Theorem
4.2
,
Theorem
4.6
is a strengthened version of Theorem
3.6

under the stronger assumptions on the Morse indexes of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
at
0\in H
.
Consequently, for bifurcations of eigenvalues of nonlinear problems
we obtain the following two corollaries, which strengthen Corollary
3.8

and correspond to Corollaries
4.3
,
4.4
, respectively.

Corollary 4.7
.

Under Hypothesis
1.6
,
suppose that the eigenvalue
\lambda^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
is isolated and
that
B(0)
is either semi-positive or semi-negative.
Then the conclusions of Theorem
4.6
hold if
(
4.27
) and (
4.28
) are, respectively, replaced by the following two equations

\displaystyle A(u)=\lambda\widehat{A}(u),\quad(\lambda,u)\in\mathbb{R}\times U^{X},

(4.31)

\displaystyle D\mathcal{L}(u)=\lambda D\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(u),\quad(\lambda,u)\in\mathbb{R}\times U.

(4.32)

Indeed, by the proof of Corollary
3.3
it is easily seen that
for sufficiently small
\delta>0
the family

\{\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}:=\mathcal{L}-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\,|\,\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta)\}

satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
4.6
.
(Of course, as in the proof Corollary
4.3
, we need to replace

\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)
and
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
by
B(0)
and
\widehat{B}(0)
, respectively.)

Similarly, we may modify the proof of Corollary
4.4
to get:

Corollary 4.8
.

Under Hypothesis
1.6
,
suppose that the following two conditions are satisfied:

(a)

B(0)
is invertible
and
\lambda^{\ast}
is an eigenvalue of
B(0)u-\lambda\widehat{B}(0)u=0
.

(b)

B(0)\widehat{B}(0)=\widehat{B}(0)B(0)
, and
B(0)

is either positive or negative on
H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}={\rm Ker}(B(0)-\lambda^{\ast}\widehat{B}(0))
.

Then the conclusions of Corollary
4.7
are true.
Moreover, if
B(0)
is positive definite, the condition (b) is unnecessary.

Since
B(0)
is invertible, the case (II) of Hypothesis
1.5
cannot occur.
The “Moreover” part strengthens Theorem
3.10
.

In applications to Lagrange systems using
Theorem
4.6
and Corollaries
4.7
,
4.8

produce stronger results than using Theorem
4.2
and Corollaries
4.3
,
4.4

(see
[
47
]
). Moreover, we may give parameterized versions of splitting lemmas for the Finsler energy functional
in
[
44
]
as Theorem
A.3
and then obtain similar results to
Theorem
4.6
and Corollaries
4.7
,
4.8
for geodesics on Finsler manifolds.

Now we give a multi-parameter bifurcation result,
which is not only a converse of Corollary
3.4
in stronger conditions, but also
a generalizations of Corollaries
4.7
,
4.8
in some sense.

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
\mathcal{L}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfy
Hypothesis
1.3
, except (C) and (D1), and let

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{j}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
,
j=1,\cdots,n
, satisfy
Hypothesis
1.4
.
Suppose also that the following conditions hold:

(A)

\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}
is an isolated eigenvalue of (
3.11
), (writting
H(\vec{\lambda}^{\ast})

the solution space of (
3.11
) with
\vec{\lambda}=\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}
),
and for each point
\vec{\lambda}
near
0\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, it holds that

\{u\in H\,|\,\mathfrak{B}_{\vec{\lambda}}u\in X\}\cup\{u\in H\,|\,\mathfrak{B}_{\vec{\lambda}}u=\mu u,\;\mu\leq 0\}\subset X

(4.33)

where
\mathfrak{B}_{\vec{\lambda}}:=B(0)-\sum^{n}_{j=1}\lambda_{j}\widehat{B}_{j}(0)
.

(B)

Either
H(\vec{\lambda}^{\ast})
has odd dimension, or
\mathfrak{B}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}\widehat{B}_{j}(0)=\widehat{B}_{j}(0)\mathfrak{B}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}

for
j=1,\cdots,\\
n
, and there exists
\vec{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{{0}\}
such that the symmetric bilinear form

H(\vec{\lambda}^{\ast})\times H(\vec{\lambda}^{\ast})\ni(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto\mathscr{Q}_{\vec{\lambda}}(z_{1},z_{2}):=\sum^{n}_{j=1}(\lambda_{j}-\lambda^{\ast}_{j})(\widehat{B}_{j}(0)z_{1},z_{2})_{H}

(4.34)

has different Morse indexes and coindexes.

(If
\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}=0
, we only need that
each
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{j}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
is as in “Moreover” part of Corollary
3.3
.)
Then
(\vec{\lambda}^{\ast},0)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times U^{X}
is a bifurcation point of
(
3.16
). Moreover, suppose that (B) is replaced by

(B’)

\mathfrak{B}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}\widehat{B}_{j}(0)=\widehat{B}_{j}(0)\mathfrak{B}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}

for
j=1,\cdots,n
, and there exists
\vec{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{{0}\}
such that
the form
\mathscr{Q}_{\vec{\mu}}
on
H(\vec{\lambda}^{\ast})
is either positive definite or negative one.

Then one of the following alternatives occurs:

(i)

(\vec{\lambda}^{\ast},0)
is not an isolated solution of (
3.16
) in

\{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}\}\times U^{X}
.

(ii)

For every
t
near
0\in\mathbb{R}
there is a nontrivial solution
u_{t}
of (
3.16
)
with
\vec{\lambda}=t(\vec{\mu}-\vec{\lambda}^{\ast})+\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}

converging to
0
in
X
as
t\to 0
.

(iii)

There is an one-sided neighborhood
\mathfrak{T}
of
0\in\mathbb{R}
such that
for any
t\in\mathfrak{T}\setminus\{0\}
,
(
3.16
) with
\vec{\lambda}=t(\vec{\mu}-\vec{\lambda}^{\ast})+\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}
has at least two nontrivial solutions converging to

0
in
X
as
t\to 0
.

Proof.

Step 1
(
Prove the first claim
).
Let
H^{+}_{\vec{\lambda}}
,
H^{-}_{\vec{\lambda}}
and
H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}}
be the positive definite, negative definite and zero spaces of

\mathfrak{B}_{\vec{\lambda}}=B(0)-\sum^{n}_{j=1}\lambda_{j}\widehat{B}_{j}(0)
.
Denote by
P^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}}
and
P^{\pm}_{\vec{\lambda}}
the orthogonal projections onto

H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}}
and
H^{\pm}_{\vec{\lambda}}=H^{+}_{\vec{\lambda}}\oplus H^{-}_{\vec{\lambda}}
,
and by
X^{\star}_{\vec{\lambda}}=X\cap H^{\star}_{\vec{\lambda}}
for
\star=+,-
, and by
X^{\pm}_{\vec{\lambda}}=P^{\pm}_{\vec{\lambda}}(X)
.
By the assumption, for each
\vec{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
near
\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}
spaces

H^{-}_{\vec{\lambda}}
and
H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}}
have finite dimensions and are contained in
X
, so

H^{-}_{\vec{\lambda}}=X^{-}_{\vec{\lambda}}
and
H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}}=X^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}}
.
Because the corresponding operator
A
with
\mathcal{L}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})

is not assumed to be
C^{1}
, Theorem
A.3
cannot be used.
But modifying its proof (as in the proof of
[
41
, Theorem 2.1]
) there exist
\delta>0
and

\epsilon>0
, a (unique)
C^{1-0}
map

\displaystyle\psi:\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}+[-\delta,\delta]^{n}\times B_{H^{0}}(0,\epsilon)\to X^{\pm}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}

which is strictly Fréchet differentiable in the second variable at
0\in H^{0}
and satisfies
\psi(\vec{\lambda},0)=0\;\forall\vec{\lambda}\in\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}+[-\delta,\delta]^{n}
, and
an open neighborhood
W
of
0
in
H
and an origin-preserving homeomorphism

\displaystyle\hskip-10.0pt(\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}+[-\delta,\delta]^{n})\times B_{H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\times\left(B_{H^{+}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)+B_{H^{-}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\right)\to(\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}+[-\delta,\delta]^{n})\times W,

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt({\vec{\lambda}},z,u^{+}+u^{-})\mapsto({\vec{\lambda}},\Phi_{\vec{\lambda}}(z,u^{+}+u^{-})),

such that for each
\vec{\lambda}\in\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}+[-\delta,\delta]^{n}
,
the functional
\mathcal{L}_{\vec{\lambda}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\vec{\lambda}}\circ\Phi_{\vec{\lambda}}(z,u^{+}+u^{-})=\|u^{+}\|^{2}-\|u^{-}\|^{2}+\mathcal{L}_{\vec{\lambda}}(z+\psi(\vec{\lambda},z))

(4.35)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\forall(z,u^{+}+u^{-})\in B_{H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\times\left(B_{H^{+}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)+B_{H^{-}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\right).

Moreover, the functional

\mathcal{L}_{\vec{\lambda}}^{\circ}:B_{H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\to\mathbb{R}
given by

\mathcal{L}_{\vec{\lambda}}^{\circ}(z)=\mathcal{L}_{\vec{\lambda}}(z+\psi({\vec{\lambda}},z))

is of class
C^{2-0}
, has first-order differential at
z_{0}\in B_{H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)
are given by

\displaystyle d\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\vec{\lambda}}(z_{0})[z]=\bigl(A(z_{0}+\psi(\vec{\lambda},z_{0}))-\sum^{n}_{j=1}\lambda_{j}\widehat{A}_{j}(z_{0}+\psi(\vec{\lambda},z_{0})),z\bigr)_{H}\quad\forall z\in H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}},

(4.36)

and
d\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\vec{\lambda}}
has strict Fréchet derivative at
0\in H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}
,

\displaystyle d^{2}\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\vec{\lambda}}(0)[z,z^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\left(P^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}\bigr[\mathfrak{B}_{\vec{\lambda}}-\mathfrak{B}_{\vec{\lambda}}(P^{\pm}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}\mathfrak{B}_{\vec{\lambda}}|_{X^{\pm}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}})^{-1}(P^{\pm}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}\mathfrak{B}_{\vec{\lambda}})\bigr]z,z^{\prime}\right)_{H},\;\forall z,z^{\prime}\in H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}.

(4.37)

Clearly, (
4
) implies
d^{2}\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}(0)=0
.
As the arguments above
[
45
, Claim 2.17]
we have

d^{2}\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\vec{\lambda}}(0)[z_{1},z_{2}]=\sum^{n}_{j=1}(\lambda_{j}-\lambda^{\ast}_{j})(\widehat{B}_{j}(0)(z_{1}+D_{z}\psi(\vec{\lambda},0)[z_{1}]),z_{2})_{H},\quad\forall z_{1},z_{2}\in H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}.

(4.38)

(The negative sign in
[
45
, (2.59)]
should be removed.)

As before, using the above results the
bifurcation problem (
3.16
) is reduced to the following one

\nabla\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\vec{\lambda}}(u)=0,\;u\in B_{H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon),\;\vec{\lambda}\in\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}+[-\delta,\delta]^{n}.

(4.39)

By (
A
) we may shrink
\delta>0
so that
for each
\vec{\lambda}\in\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}+([-\delta,\delta]^{n}\setminus\{0\})
,

\mathcal{L}\in C^{1}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfy Hypothesis
1.3
and has

0\in H
as a nondegenerate critical point.
Using Theorem
A.2
with
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}=0

(or
[
41
, Remark 2.2]
) and a consequence of (
4.35
) as in
[
45
, Theorem 2.18]

we obtain that for any Abel group
{\bf K}
and for each
\vec{\lambda}\in\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}+([-\delta,\delta]^{n}\setminus\{0\})
,

C_{q}(\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\vec{\lambda}},0;{\bf K})=\delta_{(q+{\mu_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}})\mu_{\vec{\lambda}}}{\bf K},\quad\forall q\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\},

(4.40)

where
\mu_{\vec{\lambda}}
is the Morse index of
\mathcal{L}_{\vec{\lambda}}
at
0\in H
.

By a contradiction suppose now that
(\vec{\lambda}^{\ast},0)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times U^{X}
is not a bifurcation point of
(
3.16
). Then
(\vec{\lambda}^{\ast},0)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}
is not a bifurcation point of
(
4.39
). Hence we may shrink
\epsilon>0
and
\delta>0
such that

\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\vec{\lambda}}
has a unique critical point
0
in
B_{H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)

for each
\vec{\lambda}\in\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}+[-\delta,\delta]^{n}
. As before it follows from the stability of critical groups
(cf.Theorem
2.2
) that

C_{\ast}(\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\vec{\lambda}},0;{\bf K})=C_{\ast}(\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}},0;{\bf K}),\quad\forall\vec{\lambda}\in\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}+[-\delta,\delta]^{n}.

(4.41)

This and (
4.40
) imply that the Morse index of
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\vec{\lambda}}
at
0

is constant with respect to
\vec{\lambda}\in\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}+([-\delta,\delta]^{n}\setminus\{{0}\})
.
On the other hand, for every
\vec{\lambda}\in\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}+([-\delta,\delta]^{n}\setminus\{{0}\})
,
(
4.38
) implies

d^{2}\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{2\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}-\vec{\lambda}}(0)=-d^{2}\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\vec{\lambda}}(0)

as the nondegenerate quadratic forms on
H^{0}_{{\lambda}^{\ast}}
. Note that
H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}=H(\vec{\lambda}^{\ast})
.
So if
\dim H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}
is odd,
d^{2}\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\vec{\lambda}}(0)
and
d^{2}\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{2\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}-\vec{\lambda}}(0)

must have different Morse indexes. This contradicts (
4.41
).

In other situation, since for each
j=1,\cdots,n
,
\mathfrak{B}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}\widehat{B}_{j}(0)=\widehat{B}_{j}(0)\mathfrak{B}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}

implies that
P^{\star}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}\widehat{B}_{j}(0)=\widehat{B}_{j}(0)P^{\star}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}
,

\star=0,+,-
, and
D_{z}\psi(\vec{\lambda},0)[z_{1}]\in H^{\pm}_{\vec{\lambda}}
for all
z_{1}\in H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}}
,
we deduce

\displaystyle(D_{z}\psi(\vec{\lambda},0)[z_{1}],\widehat{B}_{j}(0)z_{2})_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(D_{z}\psi(\vec{\lambda},0)[z_{1}],\widehat{B}_{j}(0)P^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}z_{2})_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(D_{z}\psi(\vec{\lambda},0)[z_{1}],P^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}\widehat{B}_{j}(0)z_{2})_{H}=0,\quad\forall z_{1},z_{2}\in H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}.

Thus (
4.38
) becomes

d^{2}\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\vec{\lambda}}(0)[z_{1},z_{2}]=\sum^{n}_{j=1}(\lambda_{j}-\lambda^{\ast}_{j})(\widehat{B}_{j}(0)z_{1},z_{2})_{H}=\mathscr{Q}_{\vec{\lambda}}(z_{1},z_{2}),\quad\forall z_{1},z_{2}\in H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}.

Then the same reasoning leads to a contradiction. The first claim is proved.

Step 2
(
Prove the second claim
).
By (
4.34
),
\mathscr{Q}_{t(\vec{\mu}-\vec{\lambda}^{\ast})+\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}=t\mathscr{Q}_{\vec{\mu}}

for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
. By replacing
\vec{\mu}
by
2\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}-\vec{\mu}
(if necessary) we may assume that
the form
d^{2}\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\vec{\mu}}(0)=\mathscr{Q}_{\vec{\mu}}
is positive definite.
Then when
t>0
with small
|t|
the forms

d^{2}\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{t(\vec{\mu}-\vec{\lambda}^{\ast})+\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}

are positive definite and so
0\in H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}
is a strict local
minimizer of
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{t(\vec{\mu}-\vec{\lambda}^{\ast})+\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}
;
when
t<0
with small
|t|
the forms

d^{2}\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{t(\vec{\mu}-\vec{\lambda}^{\ast})+\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}

are negative definite and so
0\in H^{0}_{\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}
is a strict local
maximizer of
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{t(\vec{\mu}-\vec{\lambda}^{\ast})+\vec{\lambda}^{\ast}}
.
As before the desired claims follows from Theorem
4.1
.
∎

Some results in next Sections
5
,
6
can also be generalized
to such more general multi-parameter forms.

Finally, in order to understand advantages and disadvantages of our above methods
let us state results which can be obtained with the parameterized version of the classical splitting lemma
for
C^{2}
functionals stated in Remark
A.8
and the classical Morse-Palais lemma for
C^{2}
functionals.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
U
be an open neighborhood of
0
in a real Hilbert space
H
,

\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}
an open interval containing
\lambda^{\ast}
,
and let
\{\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}\in C^{2}(U,\mathbb{R})\,|\,\lambda\in\Lambda\}
be a family of functionals
such that
\Lambda\times U\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto\nabla\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}(u)\in H
is continuous.
Suppose that the following conditions are satisfied:

(a)

For each
\lambda\in\Lambda
,
0
is a critical point of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
with
finite Morse index
\mu_{\lambda}
and nullity
\nu_{\lambda}
.

(b)

If
\nu_{\lambda^{\ast}}\neq 0
,
\nu_{\lambda}=0
for any
\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}
,
and
\mu_{\lambda}
take, respectively, values
\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}
and
\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}+\nu_{\lambda^{\ast}}

as
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
varies in
two deleted half neighborhoods of
\lambda^{\ast}
.

Then one of the following alternatives occurs:

(i)

(\lambda^{\ast},0)
is not an isolated solution in
\{\lambda^{\ast}\}\times U
of the equation

D\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}(u)=0,\quad(\lambda,u)\in\Lambda\times U.

(4.42)

(ii)

For every
\lambda\in\Lambda
near
\lambda^{\ast}
there is a nontrivial solution
u_{\lambda}
of (
4.42
) in
U

converging to
0
as
\lambda\to\lambda^{\ast}
.

(iii)

There is an one-sided neighborhood
\Lambda^{\ast}
of
\lambda^{\ast}
such that
for any
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\ast}\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}
, (
4.42
) has at least two nontrivial solutions in
U

converging to
0
as
\lambda\to\lambda^{\ast}
.

In particular,
(\lambda^{\ast},0)\in\Lambda\times U
is a bifurcation point for the equation
(
4.42
).

In fact, we only need to make suitable replacements in the proof of Theorem
4.6
.
For example, we replace symbols “
X
” by “
H
”, “
A_{\lambda}
” by “
\nabla\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
”, and
“
B_{\lambda}
” by “
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}
”, and phrases
“By Theorem
A.3
” by “the parameterized version of the classical splitting lemma for
C^{2}
functionals
stated in Remark
A.8
”, and “Theorem
A.2
with
\lambda=0
” by
“the classical Morse-Palais lemma for
C^{2}
functionals”,
“Corollary
A.5
” by “a corresponding result with Corollary
A.5
”.

Theorem 4.11
.

The conclusions of Theorem
4.10
hold true if (a) and (b) are replaced by the following conditions:

(a’)

For each
\lambda\in\Lambda
,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(0)=0
,
0<\nu_{\lambda^{\ast}}:=\dim{\rm Ker}(\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0))<\infty
.

(b’)

For some small
\delta>0
,
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
has an isolated local minimum (maximum) at zero for every

\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]
and an isolated local maximum (minimum) at
zero for every
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast})
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Theorem
4.6
, the original bifurcation problem is reduced to that of

\displaystyle\nabla\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{{\lambda}}(u)=0,\;u\in B_{H^{0}_{{\lambda}^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon),\;\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}.

Here each
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}:B_{H}(0,\epsilon)\cap H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}\to\mathbb{R}
is
a
C^{2}
functional given by (
4.30
), and
has differential at
z\in B_{H}(0,\epsilon)\cap H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
given by

D\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}^{\circ}(z)[\zeta]=(\nabla\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}(z+\psi({\lambda},z)),\zeta)_{H}

for
\zeta\in H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
. Clearly, the assumption (b’) implies that
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}^{\circ}

has an isolated local minimum (maximum) at zero for every

\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]
and an isolated local maximum (minimum) at
zero for every
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast})
.
Then Theorem
4.1
leads to the desired claims.
∎

Comparing Theorem
4.11
with
[
19
, Theorem 4.2]
we have no the (PS) condition,
but require higher smoothness of functionals.

Corresponding to Corollaries
4.3
,
4.4
(or
Corollaries
4.7
,
4.8
) we have

Corollary 4.12
.

Let
U
be an open neighborhood of
0
in a real Hilbert space
H
,
and let
\mathcal{L},\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\in C^{2}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfy

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(0)=0
and
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(0)=0
. Suppose that

\lambda^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
is an eigenvalue of

\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)u=\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)u
in
H
of finite multiplicity.
Then the conclusions of Theorem
4.10
hold true with
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}=\mathcal{L}-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}

if one of the following conditions is satisfied:

(a)

The eigenvalue
\lambda^{\ast}
is isolated,
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
has finite Morse index at
0
for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
near
\lambda^{\ast}
, and
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is either semi-positive or semi-negative.

(b)

For some small
\delta>0
,
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
has an isolated local minimum (maximum) at zero for every

\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]
and an isolated local maximum (minimum) at
zero for every
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast})
.

When
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(u)=\frac{1}{2}(u,u)_{H}
, the case (a) shows that
the Rabinowitz bifurcation theorem stated at the beginning of this section
can be obtained under the additional condition that
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
has finite Morse index at
0
for
each
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
near
\lambda^{\ast}
. The latter condition cannot imply that

\mathcal{L}
has finite Morse index at
0
if
\lambda^{\ast}\neq 0
.

Proof of Corollary
4.12
.

The case that (b) holds follows from Theorem
4.11
directly.

When the case (a) holds, since
\lambda^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
is an isolated eigenvalue of

\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)u=\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)u
in
H
of finite multiplicity,
for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
near
\lambda^{\ast}
,

\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is a bounded linear Fredholm operator.
(Hence
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
has finite nullity
\nu_{\lambda}
at
0
.)
Repeating the proof of Corollary
4.3

we can get (
4.24
) and hence the condition (b) of Theorem
4.10
is satisfied.
∎

Corollary 4.13
.

Let
U
be an open neighborhood of
0
in a real Hilbert space
H
,
and let
\mathcal{L},\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\in C^{2}(U,\mathbb{R})
satisfy

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(0)=0
and
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(0)=0
.
Suppose that the following two conditions are satisfied:

(a)

\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is invertible,
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is compact
and
\lambda^{\ast}
is an eigenvalue of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)u=\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)u
in
H
.

(b)

For each
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
near
\lambda^{\ast}
,
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}=\mathcal{L}-\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{L}}
has finite Morse index at
0\in H
,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)=\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)
,
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is either positive or negative on
H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}:={\rm Ker}(\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(0)-\lambda^{\ast}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0))
.

Then the conclusions of Theorem
4.10
hold true.
Moreover, if
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(0)
is positive definite, the condition (b) is unnecessary.

Clearly, the assumption (a) implies that
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
has finite nullity at
0
for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.
Repeating the proof of Corollary
4.3
gives the desired claim.
The “Moreover” part strengthens Theorem
3.13
.

Compare Corollaries
4.12
,
4.13
with

[
58
,
59
]
by Rabinowitz.
Similarly, we can also write a corresponding result with Theorem
4.9
.

5
Bifurcation for equivariant problems

There are abundant studies of bifurcations for equivariant maps,
see
[
2
,
7
,
26
,
27
,
28
,
65
]
and the references therein.
In this section we shall generalize main results of
[
26
,
27
]

in the setting of Section
4
.

Let
G
be a topological group. A (left)
action
of it over a Banach space
X
is a continuous
map
G\times X\to X,\;(g,x)\mapsto gx
satisfying the usual rules:
(g_{1}g_{2})x=g_{1}(g_{2}x)
and
ex=x
for all
x\in X

and
g_{1},g_{2}\in G
, where
e\in G
denotes the unite element. This action is called
linear
(resp.
isometric
) if for each
g\in G
,
X\ni x\mapsto gx\in X
is linear (and so a bounded linear operator
from
X
to itself) (resp.
\|gx\|=\|x\|
for all
x\in X
). When
X
is a Hilbert space a linear isometric action
is also called an orthogonal action (since a linear isometry must be an orthogonal linear transformation).

5.1
Bifurcation theorems of Fadell–Rabinowitz type

We first study finite dimensional situations.
The following is a refinement of
[
11
, Theorem 5.1]
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
4.1
,
let
X
be equipped with a linear isometric action of a compact Lie group
G
so that
each
f_{\lambda}
is invariant under the
G
-action. Suppose also that
the local minimums (maximums) in assumption c) of Theorem
4.1
are strict,
and that
u=0
is an isolated critical point of
f_{\lambda^{\ast}}
.
Then when the Lie group
G
is equal to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{{\rm id},-{\rm id}\}
(resp.
S^{1}

without fixed points except
0
,
which implies
\dim X
to be an even more than one),
there exist left and right neighborhoods
\Lambda^{-}
and
\Lambda^{+}
of
\lambda^{\ast}
in
\mathbb{R}

and integers
n^{+},n^{-}\geq 0
, such that
n^{+}+n^{-}\geq\dim X
(resp.
\frac{1}{2}\dim X
),
and that for
\lambda\in\Lambda^{-}\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}
(resp.
\lambda\in\Lambda^{+}\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}
)

f_{\lambda}
has at least
n^{-}
(resp.
n^{+}
) distinct critical

G
-orbits different from
0
, which converge to

0
as
\lambda\to\lambda^{\ast}
.

This is essentially contained in the proofs of
[
59
,
26
,
27
]
.
For completeness we will give most of its proof detail.

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

By assumptions we have either

\displaystyle 0\in X\;\hbox{is a strict local}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\hbox{minimizer of}\;f_{\lambda},&\quad\forall\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}),\\
\hbox{maximizer of}\;f_{\lambda},&\quad\forall\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\end{array}\right.

or

\displaystyle 0\in X\;\hbox{is a strict local}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\hbox{maximizer of}\;f_{\lambda},&\quad\forall\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}),\\
\hbox{minimizer of}\;f_{\lambda},&\quad\forall\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta].\end{array}\right.

By the assumption a) of Theorem
4.1
, replacing

f_{\lambda}
by
f_{\lambda}-f_{\lambda}(0)
if necessary, we now and henceforth assume
f_{\lambda}(0)=0\;\forall\lambda
.
Since
u=0
is an isolated critical point of
f_{\lambda^{\ast}}
, by
[
32
, page 136]
there exist mutually disjoint:

Case 1
.
0\in X
is a local minimizer of
f_{\lambda^{\ast}}
;

Case 2
.
0\in X
is a proper local maximizer of
f_{\lambda^{\ast}}
, i.e., it is a local maximizer of
f_{\lambda^{\ast}}

and
0
belongs to the closure of
\{f_{\lambda^{\ast}}<0\}
;

Case 3
.
0\in X
is a saddle point of
f_{\lambda^{\ast}}
, i.e.,
f_{\lambda^{\ast}}
takes both positive and negative values in every neighborhood of
0
.

Though our proof ideas are following
[
59
, Theorem 2.2]
and
[
26
,
27
, Theorem 1.2]
,
some technical improvements are necessary since each
f_{\lambda}
is only of class
C^{1}
.

By the assumption b) of Theorem
4.1
, the function
(\lambda,z)\mapsto Df_{\lambda}(z)
is continuous on

[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\times B_{X}(0,\epsilon)
.
It follows that

\displaystyle R_{\delta,\epsilon}:=\{(\lambda,z)\in(\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta)\times B_{X}(0,\epsilon)\,|\,z\in B_{X}(0,\epsilon)\setminus K(f_{\lambda})\}

is an open subset in
[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\times B_{X}(0,\epsilon)
, where

K(f_{\lambda})
denotes the critical set of
f_{\lambda}
.

Though the local flow
of
\nabla f_{\lambda}
must not exist,
fortunately, by suitably modifying the standard constructions in
[
54
, §4]
(see also

[
52
, Propsitions 5.29, 5.57]
) we have:

Lemma 5.2
.

There exists a smooth map
R_{\delta,\epsilon}\to X,\;(\lambda,z)\mapsto\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}(z)
,
such that for each
\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta)
the map

\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}:B_{X}(0,\epsilon)\setminus K(f_{\lambda})\to X

is
G
-equivariant and satisfies

\|\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}(z)\|\leq 2\|Df_{\lambda}(z)\|\quad\hbox{and}\quad\langle Df_{\lambda}(z),\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}(z)\rangle\geq\|Df_{\lambda}(z)\|^{2}

for all
z\in B_{X}(0,\epsilon)\setminus K(f_{\lambda})
, i.e., it is pseudo-gradient vector field of

f_{\lambda}
in the sense of
[
54
]
.

Notice that this is actually true for any compact Lie group
G
.
There exist some variants and generalizations for the notion of the pseudo-gradient vector field in
[
54
]
,
which are more easily constructed and are still effective in applications. For example,
according to
[
30
]

\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
is also called a pseudo-gradient vector field of

f_{\lambda}
even if two inequalities in Lemma
5.2
are replaced by

\|\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}(z)\|\leq\alpha\|Df_{\lambda}(z)\|\quad\hbox{and}\quad\langle Df_{\lambda}(z),\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}(z)\rangle\geq\beta\|Df_{\lambda}(z)\|^{2},

where
\alpha
and
\beta
are two positive constants.

Proof of Lemma
5.2
.

Since
(\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta)\times B_{X}(0,\epsilon)\ni(\lambda,z)\mapsto Df_{\lambda}(z)\in X^{\ast}
is continuous, for any given

(\lambda,z)\in R_{\delta,\epsilon}
we have an open neighborhood

O_{(z,\lambda)}
of
z
in
B_{X}(0,\epsilon)\setminus K(f_{\lambda})
,
a positive number
r_{(z,\lambda)}

with
(\lambda-r_{(z,\lambda)},\lambda+r_{(z,\lambda)})\subset(\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta)
and
a vector
v_{(z,\lambda)}\in X
such that
for all
(z^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})\in O_{(z,\lambda)}\times(\lambda-r_{(z,\lambda)},\lambda+r_{(z,\lambda)})
,

\|v_{(z,\lambda)}\|<2\|Df_{\lambda^{\prime}}(z^{\prime})\|\quad\hbox{and}\quad\langle Df_{\lambda^{\prime}}(z^{\prime}),v_{(z,\lambda)}\rangle>\|Df_{\lambda^{\prime}}(z^{\prime})\|^{2}.

Now all above
O_{(z,\lambda)}\times(\lambda-r_{(z,\lambda)},\lambda+r_{(z,\lambda)})

form an open cover
\mathscr{C}
of
R_{\delta,\epsilon}
,
and the latter admits a
C^{\infty}
-unit decomposition
\{\eta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\Xi}

subordinate to a locally finite
refinement
\{W_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\Xi}
of
\mathscr{C}
.
Since each
W_{\alpha}
can be contained in some open subset of form

O_{(z,\lambda)}\times(\lambda-r_{(z,\lambda)},\lambda+r_{(z,\lambda)})
, we have
a vector
v_{\alpha}\in X
such that for all
(z^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})\in W_{\alpha}
,

\|v_{\alpha}\|<2\|Df_{\lambda^{\prime}}(z^{\prime})\|\quad\hbox{and}\quad\langle Df_{\lambda^{\prime}}(z^{\prime}),v_{\alpha}\rangle>\|Df_{\lambda^{\prime}}(z^{\prime})\|^{2}.

Set
\chi(\lambda,z)=\sum_{\alpha\in\Xi}\eta_{\alpha}(\lambda,z)v_{\alpha}
. Then
\chi
is a smooth map
from
R_{\delta,\epsilon}
to
X
, and satisfies

\|\chi(z,\lambda)\|<2\|Df_{\lambda}(z)\|\quad\hbox{and}\quad\langle Df_{\lambda}(z),\chi(z,\lambda)\rangle>\|Df_{\lambda}(z)\|^{2}

for all
(z,\lambda)\in R_{\delta,\epsilon}
. Let
d\mu
denote the right invariant Haar measure on
G
. Define

R_{\delta,\epsilon}\ni(z,\lambda)\mapsto\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}(z)=\int_{G}g^{-1}\chi(gz,\lambda)d\mu\in X.

It is easily checked that
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
satisfies requirements (see the proof
[
52
, Propsition 5.57]
).
∎

Consider the ordinary differential equation

\frac{d\varphi}{ds}=-\mathscr{V}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(\varphi),\quad\varphi(0,z)=z

(5.7)

in
B_{X}(0,\epsilon)
. For each
z\in B_{X}(0,\epsilon)
this equation possesses a unique solution
\varphi(t,z)
whose
the maximal existence interval is
(\omega_{-}(z),\omega_{+}(z))
with

-\infty\leq\omega_{-}(z)<0<\omega_{+}(z)\leq+\infty
.
Note that
0\in X
is an isolated critical point of
f_{\lambda^{\ast}}
.
There exists a small neighborhood
\mathcal{B}
of
0
in
B_{X}(0,\epsilon)
such that

u=0
is a unique critical point of
f_{\lambda^{\ast}}
siting in it. Put

\displaystyle S^{+}=\{z\in\mathcal{B}\,|\,\omega_{+}(z)=+\infty\;\&\;\varphi(t,z)\in\mathcal{B},\;\forall t>0\},

\displaystyle S^{-}=\{z\in\mathcal{B}\,|\,\omega_{-}(z)=-\infty\;\&\;\varphi(t,z)\in\mathcal{B},\;\forall t<0\}.

Then
S^{+}\neq\emptyset
(resp.
S^{-}\neq\emptyset
) if there are points near
0\in\mathcal{B}
where
f_{\lambda^{\ast}}

is positive (resp. negative).
Repeating the constructions in
[
59
, Lemmas 1.15,1.16]
and
[
27
, Lemma 8.28]

(and replacing
\psi
therein by
\varphi
) we can obtain:

Lemma 5.3
.

There is a constant
\gamma>0
and a
G
-invariant open neighborhood
\mathcal{Q}
of
0
in
X

with compact closure
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
contained in
\mathcal{B}
such that

(i)

if
z\in\mathcal{Q}
then

|f_{\lambda^{\ast}}(z)|<\gamma
;

(ii)

if
(z,t)\in\mathcal{Q}\times\mathbb{R}
satisfies

|f_{\lambda^{\ast}}(\varphi(t,z))|<\gamma
, then
\varphi(t,z)\in\mathcal{Q}
;

(iii)

if
z\in\partial\mathcal{Q}
, then either

|f_{\lambda^{\ast}}(z)|=\gamma
or

\varphi(t,z)\in\partial\mathcal{Q}
for all
t
satisfying

|f_{\lambda^{\ast}}(\varphi(t,z))|\leq\gamma
.

Actually, for sufficiently small
\rho>0
with
\bar{B}_{X}(0,\rho)\subset\mathcal{B}
,

\mathcal{Q}
may be chosen as

\{z\in B_{X}(0,\epsilon)\,|\,-\gamma<f_{\lambda^{\ast}}(z)<\gamma\}\cap\{\varphi(t,z)\,|\,z\in B_{X}(0,\rho),\;\omega_{-}(z)<t<\omega_{+}(z)\}.

Clearly, when
0\in X
is a strict local maximizer (resp. minimizer) of
f_{\lambda^{\ast}}
,

\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\cap(f_{\lambda^{\ast}})^{-1}(\gamma)
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\cap(f_{\lambda^{\ast}})^{-1}(-\gamma)
)
may be empty. Moreover, for any
z\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
, if
\varphi(t,z)\notin\overline{\mathcal{Q}}

for some
t=t(z)>0
, then
f_{\lambda^{\ast}}(\varphi(t,z))<-\gamma
.

In the following, if
G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}==\{{\rm id},-{\rm id}\}
let
i_{G}
denote the genus in
[
59
]
,
and if
G=S^{1}
without fixed points except
0

let
i_{G}
denote the index
{\rm Index}^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
in
[
27
, §7]
.
Let
T^{\ast}=S^{\ast}\cap\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
,
\ast=+,-
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Both
T^{+}
and
T^{-}
are
G
-invariant compact subsets of
\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
, and also satisfy

(i)

\min\{f_{\lambda}(z)\,|\,z\in T^{+}\}>0
and

\max\{f_{\lambda}(z)\,|\,z\in T^{-}\}<0
;

(ii)

i_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(T^{+})+i_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(T^{-})\geq\dim X
and

i_{S^{1}}(T^{+})+i_{S^{1}}(T^{-})\geq\frac{1}{2}\dim X
.

By the definitions of
T^{+}
and
T^{-}
, Lemma
5.4
(i) is apparent.
Two inequalities in Lemma
5.4
(ii) are
[
26
, Lemma 2.11]
and
[
27
, Theorem 8.30]
, respectively.

Define a piecewise-linear function
\alpha:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)

by
\alpha(t)=0
for
t\leq 1
,
\alpha(t)=-t+2
for
t\in[1,2]
, and
\alpha(t)=0
for
t\geq 2
.
For
\rho>0
let
\alpha_{\rho}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be defined by

\alpha_{\rho}(t)=\alpha(t/\rho)
.
Let
d(z,\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}})=\inf\{\|z-u\|\,|\,u\in\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\}
for
z\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
.
For each
\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta)
, define

\Xi_{\rho,\lambda}:\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\to H^{0},\;z\mapsto\alpha_{\rho}(d(z,\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}}))\mathscr{V}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(z)+(1-\alpha_{\rho}(d(z,\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}})))\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}(z),

which is
G
-equivariant and Lipschitz continuous.
Since
\inf\{\|Df_{\lambda^{\ast}}(z)\|\,|\,z\in\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\}>0
we may choose
a small
\rho>0
so that

\displaystyle\inf\{\|Df_{\lambda^{\ast}}(z)\|\,|\,z\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\;\&\;d(z,\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}}))\leq 2\rho\}>0,

(5.8)

\displaystyle\inf\{\langle Df_{\lambda^{\ast}}(z),\mathscr{V}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(z)\rangle\,|\,z\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\;\&\;d(z,\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}}))\leq 2\rho\}>0.

Moreover, the function
(\lambda,z)\mapsto Df_{\lambda}(z)
is uniform continuous on

[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]\times\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
by the assumption b) of Theorem
4.1
.
Then this and Lemma
5.2
imply that there exists a small
0<\delta_{0}<\delta
such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|Df_{\lambda^{\ast}}(z)\|\leq\|Df_{\lambda}(z)\|\leq 2\|Df_{\lambda^{\ast}}(z)\|,

(5.9)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\langle Df_{\lambda^{\ast}}(z),\mathscr{V}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(z)\rangle\leq\langle Df_{\lambda}(z),\mathscr{V}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(z)\rangle\leq 2\langle Df_{\lambda^{\ast}}(z),\mathscr{V}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(z)\rangle

(5.10)

for all
z\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
satisfying
d(z,\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}}))\leq 2\rho
and for
all
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta_{0},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta_{0}]
.
(Clearly, (
5.8
) and (
5.9
) show that any critical point of
f_{\lambda}
sitting in

\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
must be in

\{z\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,d(z,\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}}))>2\rho\}
.)
By (
5.9
) and Lemma
5.2
we deduce

\displaystyle\|\Xi_{\rho,\lambda}(z)\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\alpha_{\rho}(d(z,\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}}))\|\mathscr{V}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(z)\|+(1-\alpha_{\rho}(d(z,\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}})))\|\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}(z)\|

(5.11)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\alpha_{\rho}(d(z,\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}}))\|Df_{\lambda^{\ast}}(z)\|+2(1-\alpha_{\rho}(d(z,\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}})))\|Df_{\lambda}(z)\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\|Df_{\lambda}(z)\|,\quad\forall z\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\setminus K(f_{\lambda}),\quad\forall\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta_{0},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta_{0}].

Similarly, (
5.9
)-(
5.10
) and Lemma
5.2
produce

\displaystyle\langle Df_{\lambda}(z),\Xi_{\rho,\lambda}(z)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{\rho}(d(z,\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}}))\langle Df_{\lambda}(z),\mathscr{V}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(z)\rangle+(1-\alpha_{\rho}(d(z,\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}})))\langle Df_{\lambda}(z),\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}(z)\rangle

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{\rho}(d(z,\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}}))\|Df_{\lambda^{\ast}}(z)\|^{2}+(1-\alpha_{\rho}(d(z,\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}})))\|Df_{\lambda}(z)\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\alpha_{\rho}(d(z,\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}}))\|Df_{\lambda}(z)\|^{2}+(1-\alpha_{\rho}(d(z,\partial\overline{\mathcal{Q}})))\|Df_{\lambda}(z)\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\|Df_{\lambda}(z)\|^{2},\quad\forall z\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\setminus K(f_{\lambda}),\quad\forall\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta_{0},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta_{0}].

Hence
\Xi_{\rho,\lambda}
is a locally Lipschitz continuous
pseudo-gradient vector field of
f_{\lambda}(z)
in the sense of

[
30
]
.

Replacing
\hat{V}
in the proofs of
[
59
, Lemma 1.19]
and
[
27
, Lemma 8.65]
by

\Xi_{\rho,\lambda}
and suitably modifying the proof of
[
17
, Theorem 4]
or
[
58
, Theorem 1.9]

we may obtain:

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\gamma
and
\mathcal{Q}
be as in Lemma
5.3
.
For each
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta_{0},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta_{0}]

the restriction of
f_{\lambda}
to
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}

has the
G
-deformation property, i.e.,
for every
c\in\mathbb{R}

and every
\hat{\tau}>0
, every
G
-neighborhood
U
of

K_{\lambda,c}:=K(f_{\lambda})\cap\{z\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,f_{\lambda}(z)\leq c\}
there exists an
\tau\in(0,\hat{\tau})
and
a
G
equivariant homotopy
\eta:[0,1]\times\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\to\overline{\mathcal{Q}}

with the following properties:

(i)

if
A\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
is closed and
G
-invariant, so is
\eta(s,A)
for any
s\in[0,1]
;

(ii)

\eta(s,z)=z
if
z\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\setminus(f_{\lambda})^{-1}[c-\hat{\tau},c+\hat{\tau}]
;

(iii)

\eta(s,\cdot)
is homeomorphism of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}

to
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
for each
s\in[0,1]
;

(iv)

\eta(1,A_{\lambda,c+\tau}\setminus U)\subset A_{\lambda,c-\tau}
,
where
A_{\lambda,d}:=\{z\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,f_{\lambda}(z)\leq d\}
for
d\in\mathbb{R}
;

(v)

if
K_{\lambda,c}=\emptyset
,
\eta(1,A_{\lambda,c+\tau})\subset A_{\lambda,c-\tau}
.

Let

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})=\{A\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,A\;\hbox{is closed and $G$-invariant}\},

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})_{j}=\{A\in\mathcal{E}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})\,|\,i_{G}(A)\leq j\},\;j=1,2,\cdots,

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}=\{\chi\in C(\overline{\mathcal{Q}},\overline{\mathcal{Q}})\,|\,\hbox{$\chi$
is $G$-equivariant, one to one, and $\chi(x)=x\,\forall x\in T^{-}$}\},

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{-}\cdot K=\{\varphi(s,x)\,|\,-\infty<s<0,\;x\in K\}\quad\hbox{for}\;K\subset T^{-}.

Suppose
i_{G}(T^{-})=k>0
.
For
1\leq j\leq k
define

\displaystyle G_{j}=\{\chi(\mathbb{R}^{-}\cdot K)\,|\,\chi\in\mathscr{F},K\subset T^{-}\;\hbox{belongs to $\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})$ and $i_{G}(K)\geq j$}\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{j}=\{\overline{A\setminus B}\,|\,\hbox{for some integer $q\in[j,i_{G}(T^{-})]$},\;A\in G_{q},B\in\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})_{q-j}\}.

As in the proofs of
[
26
, Lemma 2.12]
and
[
27
, Lemma 8.55]
we have:

1)

\Gamma_{j+1}\subset\Gamma_{j},\;1\leq j\leq i_{G}(T^{-})-1
.

2)

If
\chi\in\mathscr{F}
and
A\in\Gamma_{j}
, then
\chi(A)\in\Gamma_{j}
.

3)

If
A\in\Gamma_{j}
and
B\in\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})_{s}
with
s<j
, then
\overline{A\setminus B}\in\Gamma_{j-s}
.

Let us define

c_{\lambda,j}=\inf_{A\in\Gamma_{j}}\max_{z\in A}f_{\lambda}(z),\quad j=1,\cdots,k.

Then the above property 1) implies
c_{\lambda,1}\leq c_{\lambda,2}\leq\cdots\leq c_{\lambda,k}
.

Firstly, let us assume that (
5.1
) is true.

For each
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast})
,
0\in X
is always a local (strict) minimizer of
f_{\lambda}
.
Therefore for sufficiently small
\rho_{\lambda}>0
, we have

f_{\lambda}(z)>0
for any
0<\|z\|\leq\rho_{\lambda}

and so

c_{\lambda,1}\geq\min_{\|z\|=\rho_{\lambda}}f_{\lambda}(z)>0.

Using Lemma
5.5
and
repeating other arguments in
[
26
, Theorem 2.9]
and
[
27
, Lemmas 8.67, 8.72]
we obtain:

(a)

each
c_{\lambda,j}

is a critical value of
f_{\lambda}
with a corresponding critical point in
\mathscr{Q}
;

(b)

if
c_{\lambda,j+1}=\cdots=c_{\lambda,j+p}=c
then
i_{G}(K_{\lambda,c})\geq p
,
where
K_{\lambda,c}
is as in Lemma
5.5
;

(c)

all critical points corresponding to
c_{\lambda,j}
,
1\leq j\leq i_{G}(T^{-})
, converge to
z=0
as
\lambda\to\lambda^{\ast}
.

G
is equal to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{{\rm id},-{\rm id}\}
(resp.
S^{1}

Moreover, when
p>1
,
K_{\lambda,c}
contains infinitely many distinct
G
-orbits. (In fact, if
G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{{\rm id},-{\rm id}\}

this follows from
[
26
, Lemma 2.8]
.
If
G=S^{1}
without fixed points except
0
,
since
0\notin K_{\lambda,c}
and the isotropy group
S^{1}_{x}
of the
S^{1}
-action at any
x\in X
is either finite or
S^{1}
,
we deduce that the induced
S^{1}
-action on
K_{\lambda,c}
has only finite isotropy groups and thus that

K_{\lambda,c}/S^{1}
is an infinite set by
[
27
, Remark 6.16]
.)
These lead to

Claim 5.6
.

If
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast})
is close to
\lambda^{\ast}
,

f_{\lambda}
has at least
k
distinct
nontrivial critical
G
-orbits, which also converge to
0
as
\lambda\to\lambda^{\ast}
.

For every
\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]
,

0\in X
is a local maximizer of
f_{\lambda}
, by considering

-f_{\lambda}
the same reason yields:

Claim 5.7
.

If
i_{G}(T^{+})=l>0
, for every
\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]
close to
\lambda^{\ast}
,

f_{\lambda}
has at least
l
distinct nontrivial
critical
G
-orbits converging to
0
as
\lambda\to\lambda^{\ast}
.

These two claims together yield the result of Theorem
5.1
.

Next, if (
5.1
) holds true
, the similar arguments lead to:

Claim 5.8
.

if
\lambda\in(\lambda^{\ast},\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]
(resp.

\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast})
) is close to
\lambda^{\ast}
,

f_{\lambda}
has at least
l
(resp.
k
) distinct nontrivial
critical
G
-orbits, which also converge to
0
as
\lambda\to\lambda^{\ast}
.

So the expected result is still obtained.
∎

The following may be viewed as a generalization of
Fadell–Rabinowitz theorems in
[
26
,
27
]
.

Theorem 5.9
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
4.2
, let
H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
be the eigenspace of
(
4.1
) associated with
\lambda^{\ast}
. Let
H
be equipped with
an orthogonal action of a compact Lie group
G
under which

U
,
\mathcal{L}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}
are
G
-invariant.
If the Lie group
G
is equal to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{{\rm id},-{\rm id}\}
(resp.
S^{1}

and the fixed point set of the induced
S^{1}
-action on
H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
,

(H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}})^{S^{1}}=\{x\in H^{0}\,|\,g\cdot x=x\;\forall g\in S^{1}\}
, is
\{0\}
,
which implies
\dim H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
to be an even more than one),
then one of the following alternatives holds:

(i)

(\lambda^{\ast},0)
is not an isolated solution of (
4.2
) in

\{\lambda^{\ast}\}\times U
;

(ii)

there exist left and right neighborhoods
\Lambda^{-}
and
\Lambda^{+}
of
\lambda^{\ast}
in
\mathbb{R}

and integers
n^{+},n^{-}\geq 0
, such that
n^{+}+n^{-}\geq\dim H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
(resp.
\frac{1}{2}\dim H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
),
and that for
\lambda\in\Lambda^{-}\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}
(resp.
\lambda\in\Lambda^{+}\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}
)
(
4.2
) has at least
n^{-}
(resp.
n^{+}
) distinct critical

G
-orbits different from
0
, which converge to

0
as
\lambda\to\lambda^{\ast}
.

In particular,
(\lambda^{\ast},0)\in\mathbb{R}\times U
is a bifurcation point for the equation
(
4.2
).

Proof.

The first claim follows from Theorem
4.2
.
For others, by the arguments above Step 1 in the proof of Theorem
4.2

the problem is reduced to finding the
G
-critical orbits of
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}

near
0\in H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
for
\lambda
near
\lambda^{\ast}
,
where
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}:B_{H}(0,\epsilon)\cap H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}\to\mathbb{R}
is given by (
4.9
).
Suppose that (i) does not hold. By shrinking
\delta>0
, we obtain
that for each
\lambda\in[\lambda^{\ast}-\delta,\lambda^{\ast}+\delta]
,
0\in H
is an isolated critical point of

\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
and thus
0\in H^{0}_{\lambda^{\ast}}
is an isolated critical point of

\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}
. Then we have
(
4
) and (
4
), and so (ii) follows from
Theorem
5.1
.
∎

By Corollaries
4.3
and
4.4
we get

Corollary 5.10
.

If “Under the assumptions of Theorem
4.2
” in Theorem
5.9

is replaced by “Under the assumptions of one of Corollaries
4.3
and
4.4
”,
then the conclusions of Theorem
5.9
hold true.

Now we consider generalizations of
Fadell–Rabinowitz theorems in
[
26
,
27
]
in the setting of
[
40
,
41
]
.
Combing Theorem
5.1
