\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(u)\in\mathscr{L}(X)
is
C^{1}
.

Since (c’) and (d’) imply that

\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto B_{\lambda}(0)|_{X}=dA_{\lambda}(0)\in\mathscr{L}(X)

is continuous, and the spectrum
\sigma(B_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)|_{X})

does not intersect the imaginary axis,
by shrinking
\Lambda
we can assume that for some
r>0
,

\displaystyle\sigma(B_{\lambda}(0)|_{X})\cap\{z=x+iy\,|\,|x|\leq r\}=\emptyset,\quad\forall\lambda\in\Lambda,

(B.5)

and that for any
\lambda\in\Lambda
,
\sigma(B_{\lambda}(0)|_{X})
is equal to the union

\sigma(B_{\lambda}(0)|_{X})_{+}\cup\sigma(B_{\lambda}(0)|_{X})_{-}
, where

\sigma(B_{\lambda}(0)|_{X})_{+}=\{\mu\in\sigma(B_{\lambda}(0)|_{X})\,|\,{\rm Re}\mu>0\},\quad

\sigma(B_{\lambda}(0)|_{X})_{-}=\{\mu\in\sigma(B_{\lambda}(0)|_{X})\,|\,{\rm Re}\mu<0\}.

By Lemma
B.2
with
D=B_{\lambda}(0)|_{X}
, corresponding to the
spectral sets
\sigma(B_{\lambda}(0)|_{X})_{+}
and
\sigma(B_{\lambda}(0)|_{X})_{-}
we have a
decomposition of spaces,
X=X_{+}^{\lambda}\oplus X_{-}^{\lambda}
,
and projections
P_{\ast}^{\lambda}:X\to X_{\ast}^{\lambda}
(
\ast=+,-
), which
belong to
\mathscr{S}(X)
and are continuous in
\lambda
by shrinking
\Lambda
(if necessary).

Step 1
(
The case of
\sigma(B_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)|_{X})\cap(-\sigma(B_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)|_{X}))=\emptyset
).
Consider the map

\Phi:\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)\times\mathscr{S}(X)\to\mathscr{S}(X),\;(\lambda,u,Y)\mapsto Y\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(Yu)Y-\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0).

By the conclusion in the first paragraph it is not hard to check that
\Phi
is continuous, and
C^{1}
in
(u,Y)
, and satisfies
D_{Y}\Phi(\lambda^{\ast},0,id_{X})=q_{\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)}
,
where the operator

q_{\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)}:\mathscr{S}(X)\to\mathscr{S}(X),\;Z\mapsto\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)Z+Z\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0).

Since
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)=\frac{1}{2}B_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)|_{X}
,
by
[
8
, Lemma 1.4]
the operator
q_{\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)}

has a linear bounded inverse. With the classical implicit function theorem
we obtain a neighborhood
\Lambda_{0}
of
\lambda^{\ast}
in
\Lambda
,

\epsilon\in(0,\delta)
and a continuous map
\psi
from

\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X}(0,\epsilon)
to
\mathscr{S}(X)
, which is also
C^{1}

with respect to the second variable, such that
(\lambda,u)\mapsto\psi_{u}^{\prime}(\lambda,u)
is continuous and

\psi(\lambda^{\ast},0)=id_{X}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\Phi(\lambda,u,\psi(\lambda,u))\equiv 0\quad\forall(\lambda,u)\in\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X}(0,\epsilon).

(Clearly,
\Phi
and so
\psi
is
C^{1}
if the conditions in the “Moreover” part hold.)
Shrinking
\Lambda_{0}
and
\epsilon>0
such that

\|\psi(\lambda,u)-\psi(\lambda^{\ast},0)\|<1/2
for all
(\lambda,u)\in\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X}(0,\epsilon)
,
we get that each operator
\psi(\lambda,u):X\to X

has a linear bounded inverse. For each
\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}
, define

\varphi_{\lambda}:B_{X}(0,\epsilon)\to X,\;u\mapsto\psi(\lambda,u)u.

Then
\varphi_{\lambda}(0)=0
and
d\varphi_{\lambda}(0)=\psi(\lambda,0)
. Clearly, both
(\lambda,u)\mapsto\varphi_{\lambda}(u)

and
(\lambda,u)\mapsto\varphi^{\prime}_{\lambda}(u)
are
C^{0}
.
It is easily checked that

\varphi_{\lambda}
satisfies (
B.2
) (see
[
8
]
).

Since
\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}(u)[\xi]=(\psi^{\prime}_{u}(\lambda,u)[\xi])u+\psi(\lambda,u)\xi
for
\xi\in X
, we derive

\displaystyle\|[\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}(0)]^{-1}\|_{X}=\|[\psi(\lambda,0)]^{-1}\|_{X}=\|[id_{X}+\psi(\lambda,0)-\psi(\lambda^{\ast},0)]^{-1}\|_{X}\leq 2\quad\forall\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}

and

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}(u)-\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}(0)\|_{X}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\psi(\lambda,u)-\psi(\lambda,0)\|_{X}+\|\psi^{\prime}_{u}(\lambda,u)\|_{\in\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))}\|u\|_{X}

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt\leq\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|\psi^{\prime}_{u}(t\lambda,u)\|_{\in\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))}\|u\|_{X}+\|\psi^{\prime}_{u}(\lambda,u)\|_{\in\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))}\|u\|_{X}

for all
(\lambda,u)\in\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X}(0,\epsilon)
, where the second inequality comes from the mean value theorem.
Because
\psi^{\prime}_{u}(\cdot,\cdot)
is continuous,
we can shrink the neighborhood
\Lambda_{0}
of
\lambda^{\ast}
and
\epsilon>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}(u)-\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}(0)\|_{X}\leq\frac{1}{4}\quad\hbox{for all $(\lambda,u)\in\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X}(0,\epsilon)$.}

By Lemma
B.4
, for each
\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}
,

\varphi_{\lambda}(B_{X}(0,\epsilon))\subset X
is an open subset containing
B_{X}(0,\frac{\epsilon}{4})

and
\varphi_{\lambda}
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism from
B_{X}(0,\epsilon)
to
\varphi_{\lambda}(B_{X}(0,\epsilon))
.
It also holds that
B_{X}(0,\epsilon_{0}/4)\subset\varphi_{\lambda}(B_{X}(0,\epsilon_{0})))
for any
\epsilon_{0}\in(0,\epsilon]
.

(
Note
: Using the usual inverse mapping theorem we can only prove that
\varphi_{\lambda}
restricts to a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism
from some
B_{X}(0,\epsilon_{\lambda})\subset B_{X}(0,\epsilon)
onto an open neighborhood of
0
in
X
.)

Step 2
(
The case of
\sigma(B_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)|_{X})\cap(-\sigma(B_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)|_{X}))\neq\emptyset
).
We can take
M>0
such that

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0))_{+}\cap(-M^{2}\sigma(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0))_{-})=\emptyset.

(B.6)

Set
R_{\lambda^{\ast}}:=P_{+}^{\lambda^{\ast}}+MP_{-}^{\lambda^{\ast}}
and
\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}:=\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}\circ R_{\lambda^{\ast}}
.
By
[
8
, Lemma 1.2]
,

\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}(u)=(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\lambda}(u)u,u)_{H},\quad\forall u\in B_{X}(0,\delta^{\prime}),

where
\delta^{\prime}>0
is such that
R_{\lambda^{\ast}}(B_{X}(0,\delta^{\prime}))\subset B_{X}(0,\delta)
and

\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\lambda}(u)=R_{\lambda^{\ast}}{\mathcal{B}}_{\lambda}(R_{\lambda^{\ast}}u)R_{\lambda^{\ast}}
.
Then
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)=R_{\lambda^{\ast}}{\mathcal{B}}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)R_{\lambda^{\ast}}
and so

\sigma(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0))=\sigma(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0))_{+}\cup(M^{2}\sigma(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0))_{-}).

It follows from (
B.6
) that
\sigma(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0))
is disjoint with

-\sigma(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0))
. By the first step,
there exists a neighborhood
\Lambda_{0}
of
\lambda^{\ast}
in
\Lambda
,

\epsilon\in(0,\delta)
and a continuous map
\widehat{\varphi}
from

\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X}(0,\epsilon)
to
X
,
such that each
\widehat{\varphi}_{\lambda}(\cdot):=\widehat{\varphi}(\lambda,\cdot)
is an origin-preserving
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism
from
B_{X}(0,\epsilon)
onto an open neighborhood of zero of
X
containing
B_{X}(0,\frac{\epsilon}{4})
,

(\lambda,u)\mapsto\widehat{\varphi}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(u)
is
C^{0}
, and that

\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}(\widehat{\varphi}(\lambda,u))=(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)u,u)_{H}+\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}(0)=\frac{1}{2}(R_{\lambda^{\ast}}\circ(B_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)|_{X})\circ R_{\lambda^{\ast}}u,u)_{H}+{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}(0)

for
(\lambda,u)\in\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X}(0,\epsilon)
. Actually, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\varphi}_{\lambda^{\ast}}^{\prime}(0)=id_{X},\quad\|[\widehat{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(0)]^{-1}\|_{X}\leq 2\quad\hbox{and}\quad\|\widehat{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(u)-\widehat{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(0)\|_{X}\leq\frac{1}{4}

for all
(\lambda,u)\in\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X}(0,\epsilon)
.
Take
\epsilon_{0}\in(0,\epsilon]
such that
(R_{\lambda^{\ast}})^{-1}(B_{X}(0,\epsilon_{0}))\subset B_{X}(0,\epsilon)
.
Set
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(u):=R_{\lambda^{\ast}}\widehat{\varphi}(\lambda,(R_{\lambda^{\ast}})^{-1}u)
,
where
(R_{\lambda^{\ast}})^{-1}=P_{+}^{\lambda^{\ast}}+M^{-1}P_{-}^{\lambda^{\ast}}
is the inverse of
R_{\lambda^{\ast}}
. Then

\displaystyle{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}(\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(u))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}(R_{\lambda^{\ast}}\widehat{\varphi}(\lambda,(R_{\lambda^{\ast}})^{-1}u))=\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}(\widehat{\varphi}(\lambda,(R_{\lambda^{\ast}})^{-1}u))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(B_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)|_{X}u,u)_{H}+{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}(0),\quad\forall(\lambda,u)\in\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X}(0,\epsilon_{0}).

Moreover,
\widetilde{\varphi}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(u)=R_{\lambda^{\ast}}\circ\widehat{\varphi}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(u)\circ R_{\lambda^{\ast}}^{-1}
. It follows that

\widetilde{\varphi}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(0)=id_{X}
and

\|\widetilde{\varphi}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(u)-\widetilde{\varphi}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(0)\|\leq\|R_{\lambda^{\ast}}\|\|\widehat{\varphi}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(u)-\widehat{\varphi}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(0)\|\|(R_{\lambda^{\ast}})^{-1}\|.

Since
(\lambda,u)\mapsto\widehat{\varphi}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(u)
is
C^{0}
,

\|\widehat{\varphi}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(u)-\widehat{\varphi}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(0)\|\to 0
as
(\lambda,u)\to(\lambda^{\ast},0)
.
Further shrinking
\Lambda_{0}
and
\epsilon_{0}>0
we may get that

\|[\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(0)]^{-1}\|_{X}\leq 2
and
\|\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(u)-\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(0)\|_{X}\leq\frac{1}{4}

for all
(\lambda,u)\in\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X}(0,\epsilon_{0})
. By Lemma
B.4
,

\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(B_{X}(0,\epsilon_{0}))
contains
B_{X}(0,\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{4})

for each
\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}
.

Summarizing Steps 1, 2 we get the conclusions in the first part.

Step 3
. For the “Moreover” part, by the final part in Lemma
B.14
the map

\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(u)\in\mathscr{L}(X)
is
C^{1}
, so is

(\lambda,u)\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\lambda}(u)=R_{\lambda^{\ast}}{\mathcal{B}}_{\lambda}(R_{\lambda^{\ast}}u)R_{\lambda^{\ast}}
.
Then
\Phi
and so
\psi
in Step 1 is
C^{1}
. The desired claim is easily seen from the above proof.
∎

Remark B.5
.

The assumptions (f1)-(f2) and (f3) in Theorem
B.3
are used to guarantee that the map

\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(u)\in\mathscr{L}(X)

defined by (
B.4
) is
C^{1}
.
When
\Lambda=\mathbb{R}
and
A_{\lambda}=A-\lambda\widehat{A}
, where

A,\widehat{A}:B_{X}(0,\delta)\to X
are
C^{2}
maps corresponding with

C^{3}
functionals
\mathscr{L},\widehat{\mathscr{L}}:B_{X}(0,\delta)\to\mathbb{R}

satisfying (a) and (b) above (
B.1
),
the assumptions (f1)-(f3) can be replaced by condition that each of
A^{\prime\prime}
and
\widehat{A}^{\prime\prime}

is either
C^{1}
or uniformly continuous in some neighborhood of the line segment

[0,1]u:=\{tu\,|\,0\leq t\leq 1\}
for each
u\in B_{X}(0,\delta)
.
See arguments in Remark
B.1
and Lemma
B.14
.

By slightly strengthening assumptions of
[
8
, Theorem 1.2]
we can prove:

Theorem B.6
.

Let
\mathscr{L}:B_{X}(0,\delta)\to\mathbb{R}
be a
C^{2}
-functional satisfying
the conditions (a)-(b) above (
B.1
) and
d\mathscr{L}(0)=0
, and let
B:B_{X}(0,\delta)\to\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
be defined by (
B.1
).
Suppose that the following two conditions are satisfied:

(i)

There is a
C^{2}
map
A:B_{X}(0,\delta)\to X
such that

d\mathscr{L}(x)[u]=(A(x),u)_{H}
for all
x\in B_{X}(0,\delta)
and
u\in X
, and that
A^{\prime\prime}
is either
C^{1}
or uniformly continuous
in some neighborhood of any line segment in
B_{X}(0,\delta)
.

(ii)

0\in\sigma(B(0)|_{X})
and
\sigma(B(0)|_{X})\setminus\{0\}
is bounded away from the imaginary axis.

Let
X=X_{0}\oplus X_{+}\oplus X_{-}
be a direct sum decomposition of Banach spaces,
which corresponds to the spectral sets
\{0\}
,

\sigma_{+}(B(0)|_{X})
and
\sigma_{-}(B(0)|_{X})
as above. Denote by
P_{0}
,
P_{+}
and
P_{-}
the
corresponding projections as in Lemma
B.2
.
Then there exists
\epsilon\in(0,\delta)
, a
C^{2}
map
h:B_{X}(0,\epsilon)\cap X_{0}\to X_{+}\oplus X_{-}
with
h(0)=0
(which is also
C^{3}
if
A
is
C^{3}
), and
a
C^{1}
origin-preserving diffeomorphism
\varphi
from

B_{X}(0,\epsilon)
onto an open neighborhood of
0
in
X
such
that

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}\circ\varphi(x)=\frac{1}{2}(B(0)x,x)_{H}+\mathscr{L}^{\circ}(P_{0}x),\quad\forall x\in B_{X}(0,\epsilon).

(B.7)

where the functional

\mathscr{L}^{\circ}:B_{X_{0}}(0,\epsilon)\to\mathbb{R}
, defined by

\mathscr{L}^{\circ}(z)=\mathscr{L}(z+h(z))
,
is of class
C^{2}
, and has the first-order derivative at
z_{0}\in B_{X_{0}}(0,\epsilon)
given by

\displaystyle d\mathscr{L}^{\circ}(z_{0})[z]=\bigl(A(z_{0}+h(z_{0})),z\bigr)_{H},\quad\forall z\in X_{0},

(B.8)

and the second-order derivative at
0\in B_{X_{0}}(0,\epsilon)
,
d^{2}\mathscr{L}^{\circ}(0)=0
.
Moreover, after suitably shrinking
\epsilon>0
the diffeomorphism
\varphi
can be chosen to satisfy

\mathscr{L}\circ\varphi(x)=\|P_{+}x\|_{H}^{2}-\|P_{-}x\|_{H}^{2}+\mathscr{L}(h(P_{0}x)+P_{0}x),\quad\forall x\in B_{X}(0,\epsilon).

(
B.7
) and (
B.8
) imply that
0\in X_{0}
is an isolated critical point of
\mathscr{L}^{\circ}

if and only if
0\in X
is such a critical point of of
\mathscr{L}
.

Proof of Theorem
B.6
.

For conveniences let
P_{\pm}:=P_{+}+P_{-}
,
X_{\pm}:=X_{+}+X_{-}
and
H_{\pm}
be the closure of
X_{\pm}
in
H
.
A standard implicit function theorem argument yields a

\epsilon\in(0,\delta)
and a
C^{2}
map
h:B_{X}(0,\epsilon)\cap X_{0}\to X_{\pm}
with
h(0)=0
(which is also
C^{3}
if
A
is
C^{3}
),
such that

\displaystyle(id_{X}-P_{0})A(z+{h}(z))=0,\quad\forall z\in B_{X_{0}}(0,\epsilon).

For each
z\in\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X_{0}}(0,\epsilon)
, define

\displaystyle{\bf L}_{z}:B_{X_{\pm}}(0,\delta/2)\to\mathbb{R},\;u\mapsto\mathscr{L}(z+{h}(z)+u)-\mathscr{L}(z+{h}(z)),

\displaystyle{\bf A}_{z}:B_{X_{\pm}}(0,\delta/2)\to X_{\pm},\;u\mapsto P_{\pm}A(z+{h}(z)+u),

\displaystyle{\bf B}_{z}:B_{X_{\pm}}(0,\delta/2)\to\mathscr{L}_{s}(H_{\pm}),\;u\mapsto P_{\pm}\circ(B(z+{h}(z)+u)|_{H_{\pm}}).

Then
d{\bf L}_{z}(0)=0
,
d{\bf L}_{z}(x)[u]=({\bf A}_{z}(x),u)_{H}
and
d^{2}{\bf L}_{z}(x)[u,v]=({\bf B}_{z}(x)u,v)_{H}

for all
x\in B_{X_{\pm}}(0,\delta/2)
and
u,v\in X_{\pm}
.
By (ii),
{\bf B}_{0}(0)|_{X_{\pm}}=P_{\pm}\circ(B(0)|_{X_{\pm}})\in\mathscr{L}(X_{\pm})

is hyperbolic and its spectrum is bounded away from the imaginary axis.
Since
{\bf A}_{z}
is
C^{2}
, we may define
\mathcal{B}:B_{X_{0}}(0,\epsilon)\times B_{X_{0}}(0,\epsilon)\to X_{\pm}
by

\mathcal{B}(z,u):=\int^{1}_{0}(1-t){\bf A}^{\prime}_{z}(tu)dt=\int^{1}_{0}(1-t)P_{\pm}A^{\prime}(z+{h}(z)+tu)|_{X_{\pm}}dt.

Then
{\bf L}_{z}(u)=(\mathcal{B}(z,u)u,u)_{H}
.
For any
(z,u),(z_{0},u_{0})\in B_{X_{0}}(0,\epsilon)\times B_{X_{\pm}}(0,\delta/2)
we have

\mathcal{B}(z,u)-\mathcal{B}(z_{0},u_{0})=\int^{1}_{0}(1-t)P_{\pm}[A^{\prime}(z+{h}(z)+tu)|_{X_{\pm}}-A^{\prime}(z_{0}+{h}(z_{0})+tu_{0})|_{X_{\pm}}]dt.

By (i),
A
is
C^{2}
,
A^{\prime\prime}
is bounded in a convex neighborhood of the line segment

z_{0}+h(z_{0})+[0,1]u_{0}
. It follows from the mean value theorem that

\mathcal{B}(z,u)\to\mathcal{B}(z_{0},u_{0})
as
(z,u)\to(z_{0},u_{0})
.

As before, since
A
is
C^{2}
, by the differential method with parameter integral we can get
Gâteaux derivatives
D_{u}\mathcal{B}(z,u)
and
D_{z}\mathcal{B}(z,u)
,

\displaystyle D_{u}\mathcal{B}(z,u)[u^{\prime}]=\int^{1}_{0}(1-t)tP_{\pm}A^{\prime\prime}(z+{h}(z)+tu)[u^{\prime}]dt,\quad u^{\prime}\in{X_{\pm}},

\displaystyle D_{z}\mathcal{B}(z,u)[z^{\prime}]=\int^{1}_{0}(1-t)P_{\pm}A^{\prime\prime}(z+{h}(z)+tu)[z^{\prime}+h^{\prime}(z)[z^{\prime}]]dt,\quad z^{\prime}\in{X_{0}}.

Let
C_{0}:=\|P_{\pm}\|_{\mathscr{L}(X,X_{\pm})}
. For any
(z,u),(z_{0},u_{0})\in B_{X_{0}}(0,\epsilon)\times B_{X_{\pm}}(0,\delta/2)
it easily follows that

\displaystyle\|D_{u}\mathcal{B}(z,u)-D_{u}\mathcal{B}(z_{0},u_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X_{\pm},\mathscr{L}(X_{\pm}))}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{0}\int^{1}_{0}\|A^{\prime\prime}(z+{h}(z)+tu)-A^{\prime\prime}(z_{0}+{h}(z_{0})+tu_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))}dt

and

\displaystyle\|D_{z}\mathcal{B}(z,u)-D_{z}\mathcal{B}(z_{0},u_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X_{0},\mathscr{L}(X_{\pm}))}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{0}\int^{1}_{0}\|A^{\prime\prime}(z+{h}(z)+tu)-A^{\prime\prime}(z_{0}+{h}(z_{0})+tu_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))}dt

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle C_{0}\int^{1}_{0}\|A^{\prime\prime}(z+{h}(z)+tu)\circ h^{\prime}(z)

\displaystyle-A^{\prime\prime}(z_{0}+{h}(z_{0})+tu_{0})\circ h^{\prime}(z_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X_{0},\mathscr{L}(X))}dt.

Observe that

\displaystyle\|A^{\prime\prime}(z+{h}(z)+tu)\circ h^{\prime}(z)-A^{\prime\prime}(z_{0}+{h}(z_{0})+tu_{0})\circ h^{\prime}(z_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X_{0},\mathscr{L}(X))}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|A^{\prime\prime}(z+{h}(z)+tu)\circ h^{\prime}(z)-A^{\prime\prime}(z_{0}+{h}(z_{0})+tu_{0})\circ h^{\prime}(z)\|_{\mathscr{L}(X_{0},\mathscr{L}(X))}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\|A^{\prime\prime}(z_{0}+{h}(z_{0})+tu_{0})\circ h^{\prime}(z)-A^{\prime\prime}(z_{0}+{h}(z_{0})+tu_{0})\circ h^{\prime}(z_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X_{0},\mathscr{L}(X))}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|A^{\prime\prime}(z+{h}(z)+tu)-A^{\prime\prime}(z_{0}+{h}(z_{0})+tu_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))}\|h^{\prime}(z)\|_{\mathscr{L}(X_{0},X)}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\|A^{\prime\prime}(z_{0}+{h}(z_{0})+tu_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))}\|h^{\prime}(z)-h^{\prime}(z_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X_{0},X)}.

As above, by (i) we can derive from these inequalities that
both
D_{u}\mathcal{B}
and
D_{z}\mathcal{B}

are continuous. The other arguments are standard, see the following proof of Theorem
B.8
.
∎

Remark B.7
.

(I)
. (
B.7
) was proved in
[
23
, page 436, Theorem 1]

when
\mathscr{L}
is of class
C^{r}
,
A:B_{X}(0,\delta)\to X
is
C^{r-1}
, and
the operator
B(0)|_{X}=dA(0)\in\mathscr{L}(X)
restricts an isomorphism on
X_{+}+X_{-}
.
Correspondingly, (
B.2
) with
\Lambda=\{\lambda^{\ast}\}
was proved in

[
23
, page 425, Theorem 2]
(
[
71
, Theorem 1.4]
with
r=3
)
under similar conditions. As above we cannot prove that the
C^{r-2}
-smoothness of
the map
A
in the proof of
[
23
, page 425, Theorem 2]
if
r=3
. There exists the same problem in the proof

[
23
, page 436, Theorem 1]
as well.

(II)
. When
\varphi
is only required
to be a homeomorphism, using a different method (thus not involving the above problem)
Ming Jiang
[
33
, Theorem 2.5]
proved (
B.7
)
if the conditions
(i)
–
(ii)

are replaced by the following:

1)

A:B_{X}(0,\delta)\to X
is
C^{1}
,

2)

B:B_{X}(0,\delta)\to\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
is continuous,

3)

C2) in Hypothesis
1.3
, and either
0\notin\sigma(B(0))
or

0
is an isolated point of
\sigma(B(0))
.

Here is a parametric version of Theorem
B.6
.

Theorem B.8
.

Suppose that the conditions (d’) and (h) in Theorem
B.3
are respectively replaced by the following:

(sd’)

For each point
\lambda_{0}\in\Lambda
and any line segment
\ell\subset B_{X}(0,\delta)
there exist
neighborhoods
\mathscr{N}(\lambda_{0})
of
\lambda_{0}
in
\Lambda
,
and
\mathscr{N}(\ell)
of
\ell
in
B_{X}(0,\delta)

such that

\mathscr{N}(\lambda_{0})\times\mathscr{N}(\ell)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto A_{\lambda}(u)\in X

is continuous and that

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}(\lambda_{0})\times\mathscr{N}(\ell)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto dA_{\lambda}(u)\in\mathscr{L}(X)\quad\hbox{and}

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}(\lambda_{0})\times\mathscr{N}(\ell)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto d^{2}A_{\lambda}(u)\in\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))

are uniform continuous.

(wh)

For some
\lambda^{\ast}\in\Lambda
,
\sigma(B_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)|_{X})\setminus\{0\}

is bounded away from the imaginary axis.

Let
X=X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}\oplus X_{+}^{\lambda^{\ast}}\oplus X_{-}^{\lambda^{\ast}}
be a direct sum decomposition of Banach spaces,
which corresponds to the spectral sets
\{0\}
,
\sigma_{+}(B_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)|_{X})

and
\sigma_{-}(B_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)|_{X})
, and let
P_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}
,
P_{+}^{\lambda^{\ast}}
and
P_{-}^{\lambda^{\ast}}
denote the corresponding projections.
Then there exists a neighborhood
\Lambda_{0}
of
\lambda^{\ast}
in
\Lambda
,

\epsilon>0
, and

(i)

a (unique)
C^{0}
map

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}:\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\to X_{+}^{\lambda^{\ast}}\oplus X_{-}^{\lambda^{\ast}}

(B.9)

which is
C^{2}
in the second variable and
satisfies
\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,0)=0\;\forall\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}
and

\displaystyle(id_{X}-P_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}})A_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z))=0,\quad\forall(\lambda,z)\in\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon),

(B.10)

(ii)

a
C^{0}
map

\displaystyle\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X}(0,\epsilon)\to X,\quad({\lambda},x)\mapsto\Phi_{{\lambda}}(x)

(B.11)

such that for each
\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}
the map

\Phi_{\lambda}
is an origin-preserving
C^{1}
-differeomor-

phism
from
B_{X}(0,\epsilon)
onto an open neighborhood of zero of
X
containing
B_{X}\\
(0,\epsilon/4)

and satisfies

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\Phi_{\lambda}(x))=\frac{1}{2}(B_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}x,P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}x)_{H}+\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(P_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}x+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,P_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}x))

(B.12)

for any
x\in B_{X}(0,\epsilon)
; moreover
({\lambda},x)\mapsto\Phi^{\prime}_{{\lambda}}(x)
is continuous.

In addition, with
notations

X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}:=X_{+}^{\lambda^{\ast}}\oplus X_{-}^{\lambda^{\ast}}
and

P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}:=P_{+}^{\lambda^{\ast}}+P_{-}^{\lambda^{\ast}}
,
we also have:

(iii)

(\lambda,z)\mapsto d_{z}\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)
is continuous and

d_{z}\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)=-[P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}\circ({B}_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z))|_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}})]^{-1}\circ(P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}\circ({B}_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z))|_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}})).

(B.13)

(iv)

The functional

\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\circ}:B_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\to\mathbb{R},\;z\mapsto\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}({\lambda},z))

(B.14)

is of class
C^{2}
, and has the first-order derivative at
z_{0}\in B_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)
given by

\displaystyle d\mathscr{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}(z_{0})[z]=\bigl(A_{\lambda}(z_{0}+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z_{0})),z\bigr)_{H},\quad\forall z\in X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}},

(B.15)

and the second-order derivative at
0\in B_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)

\displaystyle d^{2}\mathscr{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}(0)[z,z^{\prime}]=\left(P_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}\bigr[{B}_{\lambda}(0)-{B}_{\lambda}(0)(P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}{B}_{\lambda}(0)|_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}})^{-1}(P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}{B}_{\lambda}(0))\bigr]z,z^{\prime}\right)_{H},

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\forall z,z^{\prime}\in X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}.

(B.16)

(v)

The map
z\mapsto z+\mathfrak{h}({\lambda},z))
induces an one-to-one correspondence
between the critical points of
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\circ}
near
0\in X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}

and those of
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
near
0\in X
.

Let
G
be a compact Lie group acting on
H
orthogonally,
which induces a
C^{1}
isometric action on
X
. Suppose that each
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
is
G
-invariant and that

A_{\lambda},B_{\lambda}
are equivariant. Then for each
\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}
,
both
\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,\cdot)
and
\Phi_{\lambda}
are equivariant, and

\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\circ}
is
G
-invariant.

Moreover, if
\Lambda
is a nonempty open subset of a Banach space
Z
,
then the maps in (
B.9
) and (
B.11
)
are of class
C^{1}
provided that (sd’) is replaced by the following three conditions:

(f1)

\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto A(\lambda,u):=A_{\lambda}(u)\in X

and

\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto D_{2}A(\lambda,u)\in\mathscr{L}(X)

are
C^{1}
. Here
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
are differentials of

with respect to
\lambda
and
u
, respectively.

(sf2)

Either
\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto D^{2}_{2}A(\lambda,u)\in\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))

is
C^{1}
, or for each point
\lambda_{0}\in\Lambda
and any line segment
\ell\subset B_{X}(0,\delta)
there exist
neighborhoods
\mathscr{N}(\lambda_{0})
of
\lambda_{0}
in
\Lambda
,
and
\mathscr{N}(\ell)
of
\ell
in
B_{X}(0,\delta)

such that

\mathscr{N}(\lambda_{0})\times\mathscr{N}(\ell)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto D^{2}_{2}A(\lambda,u)\in\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))

is uniform continuous.

(sf3)

Either
\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto D_{1}D_{2}A(\lambda,u)\in\mathscr{L}(Z,\mathscr{L}(X))

is
C^{1}
, or for each point
\lambda_{0}\in\Lambda
and any line segment
\ell\subset B_{X}(0,\delta)
there exist
neighborhoods
\mathscr{N}(\lambda_{0})
of
\lambda_{0}
in
\Lambda
,
and
\mathscr{N}(\ell)
of
\ell
in
B_{X}(0,\delta)

such that

\mathscr{N}(\lambda_{0})\times\mathscr{N}(\ell)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto D_{1}D_{2}A(\lambda,u)\in\mathscr{L}(Z,\mathscr{L}(X))

is uniform continuous.

Clearly, (sd’) and these three assumptions hold if
\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto A(\lambda,u)\in X
is
C^{3}
.

Proof.

Step 1
. By the first two conditions in (sd’), and (wh),
a standard implicit function theorem argument yields (i) and (iii).
As in Step 2 of the proof of
[
41
, Lemma 3.1]
we can get (
B.15
).
Then it and (
B.13
) lead to (
(iv)
).

By shrinking
\Lambda_{0}
and
\epsilon
we can assume that

z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z))\in B_{X}(0,\delta/2)
for all
(\lambda,z)\in\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)
.
Let
H_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}
be the closure of
X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}
in
H
.
For each
(\lambda,z)\in\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)
, define maps

\displaystyle{\bf L}_{(\lambda,z)}:B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta/2)\to\mathbb{R},\;u\mapsto\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)+u)-\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)),

\displaystyle{\bf A}_{(\lambda,z)}:B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta/2)\to X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}},\;u\mapsto P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}A_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)+u),

\displaystyle{\bf B}_{(\lambda,z)}:B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta/2)\to\mathscr{L}_{s}(H_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}),\;u\mapsto P_{+}^{\lambda^{\ast}}\circ(B_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)+u)|_{H_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}).

Then
d{\bf L}_{(\lambda,z)}(0)=0
,

d{\bf L}_{(\lambda,z)}(x)[u]=({\bf A}_{(\lambda,z)}(x),u)_{H}
and
d^{2}{\bf L}_{(\lambda,z)}(x)[u,v]=({\bf B}_{(\lambda,z)}(x)u,v)_{H}

for all
x\in B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta/2)
and for all
u,v\in X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}
.
Moreover, the condition (wh) implies that

{\bf B}_{(\lambda^{\ast},0)}(0)|_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}=P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}\circ(B_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)|_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}})\in\mathscr{L}(X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}})

is hyperbolic and its spectrum is bounded away from the imaginary axis.
Since
{\bf A}_{(\lambda,z)}
is
C^{2}
, we can define a map

\mathcal{B}:\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\times B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta/2)\to\mathscr{L}(X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}})
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(\lambda,z,u):=\int^{1}_{0}(1-t){\bf A}^{\prime}_{(\lambda,z)}(tu)dt=\int^{1}_{0}(1-t){\bf B}_{(\lambda,z)}(tu)|_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}dt

(B.17)

for any
(\lambda,z,u)\in\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\times B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta/2)
.
Then
{\bf L}_{(\lambda,z)}(u)=(\mathcal{B}(\lambda,z,u)u,u)_{H}
.
As in Step 1 of the proof of Lemma
B.14
, we can use the first condition in (sd’) to show that
\mathcal{B}
is continuous.

By the proof of Theorem
B.6
it is easily seen that
\mathcal{B}(\lambda,z,u)

has Gâteaux derivatives
D_{u}\mathcal{B}(\lambda,z,u)
in
u
and
D_{z}\mathcal{B}(\lambda,z,u)
in
z
,

\displaystyle D_{u}\mathcal{B}(\lambda,z,u)[u^{\prime}]=\int^{1}_{0}(1-t)tP_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)+tu)[u^{\prime}]dt,\quad u^{\prime}\in X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}},

\displaystyle D_{z}\mathcal{B}(\lambda,z,u)[z^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\int^{1}_{0}(1-t)P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)+tu)[z^{\prime}+D_{z}\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)[z^{\prime}]]dt,\quad z^{\prime}\in X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}.

Let
C:=\|P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}\|_{\mathscr{L}(X,X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}})}
. For any
(\lambda,z,u),(\lambda_{0},z_{0},u_{0})\in\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\times B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta/2)

it easily follows from the first equation that

\displaystyle\|D_{u}\mathcal{B}(\lambda,z,u)-D_{u}\mathcal{B}(\lambda_{0},z_{0},u_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}},\mathscr{L}(X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}))}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\int^{1}_{0}\|A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)+tu)-A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda_{0}}(z_{0}+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda_{0},z_{0})+tu_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))}dt.

As in Step 3 of the proof of Lemma
B.14
, we can use the second condition in (sd’) to show that
D_{u}\mathcal{B}
is continuous
at
(\lambda_{0},z_{0},u_{0})
.

Similarly, as in the proof of Theorem
B.6
we derive

\displaystyle\|D_{z}\mathcal{B}(\lambda,z,u)-D_{z}\mathcal{B}(\lambda_{0},z_{0},u_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}},\mathscr{L}(X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}))}

\displaystyle\leq C\int^{1}_{0}\|A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)+tu)-A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda_{0}}(z_{0}+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda_{0},z_{0})+tu_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))}dt

\displaystyle+C\int^{1}_{0}\|A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)+tu)\circ\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}_{z}(\lambda,z)

\displaystyle-A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda_{0}}(z_{0}+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda_{0},z_{0})+tu_{0})\circ\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}_{z}(\lambda_{0},z_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))}dt

and

\displaystyle\|A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)+tu)\circ\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}_{z}(\lambda,z)

\displaystyle-A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda_{0}}(z_{0}+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda_{0},z_{0})+tu_{0})\circ\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}_{z}(\lambda_{0},z_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)+tu)

\displaystyle-A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda_{0}}(z_{0}+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda_{0},z_{0})+tu_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))}\|\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}_{z}(\lambda,z)\|_{\mathscr{L}(X_{0},X)}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\|A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(z_{0}+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda_{0},z_{0})+tu_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))}\|\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}_{z}(\lambda,z)-\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}_{z}(\lambda_{0},z_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X_{0},X)}.

By (
B.13
),
(\lambda,z)\mapsto\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}_{z}(\lambda,z)
is continuous.
Using the second condition in (sd’) again we deduce that

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)+tu)-A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda_{0}}(z_{0}+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda_{0},z_{0})+tu_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))}\to 0

as
\lambda\to\lambda_{0}
and
u\to u_{0}
, and that

\|A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)+tu)\|_{\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))}

uniformly converges to
\|A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda_{0}}(z_{0}+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda_{0},z_{0})+tu_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))}

with respect to
t\in[0,1]
. Hence
D_{z}\mathcal{B}
is continuous
at
(\lambda_{0},z_{0},u_{0})
.

In summary, we have proved that
(\lambda,z,u)\mapsto\mathcal{B}(\lambda,z,u)

is continuous,
C^{1}
in each of
z
and
u
, and both

(\lambda,z,u)\mapsto\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{z}(\lambda,z,u)

and
(\lambda,z,u)\mapsto\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{u}(\lambda,z,u)
are continuous.

Step 2
. Since
\mathcal{B}(\lambda^{\ast},0,0)=\frac{1}{2}{\bf B}_{(\lambda^{\ast},0)}(0)|_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}
we can take
M>0
such that

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathcal{B}({\lambda^{\ast}},0,0))_{+}\cap(-M^{2}\sigma(\mathcal{B}({\lambda^{\ast}},0,0))_{-})=\emptyset.

(B.18)

Set
R_{\lambda^{\ast}}:=P_{+}^{\lambda^{\ast}}|_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}+MP_{-}^{\lambda^{\ast}}|_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}
and

\widehat{\bf L}_{(\lambda,z)}:={\bf L}_{(\lambda,z)}\circ R_{\lambda^{\ast}}
.
As before we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\bf L}_{(\lambda,z)}(u)=(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}(\lambda,z,u)u,u)_{H},\quad\forall u\in B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta_{1}),

(B.19)

where
\delta_{1}>0
is such that
R_{\lambda^{\ast}}\left(B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta_{1})\right)\subset B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta/2)
, and

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{B}}(\lambda,z,u):=R_{\lambda^{\ast}}{\mathcal{B}}(\lambda,z,R_{\lambda^{\ast}}u)R_{\lambda^{\ast}}.

(B.20)

The latter implies

\sigma(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}({\lambda^{\ast}},0,0))=\sigma(\mathcal{B}({\lambda^{\ast}},0,0))_{+}\cup(M^{2}\sigma(\mathcal{B}({\lambda^{\ast}},0,0))_{-})
and hence

\displaystyle\sigma(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}({\lambda^{\ast}},0,0))\cap(-\sigma(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}({\lambda^{\ast}},0,0)))=\emptyset

(B.21)

by (
B.18
).
Consider the map

\Psi:\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\times B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta_{1})\times\mathscr{S}(X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}})\to\mathscr{S}(X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}})

defined by
\Psi(\lambda,z,u,Y)=Y\widehat{\mathcal{B}}(\lambda,z,Yu)Y-\widehat{\mathcal{B}}(\lambda^{\ast},0,0)
.
Then
\Psi
is continuous,
C^{1}
in
(z,u,Y)
, and

\Psi^{\prime}_{z}(\lambda,z,u,Y)
,
\Psi^{\prime}_{u}(\lambda,z,u,Y)
and
\Psi^{\prime}_{Y}(\lambda,z,u,Y)
continuously depend on
(\lambda,z,u,Y)
.
Since

D_{Y}\Psi(\lambda^{\ast},0,0,id_{X}):\mathscr{S}(X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}})\to\mathscr{S}(X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}),\;Z\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{B}}({\lambda^{\ast}},0,0)Z+Z\widehat{\mathcal{B}}({\lambda^{\ast}},0,0)

is a Banach space isomorphism by (
B.21
) and
[
8
, Lemma 1.4]
,
as in the proof of Theorem
B.3
using the implicit function theorem
we can, by shrinking the neighborhood
\Lambda_{0}
of
\lambda^{\ast}
and
\epsilon>0
, get

\delta_{2}\in(0,\delta_{1})
and a continuous map

\psi:\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\times B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta_{2})\to\mathscr{S}(X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}})

such that

\bullet
for all
(\lambda,z,u)\in\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\times B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta_{2})
,

\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}&\psi(\lambda^{\ast},0,0)=id_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}},\quad\Psi(\lambda,z,u,\psi(\lambda,z,u))\equiv 0\quad\hbox{and}\\
&\|\psi(\lambda,z,u)-\psi(\lambda^{\ast},0,0)\|<1/2,\end{array}\right\}

(B.22)

\bullet

\psi(\lambda,z,u)
is
C^{1}
in
(z,u)
, both
\psi^{\prime}_{z}(\lambda,z,u)
and
\psi^{\prime}_{u}(\lambda,z,u)
continuously depend on
(\lambda,z,u)
.
Clearly, (
B.22
) implies that each operator
\psi(\lambda,z,u):X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}\to X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}

has a linear bounded inverse.
Now for any
(\lambda,z,u)\in\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\times B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta_{2})

it holds that

\displaystyle\psi(\lambda,z,u)\widehat{\mathcal{B}}(\lambda,z,\psi(\lambda,z,u)u)\psi(\lambda,z,u)\equiv\widehat{\mathcal{B}}(\lambda^{\ast},0,0)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}R_{\lambda^{\ast}}\left({\bf B}_{(\lambda^{\ast},0)}(0)|_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}\right)R_{\lambda^{\ast}}.

(B.23)

Moreover, from (
B.19
) we derive

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)+R_{\lambda^{\ast}}u)-\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)),

(B.24)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bf L}_{(\lambda,z)}(R_{\lambda^{\ast}}u)=(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}(\lambda,z,u)u,u)_{H},\quad\forall u\in B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta_{1}).

By further shrinking the neighborhood
\Lambda_{0}
of
\lambda^{\ast}
and
\epsilon>0
, we can get

\delta_{3}\in(0,\delta_{2})
such that

\psi(\lambda,z,u)u\in B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta_{1}),\quad\forall(\lambda,z,u)\in\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\times B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta_{3})

because of (
B.22
). Then (
B
) and
(
B.24
) lead to

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)+R_{\lambda^{\ast}}\psi(\lambda,z,u)u)-\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)),

(B.25)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}(\lambda,z,\psi(\lambda,z,u)u)\psi(\lambda,z,u)u,\psi(\lambda,z,u)u)_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\psi(\lambda,z,u)\widehat{\mathcal{B}}(\lambda,z,\psi(\lambda,z,u)u)\psi(\lambda,z,u)u,u)_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(({\bf B}_{(\lambda^{\ast},0)}(0)|_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}})R_{\lambda^{\ast}}u,R_{\lambda^{\ast}}u)_{H}.

Take
\delta_{4}\in(0,\delta_{3})
such that
R_{\lambda^{\ast}}\left(B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta_{4})\right)\subset B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta_{3})
. Then
(
B.25
) yields

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}\big(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)+R_{\lambda^{\ast}}\psi(\lambda,z,(R_{\lambda^{\ast}})^{-1}u)(R_{\lambda^{\ast}})^{-1}u\big)-\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)),

(B.26)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(({\bf B}_{(\lambda^{\ast},0)}(0)|_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}})u,u)_{H}=\frac{1}{2}(B_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)u,u)_{H}

for all
(\lambda,z,u)\in\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\times B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta_{4})
,
where
(R_{\lambda^{\ast}})^{-1}=P_{+}^{\lambda^{\ast}}|_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}+M^{-1}P_{-}^{\lambda^{\ast}}|_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}
is the inverse
of
R_{\lambda^{\ast}}
.

Take
\delta_{5}=\min\{\epsilon,\delta_{4}\}
. Then for
x\in B_{X}(0,\delta_{5})
we have

z=P_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}x\in B_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)
and
u=P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}x\in B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta_{4})
.
For each
\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}
, define
\Phi_{\lambda}:B_{X}(0,\delta_{5})\to X
by

\displaystyle\Phi_{\lambda}(x)=z+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,z)+R_{\lambda^{\ast}}\psi(\lambda,z,(R_{\lambda^{\ast}})^{-1}u)(R_{\lambda^{\ast}})^{-1}u,

(B.27)

where
z=P_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}x
and
u=P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}x
. Then

\Phi_{\lambda}(0)=0
,
\Phi_{\lambda}
is
C^{1}
,
and both
(\lambda,x)\mapsto\Phi_{\lambda}(x)
and
(\lambda,x)\mapsto\Phi^{\prime}_{\lambda}(x)
are continuous.
For
z^{\prime}\in X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}
and
y^{\prime}\in X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}
a direct computation
leads to

\displaystyle d\Phi_{\lambda}(0)[z^{\prime}+y^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle z^{\prime}+d_{z}\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,0)[z^{\prime}]+R_{\lambda^{\ast}}\psi(\lambda,0,0)(R_{\lambda^{\ast}})^{-1}y^{\prime}

(B.28)

and so
d\Phi_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)[z^{\prime}+y^{\prime}]=z^{\prime}+y^{\prime}
because
d_{z}\mathfrak{h}(\lambda^{\ast},0)=0
by (
B.13
).
From these and (
B.22
) we get

\displaystyle d\Phi_{\lambda}(0)[z^{\prime}+y^{\prime}]-z^{\prime}-y=d_{z}\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,0)[z^{\prime}]+R_{\lambda^{\ast}}(\psi(\lambda,0,0)-\psi(\lambda^{\ast},0,0))(R_{\lambda^{\ast}})^{-1}y^{\prime}

and therefore we may shrink
\Lambda_{0}
so that
\|d\Phi_{\lambda}(0)-id_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}\|<\frac{1}{2}
for all
\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}
.
Then we have

\|[d\Phi_{\lambda}(0)]^{-1}\|\leq 2\quad\forall\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}

as before.
Since
\psi^{\prime}_{z}(\lambda,z,u)
and
\psi^{\prime}_{u}(\lambda,z,u)
continuously depend on
(\lambda,z,u)
,
shrinking
\Lambda_{0}
and
\delta_{5}>0
again, it follows from (
B.27
) and (
B.28
) that

\|d\Phi_{\lambda}(x)-d\Phi_{\lambda}(0)\|\leq\frac{1}{4}\quad\forall(\lambda,x)\in\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X}(\theta,\delta_{5}).

By Lemma
B.4
, for each
\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}
,

\Phi_{\lambda}(B_{X}(0,\delta_{5}))\subset X
is an open neighborhood of zero in
X
containing
B_{X}(0,\frac{\delta_{5}}{4})

and
\Phi_{\lambda}
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism from
B_{X}(0,\delta_{5})
to
\Phi_{\lambda}(B_{X}(0,\delta_{5}))
.
Moreover, (
B.26
) yields (
B.12
) with
\epsilon=\delta_{5}
.

It is clear that the positive number
\epsilon
can be replaced by
\delta_{5}
in all conclusions of Theorem
B.8
which have been proved.

Claims in (iv) and (v) easily follows as in
[
41
,
45
]
.

Under the assumptions of the “Moreover” part, by the above arguments and the final part of proof of Lemma
B.14

it is not hard to check that the maps

\mathcal{B}:\Lambda_{0}\times B_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\epsilon)\times B_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}(0,\delta/2)\to\mathscr{L}(X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}})

and

.
Therefore the desired claims are easily seen from the above proof.
∎

Remark B.9
.

The assumptions (f1) and (sf2)-(sf3) in Theorem
B.8
guarantee that the map

\mathcal{B}
in (
B.17
) (and hence
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
in (
B.20
))
is
C^{1}
. When
\Lambda=\mathbb{R}
and
A_{\lambda}=A-\lambda\widehat{A}
, where

A,\widehat{A}:B_{X}(0,\delta)\to X
are
C^{2}
maps corresponding with

C^{3}
functionals
\mathscr{L},\widehat{\mathscr{L}}:B_{X}(0,\delta)\to\mathbb{R}

satisfying (a) and (b) above (
B.1
),
if each of
A^{\prime\prime}
and
\widehat{A}^{\prime\prime}
is either
C^{1}
or uniformly continuous in some neighborhood of
any segment in
B_{X}(0,\delta)

it is easily seen from the above proof that the corresponding maps
\mathcal{B}
and

\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
are also
C^{1}
. Hence Theorem
B.8
is still true in this situation.

Since
0\in X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}
is an isolated critical point of
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\circ}
if and only if

0\in X
is such a critical point of
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
,
as usual Theorem
B.8
lead to:

Corollary B.10

(Shifting)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
B.8
,
let
\nu_{\lambda^{\ast}}:=\dim X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}<\infty
and
\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}:=\dim X_{-}^{\lambda^{\ast}}<\infty
.
For any Abel group
{\bf K}
, and for any given
\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}
, if
0\in X
is an
isolated critical point of
\mathscr{L}
then

C_{q}(\mathscr{L}_{\lambda},0;{\bf K})\cong C_{q-\mu_{\lambda^{\ast}}}(\mathscr{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda},0;{\bf K})
for all

q\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.

Claim B.11
.

In Theorem
B.8
, if for some
\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}\setminus\{\lambda^{\ast}\}
,

0\in X
is a nondegenerate critical point of
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
,
i.e., the operator
B_{\lambda}(0)|_{X}\in\mathscr{L}(X)
is hyperbolic (
[
72
]
),
then
0\in X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}
is such a critical point of
\mathscr{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}
too.

Proof.

Let
z\in X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}
be such that
d^{2}\mathscr{L}^{\circ}_{\lambda}(0)[z,z^{\prime}]=0\;\forall z^{\prime}\in X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}
.
By (
(iv)
) we deduce

\displaystyle P_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}\bigr[{B}_{\lambda}(0)-{B}_{\lambda}(0)(P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}{B}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)|_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}})^{-1}(P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}{B}_{\lambda}(0))\bigr]z=0.

(B.29)

Moreover, differentiating the equality in (
B.10
) at
z=0
and using (
B.13
) we get that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(id_{X}-P_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}})B_{\lambda}(0)|_{X}(z+d_{z}\mathfrak{h}(\lambda,0)z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(id_{X}-P_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}})B_{\lambda}(0)|_{X}(z-[P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}{B}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)|_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}]^{-1}(P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}{B}_{\lambda}(0))|_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}}z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(id_{X}-P_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}})[B_{\lambda}(0)-B_{\lambda}(0)(P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}{B}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)|_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}})^{-1}(P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}{B}_{\lambda}(0))|_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}})]z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[B_{\lambda}(0)-B_{\lambda}(0)(P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}{B}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)|_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}})^{-1}(P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}{B}_{\lambda}(0))|_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}})]z

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle P_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}[B_{\lambda}(0)-B_{\lambda}(0)(P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}{B}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)|_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}})^{-1}(P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}{B}_{\lambda}(0))|_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}})]z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[B_{\lambda}(0)-B_{\lambda}(0)(P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}{B}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)|_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}})^{-1}(P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}{B}_{\lambda}(0))|_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}})]z,

where the final equality comes from (
B.29
). Since
B_{\lambda}(0)
is invertible, we deduce

z=(P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}{B}_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0)|_{X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}})^{-1}(P_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}{B}_{\lambda}(0))|_{X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}})z.

Note that
z\in X_{0}^{\lambda^{\ast}}
and that the right side belongs to
X_{\pm}^{\lambda^{\ast}}
.
Hence
z=0
.
∎

In general, it is not easy to judge whether
\sigma(B(0)|_{X})\setminus\{0\}
is bounded away from the imaginary axis.
However, it has the following equivalent version:

(*)

0
is at most an isolated point of

\sigma(B(0)|_{X})
and
B(0)|_{X}
induces a hyperbolic operator on the quotient space
X/X_{0}
,
where
X_{0}={\rm Ker}(B(0)|_{X})
.

In fact, it is clear if
0\notin\sigma(B(0)|_{X})
. Assume that
\sigma_{0}:=\{0\}\subset\sigma(B(0)|_{X})
.
Since
\sigma_{1}:=\sigma(B(0)|_{X})\setminus\{0\}
is bounded away from the imaginary axis,
0
is an isolated point of
\sigma(B(0)|_{X})
.
By Lemma
B.2
there exists a direct sum decomposition of the Banach space
X
,
X=X_{0}+X_{1}
,
where
X_{0}={\rm Ker}(B(0)|_{X})
and
X_{1}
is an invariant subspace on which
B(0)|_{X}
has the spectrum
\sigma_{1}
.
Note that the quotient space
X/X_{0}
and the induced operator of
B(0)|_{X}
on it can be identified with

X_{1}
and
B(0)|_{X_{1}}
, respectively. Hence (*) is satisfied. Conversely, since
0
is an isolated point of

\sigma(B(0)|_{X})
we have a decomposition
X=X_{0}+X_{1}
as above, and
B(0)|_{X_{1}}
is a hyperbolic operator on
X_{1}\equiv X/X_{0}
.
It follows that
\sigma(B(0)|_{X})\setminus\{0\}
is bounded away from the imaginary axis.

For a class of operators used in this paper the following lemma determines when
0

is at most an isolated point of
\sigma(B(0))
.

Lemma B.12
.

Let a self-adjoined operator
\mathfrak{B}\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
be a sum
\mathfrak{B}=\mathfrak{P}+\mathfrak{Q}
, where

\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{L}(H)
is invertible, and
\mathfrak{Q}\in\mathscr{L}(H)
is compact. Let

0\in\sigma(\mathfrak{B})
. Then
0
is an
isolated point of
\sigma(\mathfrak{B})
and an eigenvalue of
\mathfrak{B}
of the finite multiplicity. (
[
8
, Lemma 2.2]
)

In addition, if
\mathfrak{P}
is also positive definite, then every
\lambda<\inf\{(\mathfrak{P}u,u)_{H}\,|\,\|u\|_{H}=1\}

is either a regular value of
\mathfrak{B}
or an isolated point of

\sigma(\mathfrak{B})
, which is also an eigenvalue of finite multiplicity. (
[
41
, Proposition B.2]
)

As before let
H^{0}
,
H^{+}
and
H^{-}
be null, positive and negative definite spaces of
B(0)
,
and let
P^{\ast}:H\to H^{\ast}
for
\ast=0,+,-
, be the orthogonal projections.
Clearly,
X_{0}\subset H^{0}
. The following lemma shows how the Morse index
\mu

is computed.

Lemma B.13
.

If
X_{0}=H^{0}
and
either
\dim H^{-}<\infty
or
\dim H^{+}<\infty
, then

X_{\ast}
are dense subspaces of
H^{\ast}
for
\ast=+,-
, moreover

X_{-}=H^{-}
(resp.
X_{+}=H^{+}
) as

\dim H^{-}<\infty
(resp.
\dim H^{+}<\infty
).
(These imply
X_{\ast}=H^{\ast}\cap X
and
P_{\ast}=P^{\ast}|_{X}

for
\ast=0,+,-
, and hence

X_{\pm}=X\cap H^{\pm}=(P^{+}+P^{-})(X)
, where
H^{\pm}:=H^{+}+H^{-}
.)

Proof.

Since
X
is dense in
H
, it is easily proved that
X_{\pm}:=X_{+}\oplus X_{-}
is dense in

H^{\pm}
. Let
X_{+}^{H}
and
X_{-}^{H}
be the closure of
X_{+}
and
X_{-}
in
H
, respectively.
They are orthogonal in
H
, invariant with respect to
B(0)
and
H^{\pm}=X_{+}^{H}+X_{-}^{H}
.
Since
B(0)
is semi-positive (resp. semi-negative) on
X_{+}^{H}
(resp.
X_{-}^{H}
)
by Lemma
B.2
, and
B(0)
has no nontrivial kernel on
H^{\pm}
, we deduce that

B(0)
must be positive (resp. negative) on
X_{+}^{H}
(resp.
X_{-}^{H}
).
Let
\dim H^{-}<\infty
. If
X^{H}_{-}\cap H^{-}\neq\{0\}
, it is an invariant subspace of
B(0)
.
Denote by
X^{H}_{--}
and
H^{--}
the orthogonal complements of
X^{H}_{-}\cap H^{-}
in
X^{H}_{-}
and
H^{-}
,
respectively. Then
X^{H}_{--}+H^{--}+X^{H}_{-}\cap H^{-}
is a negative definite subspace of
B(0)
.
But
\dim H^{-}
is the maximal dimension of negative definite subspaces of
B(0)
.
Hence
X^{H}_{--}=\{0\}
, and thus
X^{H}_{-}=X^{H}_{-}\cap H^{-}
. The latter means
X^{H}_{-}\subset H^{-}

and so
X^{H}_{+}\supseteq H^{+}
. For any
x\in H^{-}
, since

X_{\pm}=X_{+}\oplus X_{-}
is dense in
H^{\pm}=H^{+}+H^{-}
, we have a sequence
(x_{n}^{+}+x_{n}^{-})\subset X_{+}\oplus X_{-}

such that
x_{n}^{+}+x_{n}^{-}\to x
. Note that

\|x_{n}^{+}+x_{n}^{-}-x_{m}^{+}-x_{m}^{-}\|^{2}=\|x_{n}^{+}-x_{m}^{+}\|^{2}+\|x_{n}^{-}-x_{m}^{-}\|^{2}
.
We get
x_{n}^{+}\to x^{+}\in X^{H}_{+}
and
x_{n}^{-}\to x^{-}\in X^{H}_{-}
. Hence
x=x^{+}+x^{-}
.
But
(x,x^{+})_{H}=0
. So
x^{+}=0
and
x=x^{-}
. This shows that
X_{-}
is dense in
H^{-}

and thus
X_{-}=X_{-}^{H}=H^{-}
(since
\dim H^{-}<\infty
). The latter implies
X_{+}^{H}=H^{+}
.
Similarly, we can deal with the case that
\dim H^{+}<\infty
.
∎

Lemma B.14
.

Let
\Lambda
be a metric space, and

\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}:B_{X}(0,\delta)\to\mathbb{R}
,
\lambda\in\Lambda
, be
a family of
C^{2}
-functionals satisfying the conditions (a)-(b) and
d\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(0)=0\;\forall\lambda
.
Suppose that the corresponding operators
A_{\lambda}

satisfies the assumptions (c’)-(d’) in Theorem
B.3
.
Then
\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(u)\in\mathscr{L}(X)
is continuous,
C^{1}
in
u
, and

\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(u)\in\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))
is also continuous.
Moreover, if
\Lambda
is a nonempty open subset of a Banach space
Z
and the assumptions (c’) and (f1)-(f3) in Theorem
B.3

are satisfied, then
\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(u)\in\mathscr{L}(X)
is
C^{1}
.
In particular, for
C^{2}
maps
A,\widehat{A}:B_{X}(0,\delta)\to X
, if each of
A^{\prime\prime}
and
\widehat{A}^{\prime\prime}
is either
C^{1}
or
uniformly continuous in some neighborhood of the line segment
[0,1]u:=\{tu\,|\,0\leq t\leq 1\}
for each
u\in B_{X}(0,\delta)
,
then

\mathbb{R}\times B_{X}(0,\delta)\to\mathscr{L}(X),\;(\lambda,u)\mapsto\mathcal{B}(\lambda,u)=\int^{1}_{0}(1-t)A^{\prime}(tu)dt-\lambda\int^{1}_{0}(1-t)\widehat{A}^{\prime}(tu)dt

is
C^{1}
.

Proof.

Step 1
(
Prove that
\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(u)\in\mathscr{L}(X)

is continuous
). Fix a point
(\lambda_{0},u_{0})\in\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)
. Then
for any
(\lambda,u)\in\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)
it holds that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(u)-\mathcal{B}_{\lambda_{0}}(u_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X)}\leq\int^{1}_{0}(1-t)\|A^{\prime}_{\lambda}(tu)-A^{\prime}_{\lambda_{0}}(tu_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X)}dt.

(B.30)

By (d’) there exist neighborhoods
\mathscr{N}(\lambda_{0})
of
\lambda_{0}
in
\Lambda
, and
\mathscr{N}([0,1]u_{0})
of

[0,1]u_{0}
in
B_{X}(0,\delta)
such that

\mathscr{N}(\lambda_{0})\times\mathscr{N}([0,1]u_{0})\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto A^{\prime}_{\lambda}(x)\in\mathscr{L}(X)

is uniform continuous, that is, for any
\varepsilon>0
, we have
\nu>0
such that

\|A^{\prime}_{\lambda_{1}}(u_{1})-A^{\prime}_{\lambda_{2}}(u_{2})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X)}<\varepsilon

provided that
(\lambda_{i},u_{i})\in\mathscr{N}(\lambda_{0})\times\mathscr{N}([0,1]u_{0})
,
i=1,2
,
satisfies
d(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})<\nu
and
\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|_{X}<\nu
.
Thus if
(\lambda,u)\in\Lambda\times B_{X}(\theta,\delta)
satisfies
d(\lambda,\lambda_{0})<\nu
and
\|u-u_{0}\|_{X}<\nu

then
\|A^{\prime}_{\lambda}(tu)-A^{\prime}_{\lambda_{0}}(tu_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X)}<\varepsilon

for all
t\in[0,1]
. It follows that

\|\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(u)-\mathcal{B}_{\lambda_{0}}(u_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X)}\leq\varepsilon\int^{1}_{0}(1-t)dt=\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon.

Step 2
(
Prove that
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}:B_{X}(0,\delta)\to\mathscr{L}(X)

is Gâteaux differentiable
).
For fixed
u\in B_{X}(0,\delta)
and
h\in X
we have
\rho>0
such that
u+sh\in B_{X}(0,\delta)

for all
s\in[-\rho,\rho]
. Then the function
[-\rho,\rho]\times[0,1]\ni(s,t)\mapsto(1-t)A^{\prime}_{\lambda}(t(u+sh))\in\mathscr{L}(X)
is continuous and has a continuous partial derivative in
s

[-\rho,\rho]\times[0,1]\ni(s,t)\mapsto\frac{\partial}{\partial s}((1-t)A^{\prime}_{\lambda}(t(u+sh)))

=(1-t)tA^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(t(u+sh))h\in\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X)).

By the differential method with parameter integral we get

\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(u+sh)=\int^{1}_{0}(1-t)tA^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(t(u+sh))hdt.

In particular,
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
has a Gâteaux derivative
D\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(u)\in\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))
at
u
given by

D\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(u)[h]=\int^{1}_{0}(1-t)tA^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(tu)hdt=\left(\int^{1}_{0}(1-t)tA^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(tu)dt\right)h,\quad\forall h\in X.

Step 3
(
Prove that
\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(u)\in\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))

is continuous
). For a fixed point
(\lambda_{0},u_{0})\in\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)
,
by (d’) we have neighborhoods
\mathscr{N}(\lambda_{0})
of
\lambda_{0}
in
\Lambda
, and
\mathscr{N}([0,1]u_{0})
of

[0,1]u_{0}
in
B_{X}(0,\delta)
such that

\mathscr{N}(\lambda_{0})\times\mathscr{N}([0,1]u_{0})\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(x)\in\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))

is uniform continuous. Then there exists
\nu>0
such that when
(\lambda,u)\in\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)
satisfies
d(\lambda,\lambda_{0})<\nu
and
\|u-u_{0}\|_{X}<\nu

the following holds:

\|A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(tu)-A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda_{0}}(tu_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))}<\varepsilon,\quad\forall t\in[0,1].

Hence for such
(\lambda,u)
we deduce

\displaystyle\|D\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(u)-D\mathcal{B}_{\lambda_{0}}(u_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int^{1}_{0}(1-t)t\|A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(tu)-A^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda_{0}}(tu_{0})\|_{\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))}dt

(B.31)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{6}\varepsilon.

That is,
\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto D\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(u)\in\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))

is continuous. In particular,
B_{X}(0,\delta)\ni u\mapsto D\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(u)\in\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))

is continuous, and so
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
has a Fréchet derivative
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(u)=D\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(u)
at
u
.
Then
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
is
C^{1}
.

Step 4
(
Prove the conclusion in the “Moreover” part
). Write

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(\lambda,u)=\int^{1}_{0}(1-t)D_{2}A(\lambda,tu)dt.

Since (f1) and (f2) imply (c’) and (d’),
\mathcal{B}
is continuous,
C^{1}
in
u
, and

\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto D_{2}\mathcal{B}(\lambda,u)\in\mathscr{L}(X,\mathscr{L}(X))

is also continuous.
Similarly, from the second condition in (f1) we may derive that

\mathcal{B}(\lambda,u)
has a Gâteaux derivative in
\lambda
given by

D_{1}\mathcal{B}(\lambda,u)=\int^{1}_{0}(1-t)D_{1}D_{2}A(\lambda,tu)dt.

As above using (f3) we get that
\Lambda\times B_{X}(0,\delta)\ni(\lambda,u)\mapsto D_{1}\mathcal{B}(\lambda,u)\in\mathscr{L}(Z,\mathscr{L}(X))

is continuous. Hence
\mathcal{B}
is
C^{1}
.

The final claim is easily seen from the above arguments.
∎
